# crew

Pool LLMs with friends. Open a project and you are in, share it when you want somebody with you, and everyone's local LLM CLIs become shared agents. Chat, docs, and a dashboard, synced through git.

## Commands

- `yarn dev` — run the app
- `yarn build` — build main, preload, and renderer
- `yarn start` — run the built app, wearing the icon it ships with
- `yarn preview` — the same run, wearing the blueprint
- `yarn test tests/<name>.test.ts` — one suite (vitest), which is how tests are normally run
- `yarn test` — the whole suite, which takes a long time. Only when asked for it
- `yarn tsc --noEmit` — typecheck
- `yarn covers` — draw every music cover on one page
- `yarn selection-check` — select everything in a real window and see what it painted
- `yarn preview-check` — stand a page up in a real window and see what it reached
- `yarn design-header-look` — stand the design header up in a real window and read where every control landed
- `yarn docs-look` — stand the docs page up in a real window and read where the list, the writing and the trail landed
- `yarn plus-look` — press through the composer's plus in a real window and read the card back every frame of the way
- `yarn composer-look` — fill the composer past its ceiling in a real window, scroll it, and photograph both edges
- `yarn card-look` — stand the schedule and the tool cards up in a real window and photograph them, growing a box of words until it scrolls
- `yarn doc-click-look` — click past the end of every kind of row in a real window and read where the caret landed
- `yarn doc-numbers-look` — nest a numbered list six deep in a real window and read what each level really counted
- `yarn scrim-check` — stand the chrome over a real column in a real window and read the pixels down it
- `yarn codex-check` — run a real Codex turn and read what came back off it
- `yarn kimi-check` — run a real Kimi turn and read what came back off it
- `yarn gemini-check` — run a real Gemini turn and read what came back off it
- `yarn goal-check` — run a real goal turn and see whether the condition really held it open
- `yarn scribe-edit-check` — read real dictations back through a real model on this computer and see what it wrote
- `yarn dist` — build a mac dmg
- `crew` opens a crew on a folder from a terminal. It ships inside the app, and the This computer page in the settings puts it on PATH. `npm link` in this checkout does the same from here

## Writing

- No em dashes, and no semicolons used in their place. Write plain sentences.
- The app is called Crew, and it is capitalized wherever it is named. It is a name, not a word.
- Plain words. No over-selling, no narration. State things simply.
- UI copy is for everyone, not just engineers. Avoid engineering jargon.
- Copy never echoes the request that produced it. Placeholders and empty states describe what the user can do in general terms, never the specifics of what was asked for or how it was built.
- A heading stands on its own. A line under it saying the same thing again in longer words, or explaining what the field below it is for, is a row of type spent on nothing. Write a second line only when it says something the screen cannot say without it, and never one that starts by naming what the thing is.
- Every line has to say something the screen cannot say on its own. Naming back what a person is already looking at is the obvious half of a bad line, and the flourish tacked on the end is the other: "cursors and all", "and more", "in seconds". Both are filler, and one screen of it teaches people to stop reading the words everywhere else in the app. Take the filler out, and if nothing is left standing, the screen is better with no line on it.
- Never explain the machinery. A platform that will not hand over a key, a model coming down, a fallback that exists because the good path sometimes fails: none of that is the person's to carry, and a line that carries it is the conversation that built the feature read aloud in the product. Write the consequence for the person, or write nothing.
- The skill in `.claude/skills/crew-copy` is the rest of it: the test a line has to pass before it is written, and where each rule came from.
- A line written from the brief reads like the brief. Copy that hands back the parts a feature was asked for, "agents you mention in board chat draw here too", is the request read aloud, and everyone can hear it. Write from what is in front of the person and what they can do next, and name none of the machinery behind it: not the panel, not the mode, not the mechanism, not the model.

## Code

- No code comments, in any file, ever. Humans add comments when they want them. A file already full of them is not an invitation to add another: matching the surrounding style is the one reason that never counts, because every comment in there was written by somebody who wanted it and none of them were written by whoever is reading the diff. Why a thing works the way it does belongs in this file. Why a change was made belongs in the message it was committed under.
- Small files. SOLID. DRY. Readable over clever.
- TypeScript everywhere. React and Tailwind in the renderer.
- Integration tests over unit tests. Every feature ships with coverage of its behavior. Tests live in `tests/` and boot real servers and runners on loopback.

## Design

- Dark mode. Tokens live in `src/renderer/src/styles.css` (Tailwind `@theme`): ink scale for surfaces (`ink-900` background, `ink-800` raised, `ink-700` borders and sunken bars), fg scale for text (`fg`, `fg-secondary`, `fg-muted`, `fg-faint`).
- White is the single action color. `positive` and `danger` appear only for status.
- Type ramp: xs 11, sm 13, base 14, lg 16. System sans. The word "Crew" is set in mono.
- Mono set on the same line as the sans comes out sitting high: the two faces carry their text at different heights, and a row centers boxes rather than baselines. `.mono-inline` in `styles.css` is the pixel that puts them back on one line, and it is what any mono beside sans wears.
- Radii: `rounded-card` (20px) for cards, `rounded-shell` (30px) for the composer. Buttons, tabs, and inputs are pills.
- A field that holds a paragraph is not a pill, and it is not a card either. `TextArea` in `TextField.tsx` is the pair to `TextField`, solid and on glass, and it wears `rounded-xl`: the curve that reads as a pill on a 36 high row reads as a lozenge on a 70 high one, and a card's 20 on a box that size puts the first line of type inside the bend. Squarer than the pills around it and softer than the card it stands on.
- Icons are Crew's own, and they all come from `src/renderer/src/icons`. Nothing imports an icon library. Every mark is drawn through `glyph()` in `src/renderer/src/components/glyph.tsx`, on a 24 grid with a round 2 stroke, against the keylines in `src/renderer/src/icons/keylines.ts`: a square sits on 17, a circle on 18.5, a rect keeps `sqrt(w * h)` at 17, a diagonal takes the whole 19.5 live area, a bare run of line sits on 15, a solid on 16. Equal on the ruler is not equal to the eye, which is the whole reason a circle is not 17. `yarn icons` draws the set on one page and measures every mark against its own family, and `tests/icons.test.ts` holds it there. The skill in `.claude/skills/crew-icons` is the rest of it. Three sets stand beside `icons/` for now and are drawn the same way: `src/renderer/src/design/glyphs.tsx` for the canvas, `src/renderer/src/components/toolGlyphs.tsx` for what an agent did, and `src/renderer/src/components/doc/docGlyphs.tsx` for the blocks a doc is written in. The one mark that moves, `ThinkingMark.tsx`, is drawn to the same grid from the numbers `toolGlyphs.tsx` holds. Nothing else draws an icon by hand.
- A provider wears its own mark rather than one of Crew's, and it is the one place in the app that draws a picture nobody here made. The five in `src/renderer/src/media/providers` are the vendors' own app icons, kept as they were given, and `ProviderMark` draws one as a rounded square that reads its size off the class it is handed. An icon in `icons/` is Crew speaking, and a mark saying which model is about to run has to be the one somebody already knows off their own dock, so drawn again on the 24 grid it would be Crew's drawing of somebody else's logo, which is the one thing a logo does not survive.
- A mark is rimmed by an `InsetRing` in the foreground at an opacity, and that is the one thing about it said in a color that flips. Two of the five are black to their own edge and two are white to theirs, while the surface under them goes from near black to near white with the theme, so whichever pair matches the surface has no edge at all and reads as a mark floating on the panel. The foreground is the opposite of the surface by definition, so `ring-fg/5` is white over a black icon in dark mode, near black over a white one in light, and gone into the pair that already stood away. It is faint on purpose: the rim is there to give the two marks that reach their own edge an edge to stand on, and anything stronger reads as a frame the artwork stops short of. A fixed white or black is one of those two cases drawn wrong. It is inset and takes the tile's own radius, since a ring outside the box is cut the moment the row it stands in clips.
- Every mark is drawn at the size it was given, and the rim is why that holds. Grok's body is a circle at about half its own frame where the other four carry theirs to about three quarters, and the rest of its bounding box is two hairline tips that are gone by the 16 it is worn at, so on the ruler they match and to the eye Grok is the small one. A zoom was tried on it and taken back out: what it wanted was an edge rather than a scale, and rimmed the five read level with nobody's artwork touched. `tests/provider-mark.test.ts` holds both.
- A divider inside a popover, a menu or a hover card runs edge to edge. It bleeds back through the padding the card holds its content in, rather than stopping short of both sides. `MenuDivider` does that for a menu, `CardRule` for a hover card. A panel that draws its own rows, like the Actions palette or the review card, takes `flush` on the `Popover` and holds its own padding, so a rule on one of those rows is already the full width. Never hand a `Popover` a padding class to cancel the one it wears: the two weigh the same, which one wins is decided by the order Tailwind happened to write them in, and the divider quietly stops short on both sides.
- Anything that floats stands above whatever opened it, and the app has one ladder for that. Chrome is `z-30` and `z-40`, the toasts are `z-[45]`, panels and the things the huddle puts on screen are `z-50`, a dialog or a takeover is `z-[60]`, and `Popover`, `Tooltip` and `HoverCard` are `z-[70]` above all of it. That last one is the whole point of the ladder rather than a detail of it: a modal can hold a menu, the agent maker holds two, and a floating layer underneath the dialog layer draws every one of those behind the card that opened it, which reads as a button that does nothing. Nothing is ever drawn above `z-[70]`, and a modal is never opened from a popover that is still open, which is the one case the order could not settle.
- The page is one rung of that ladder rather than a set of them, and `isolate` on the column in `App` is what holds it there. A number written inside the page is the page's own from then on, so the design board's header standing over the top bar is a thing settled between those two and nothing else, and the rail, the window corner and everything above them stand over the whole page whatever it does. Without it the two `z-50`s were a tie broken by the order the tree happens to be written in, and the header drew over the rail hovering above it.
- A popover opened from inside a popover stands beside it in the body rather than inside it, so every click on the one on top used to read as a click outside the one underneath, and the pair went down together. `Popover` tells the ones it stands inside where it is, all the way up, so a click or a scroll anywhere in the stack is inside every popover in it. That comes with using `Popover` and nothing has to be handed around for it, which is what lets the photo picker's own menu open off the face in the profile menu.
- A popover never closes because the page under it moved. The thread scrolls itself while an agent works, and every menu opened over it went down with the first scroll event that reached the window, which reads as a menu vanishing mid-aim. It follows instead: one hung off a button reads that button's box every frame, and one opened at a point takes hold of whatever was under the point and moves by however far that has moved since. A scroll listener is not what does this, because content landing above a message moves it without anything scrolling at all. A menu whose anchor has gone off the top of the view holds its place rather than closing, and a click outside or Escape is still the way out.
- A tooltip and a hover card do go down when the page under them moves, and the page under them is the one they are standing on rather than any page in the app. A scroll is listened for on the window in the capture phase, so every scroller anywhere reaches every one of them: the thread following a run took down the tooltip on Show panel and the card on the composer's own header line, neither of which had moved a pixel. `movedBy` in `components/movedBy.ts` is the one rule, and it asks whether the scroller that fired really holds the thing the card or the tip hangs off. The same question decides the other direction, which is `hoverCardIn` rather than a plain `hoverCardOpen`: a thread holds its own scrolling still for a card being read inside it, and a card being read in the chrome above it is no reason for the run to stop following. `tests/floating-scroll-probe.test.ts` holds both.
- Popovers and menus use the `.glass` class: semi-transparent dark, backdrop blur and saturation, like Mobbin. Chrome that floats over the design canvas adds `.glass-strong`, because the artwork behind it can be any color and a white frame turns plain glass into pale grey. That one is deeper and blurs twice as hard: a white shape on the canvas reads straight through an ordinary tint, and the rule is that what comes through a floating panel is the color of what is behind it, never its edges. The look is held in `--glass-bg`, `--glass-line` and `--glass-shadow`, so anything that has to wear glass without the class, like the chrome a library draws for itself, reads the same three values.
- How far off opaque a panel may sit is decided by the words underneath it, and the pill wrote that scale down first: nothing shows through at 0.97, at 0.9 the line under it is a shape rather than a sentence, and by 0.84 the words are legible. Ordinary glass sat at 0.78 and the rail at 0.72, both under the pill's own floor, so a menu opened over the rail stood there with a thread title readable straight through it. The tint is what has to do that work rather than the blur, because a blur is the one part of this a machine can decline: it is the compositor's, it is gone in software rendering and gone again anywhere there is no page behind the window, and a panel that only reads as a panel while the GPU is willing is a panel that is see-through on somebody's laptop. Measured over the hardest backdrop there is, black against white, 0.78 let 56 levels through and 0.92 lets 20, and that 20 is the tint's alone. The two that stand under the line stand over something that is not the app: the pinned rail is a hole onto the macOS material, and the music bar is thin on purpose so the cover's own light can come up through it. `tests/glass-rule.test.ts` holds every one of them and names those two.
- Nothing on glass is set in a solid grey. Glass lifts with whatever is behind it, and a photo under a panel raises it past `fg-faint` and `fg-muted`, which then vanish. Text and marks on a floating panel take the foreground color at an opacity, `text-fg/45` for a quiet label and `text-fg/70` for a row, so they always sit above the surface rather than beside it.
- Nothing in the app is selectable until it says it is. The app is a window rather than a page, so the base layer in `styles.css` takes `user-select` off the body, and a field, a textarea and anything editable get it back at once. A button, a tab, a label, a heading and the line under an empty screen are things to press and read, and a drag that started anywhere near one used to paint half the chrome blue. Content asks for it back with `select-text` on the box that holds it, and only there: what somebody wrote, what an agent said, a plan, a diff, a command and what it printed. A marker standing inside one of those boxes wears `select-none` of its own, the `+` and `−` down a diff and the `$` in front of a command, so what comes away is the code and not the gutter. A click that closes what it is written over, the way a thought folds up, asks `selecting()` first, or a drag to copy the words takes them off the screen as it lets go. A placeholder is the one thing in a field nobody wrote, so a field only says it is selectable once it holds something: empty, it is a line of the app's own words standing in a box, and being inside a field is what painted it over on a select all. Nothing is lost by shutting it, since an empty field has nothing to take away, and it opens again on the first keystroke. The placeholder rule stays outside the CSS layer so unlayered library styles cannot override it. `tests/selection-rule.test.ts` holds the default and `tests/selection-probe.test.ts` holds the pair, a name shut and a message open, and `yarn selection-check` builds the real stylesheet, selects everything in a real window, and reads the pixels.
- The selection band is written down once, as `--color-selection`, and `::selection` reads it. Anything opaque drawn over text a person can select hides that band and has to paint it back: the composer covers the system emoji with a patch of its own surface, so it takes the textarea's selection and paints `bg-selection` under the sprite for any emoji the highlight reaches. Without it a highlighted line reads as a hole where every emoji stands.
- A picture standing on a character takes the room that character takes and never a pixel more, and that rule is about the caret rather than about the spacing. An emoji anywhere somebody can type is the machine's own glyph with one of ours drawn over it, and the caret beside it is drawn against the character's own edge and runs the height of the line, so the size of the picture is the whole of whether a caret can be seen. Drawn as tall as the line it takes the caret away outright, which is what a patch the height of the composer's own row was doing. Drawn as wide as the character it takes away one of the two sides, since a caret is drawn just left of the place it marks: the one after an emoji lands inside the character's own box and the one before it lands in the character in front. So the picture is a square shorter than the line, and in a doc it gives back the column at each edge as well. A line with a picture in it that cannot be typed beside is a line people quietly stop using.
- Nothing verifies that, and it is worth saying why rather than leaving a gap. A caret only paints in a window that has focus, so the one check that could read it would take the screen off whoever is working, which is not a thing to run on somebody's machine. `tests/emoji-caret-rule.test.ts` holds the rule that decides it instead, read off the stylesheet and off the composer, the way `tests/selection-rule.test.ts` holds the selection default.
- `color: transparent` is what takes the machine's own emoji away, and it is the only thing that does: it is a bitmap glyph on a Mac, so nothing about the font size or the family hides it, and it is why a doc can draw the sheet over the character it was written as without touching what is written down. It works inside a textarea as well as in ordinary text.
- **Anything set in a transparent color has to say what color the caret beside it is.** A caret with nothing said about it takes the color of the text it stands in, and the caret after a character belongs to that character rather than to the word after it, so hiding a glyph hides the caret next to it and the line reads as one that can only be typed into from the left. The composer never had it because its field already wears `caret-fg`, and the doc had it until `caret-color` was said on the span. It is not a thing the size of anything drawn over the character can answer, which is what made it look like a painting problem for as long as it did.
- Reusable primitives in `src/renderer/src/components`: `Avatar`, `AvatarStack`, `AgentIcon`, `Pill`, `Spinner`, `Skeleton`, `InsetRing`, `Popover`/`MenuItem`, `Modal`, `ScreenSwap`, `Select`, `SearchField`, `Tooltip`, `HoverCard`, `Composer`, `TopBar`, `DayDivider`, `ZoomView`, `Toaster`. Use them before writing new ones. Never use the native `title` attribute, use `Tooltip`. When something should be a primitive and there is no primitive for it yet, write one rather than a one-off.
- `Skeleton` is what stands in for content that has not arrived. One shimmer, and every instance is pinned to the same start time the way `Spinner` is, so a stack of them breathes together instead of rippling. Its own class carries `position`, and a plain CSS rule beats a Tailwind utility whichever order they are written in, so a skeleton that has to float over something goes inside a positioned wrapper rather than being handed `absolute`. Handed it directly, it keeps `relative`, collapses to nothing, and reads as an empty box.
- A tooltip never shows while the popover it opens is open. Pass `disabled` to the `Tooltip` around any button that opens a `Popover`.
- A line cut down to fit stands its whole self up on hover. The head of a thread's composer holds one line for what the thread is about, and the message it was opened on runs to paragraphs, so hovering it opens the rest of it in a card rather than sending somebody to the top of the thread to read what they are already looking at. `ThreadAsk` is that line and `askHasMore` is when there is anything to open: cut off in its own box, cut short by the title, or written over more lines than the one the header draws. A card that only says the line again is a card earning nothing.
- A `HoverCard` handed nothing to say does not stand, and that is said in the card rather than by whoever wraps it. A caller that swaps the card out for the plain thing underneath takes that thing out of the tree and puts it back, which loses whatever it had measured about itself, so a line that opens a card once it knows it is truncated would measure itself, wrap, unwrap, and settle on saying nothing.
- A card holding more than it can show is scrolled inside itself, so the page moving under it takes it down and its own scrolling does not. Read the plain way, one turn of the wheel closes the card being read.
- The box a `Tooltip` puts around what it wraps is a flex one, so the button inside it is the whole of it. An inline box would carry a line of text it has no text for, and the few pixels a descender leaves under the button are what push it off center in every row it stands in. Anything else that wraps a control for positioning has to be flex for the same reason.
- Agents always render `AgentIcon` (a deterministic generated pet seeded by the agent id), never an initial `Avatar`. Humans keep `Avatar`.
- A pet stands white on a picture of its own. The background is the same scene the music covers are photographed in, worked out from the agent's id, and `GeneratedField` is the one place that field is built: a helper's mark wears it through its own silhouette and a face wears it through a circle, so the two are one picture in two shapes rather than the same three layers written twice. The pet over it is solid white with its eyes cut out rather than painted, the way the arrow is cut out of the tile it stands in, so what comes through them is whatever the picture is doing behind rather than one fixed color over a surface that changes under it. Nothing is drawn under the white: a shadow lifts a mark off a photograph and it also makes it an object standing on one, and a face is a shape rather than a sticker. The hue the pet was seeded with is still there and is still the whole of what a board cursor takes its color from, since a cursor has to be one legible color and not a picture.
- Nothing a pet is made of comes to a corner. There are two families so that not every one of them is a blob, the curved one closed with quadratics through its midpoints and the angular one with flat sides and a count, and the angular one is turned at every vertex by `CORNER` rather than pointed. The cut is held under a share of the shorter of the two edges meeting there, or a corner on a short edge reaches past the middle of it, the two cuts cross, and the outline folds inside out. The stroke's own round join was already trying to do this at 3.5 and could not be seen doing it at the size a pet is worn.
- The picture under a face is stood back from the light, `FIELD_LIGHT`, so the white shape is the brightest thing in the tile. It is taken off the picture rather than laid over it as an ink: less light keeps every color in the palette and what each one is beside the others, where a black scrim is a grey film that flattens the whole of it and would put back the near black the covers are written to keep out. A helper's mark is the picture itself and carries nothing white, so it is left at the light a cover is photographed in, and the number is `pet.ts`'s rather than the field's for that reason. It matters most where a cover never has to work: a cover is looked at on its own and a face is worn at 20 across in a row of them, so the pale palettes are the ones this is set for.
- How much of the tile the pet takes is `BODY`, and it is applied to the shape rather than built into it, because it is a drawing decision and not something the seed answers. At its own size the body fills the circle and what is left of the picture is a rim, which reads as a white shape on a colored ring rather than as a shape standing on a photograph.
- The gap between the two eyes is opened at the size the pet is really drawn at, never seeded wider. Two holes closer together than `MIN_BRIDGE` are one slot, and a slot across the middle of a white shape is not a face: at 20 across, which is what a pet is worn at in a mention menu, the narrowest pair a seed hands out leaves under half a pixel between them and they merge. Equal on the ruler is not equal to the eye, the same reason a circle is not 17 on the keylines, and opening it at draw time is what keeps one pet one pet at every size rather than redrawing everybody's face for the sake of the smallest one it is worn at.
- `art/pet.ts` holds the pet's numbers and `AgentIcon` draws them, the same split the covers and the games hold, which is what lets `yarn covers` draw every face on one page and a suite read one without a canvas in the room. The order `makePet` reads its stream in is never changed: the numbers come off one stream, and reading them another way is a different face for everybody who already has one, which is why the corners were turned inside the shape it already had rather than by drawing another. `tests/agent-face-probe.test.ts` pins it.
- Either one can wear a photo somebody uploaded, and both look it up themselves rather than being handed it: `AgentIcon` by the agent's id, `Avatar` by the person's name, which is what a message carries and what the host keys people by. The one exception is the tray panel, which has no session to look anything up in and is handed the picture. `PhotoPicker` is how a face is changed, and it goes around whichever mark it is: the face itself is the control, hovered and clicked, and nothing anywhere says photo in words. What comes up on it is the upload mark, since the one thing a click does is put a picture on, and the scrim it arrives with stops under the presence dot rather than over it: the dot says whether somebody is here, and a face being hovered is no reason to stop saying it. That is a `z-10` on the dot inside `Avatar` and `AgentIcon`, because the scrim is drawn by the picker standing outside them and would otherwise win on order alone. It stands wherever your own face does, on your row in the Crew tab and on the face the profile menu opens on. An agent's photo is the owner's to set, and a person's is nobody's but their own: `member.avatar` carries no id, so the only face it can reach is the one who sent it.
- A mark never says where it sits in the row it is handed to. `Avatar`, `AgentIcon` and `AgentPhoto` carried `self-start` of their own, which beats the `items-center` of every row they stand in, so the pet rode high in the mention menu, the reply quote, the thread bar and the ask row. The row decides: `items-center` for a face beside one line, `items-start` on the few that hold a face beside a paragraph, which is a chat message and a feed card.
- Small interactions matter: hover states on everything interactive, `animate-pop` for popovers, `animate-rise` for feed items, scale on press.
- **Hover, focus, selected and active backgrounds on separate controls never touch or overlap.** A list whose rows can paint a state keeps a visible gap between every row, including when two adjacent controls are in different states. The separation comes from the parent layout so every pair holds it. A control inside another painted control uses text, a mark or a ring for its state instead of laying a second background over the first.
- A button never grows on hover. Press is where a scale belongs, and a bar the width of a column swelling under the pointer reads as a wobble rather than as a lift. A solid button says it is hovered by going off white, `hover:bg-fg/90`, and an outlined one by its border and a wash.
- **A hidden control never moves a visible one when it appears.** Anything revealed on hover or focus either stands outside the stable layout or has its room held in every state. The remove action in `PluginRow` stands before Open or Connect without taking their place in the row, so the visible action stays at the same edge before and after the pointer arrives. `tests/plugins-probe.test.ts` holds both installed plugin states there.
- **An invisible icon never pushes a visible icon out of place.** A control revealed on hover is anchored outside the row's flex layout. If a status mark owns that edge while the control is hidden, both marks share the same anchored position and trade through opacity. The hidden control leaves no blank slot beside the visible mark.
- A box only clips while something is really travelling through it. `ScreenSwap` in a card wears `overflow-hidden` for the length of a screen change and `overflow-visible` the rest of the time, because anything standing past its own edge at rest is cut: a pill that presses, a ring, a shadow. The whole way in was drawn with flat sides down both edges before this.
- **A card carries its width as well as its height, or half a change is a movement and the other half is a jump.** `ScreenSwap` took the height alone, which is the whole of it where every screen is one width and half of it where they are not: the plus in the composer holds its rows, the agents that are here and the whole GIF panel, and measured in a real window those are 170, 226 and 382 across. `width` is what says the screens size themselves, and it is asked for rather than assumed, because a card that pins its own width is the other half of the app, the track menu and the branches among them, and their rows truncate against that width rather than standing at it: sized by what is in them they would blow out to the longest name in the list. The screens wear the card's own corners for the same reason the card does, since a screen travelling through a box that clips is cut square at the bend otherwise.
- **A popover standing above what opened it is pinned by its bottom edge, never by a top worked back from its height.** The two put the box in the same place and only one of them holds while the box is moving. A height comes back through a `ResizeObserver`, which is a render after the paint that changed it, so a card growing 300 pixels over a fifth of a second walked its own bottom edge down the screen and snapped it back on every frame of the way, over the composer it was opened from. Said as a `bottom` there is nothing to read and nothing to put back, and the box grows upward on its own. Both clamps are the ones that were already there, so a card too tall for the room above still stands 8 from the top of the window, and `tests/typography-popover-probe.test.ts` holds the placing and which edge is really carrying it.
- Nothing that fills a scroller may move. Padding is inside a height, so a box at `min-h-full` is already the whole of what the scroller can hold, and eight pixels of an arrival push it past the bottom: a scrollbar comes for the length of the animation, takes ten pixels off the width while it is there, and hands them back, which reads as the page jerking sideways as it lands. The way in animates the content inside that box, which has the padding to move in, rather than the box itself. Anything else that arrives, rises or springs inside a scroller is held to the same rule.
- Nothing is brought into view by asking the page. `scrollIntoView` moves every scroller standing between the element and the document, and two of the app's own show no scrollbar, so whatever they lose can never be put back by hand: `#root`, which clips the whole app, and the side panel's own box, which is a scroll container across forever because its left border leaves the content a pixel wider than the box it stands in. A tab asking to be brought into view takes the panel 186 across while the panel is still opening and leaves it there, and it lands at 1 rather than 0 every other time. The same call the moment that box has any height to give clips the header by 17, which is the pill's own offset inside a 70 high header, and there is no gesture that puts it back.
- `scrollInto.ts` is the one way, and both halves of it are handed the box they may move rather than reaching for whatever is above them. `bringInto` moves the least it can and `centerIn` puts a line in the middle, which is what a file opened on a change wants. Handed nothing it finds the nearest thing that really scrolls, and a box that only clips is never one of those, which is the whole of what keeps it off the two above. A tree row deep in a recursion is the case that asks for nothing, since there is no ref to hand it. `tests/scroll-into.test.ts` holds what each of them moves and `tests/scroll-rule.test.ts` holds that nothing anywhere asks the page.
- **The top of a page is the top of the window, and a message is read the whole way up to it.** Nothing is held opaque under the bar. It was 70 pixels of ink-900 behind the bar and 40 of gradient below that, and then a 112 scrim with its first 48 held, and both of those cut the column an inch short: what was left over the top of every chat and every thread was a band of the app spent on nothing, with the words dissolving before they reached it. `--page-scrim` is the fade, 56, and `.page-scrim` is the ramp it is drawn with, full at the window's own edge and eased to nothing by the end, so a line crosses the whole of the bar's band and it is the window that takes it. Read off a white block the ink left standing is 1 at the edge, 0.26 at the middle of the bar and gone by 54. What that costs is a line passing behind the faces at the right of the bar, and it is the right price: they are opaque marks on a column that is 660 wide and centered, so they are only ever met in a narrow one, and a strip of dead chrome is met on every screen in the app.
- The ease is the half that matters rather than the length. Alpha falls at a constant rate down a plain `to-transparent` gradient and the eye does not, so a linear ramp reads as a grey band with a shelf at each end. A smootherstep leaves the window's edge at no slope at all and reaches transparent the same way, so there is no corner at either end to find: the biggest step down the whole run reads as nine levels out of 235 with a kink of three, against the 235 of the design board's own band.
- Where a page comes to rest is a second number, `--page-rest`, 112, taken off the variable by the chat column and the thread column rather than written out as a padding in each. They are two numbers because they answer two things, how far a line stays readable as it goes and where the first line of a short chat stands, and that one has to clear the bar rather than rest behind the faces. The ramp itself is `--scrim-ramp`, written once and read by both scrims, since the design board fades in its own colour rather than in the app's and only the colour and the depth differ. `tests/page-scrim.test.ts` holds the ramp, the resting gap and the board's own fade, and `scripts/scrim-check.mjs` is the only thing that can see the rest of it, because it stands the real chrome over a real column in a real window and reads the pixels back.
- **The room above and below what somebody is typing belongs to the scroller rather than to the composer.** Held by the card it is a band of the app standing over a line cut in half: a long message scrolls under it and stops short of the top with dead surface above, and the same at the foot with the send button an inch under a severed line. Inside the box that room scrolls away with the words, so a line passing either edge runs right to it and a message at rest still stands off both by the same measure the card holds at its sides. `--composer-scrim` is that room, and the two fades are as deep, painted in the composer's own surface and masked with `--scrim-ramp` and `--scrim-ramp-up`, so what leaves the box dissolves the way a line leaving a page does. The card clips for it, since anything run to a rounded edge stands past the corner otherwise, and that is the one thing the composer really has travelling through it. What is attached is none of it: the tray stands above the box in the card's own padding and the words scroll up under it. `COMPOSER_MAX` is grown by both fades, or a box that gave its room to the scroller shows two fewer lines than it did. `yarn composer-look` is the only thing that can see any of this, since a fade that lands on the leading above a line is invisible in every number a suite can read.
- A scroller that means to have no bar wears `no-scrollbar` rather than saying it in a utility of its own. The app draws its own scrollbar and it takes ten pixels of whatever it stands in, so a horizontal one under a row of pills grows the row rather than clipping it, and a vertical one lands over the rows in a menu. Two places wrote the standard property and the webkit pseudo out by hand and six wrote only one of the two.
- Labels like "You" go in a `Pill`, never in parentheses.
- No logo or branding beyond the word "Crew". No emoji in the UI. Gradients only as scrims where content scrolls under chrome.
- The app icon is three overlapping discs, the same stack of members the app draws everywhere else. All three are the same size, each cut out of the one behind it by a mask, so nothing shrinks a disc. The tile is glass like the system icons: a top-to-bottom gradient, a sheen over the top half, and a rim that catches light at the top and bottom. Black and white, and it flips with the theme picked in the app.
- The same three discs are the mark in the top left of the app, in place of the word "crew", and they stand at the head of the way in for the same reason. `CrewMark` draws them at whatever `currentColor` is, and `live` is the one in the top bar, which `CrewLogo` wraps in the button that hovers and clicks it. `CrewLogo` takes the height it should be, so the one on the way in is the same mark at a larger size rather than a second drawing of it. Nothing anywhere sets the word in type.
- Touching it, the crew arrives. The three discs come in left to right, each one sliding in from its own right and settling into the stack the way faces do as people join a session, and the light runs across them as the last one lands. It is the mask that moves rather than the paint: a disc carries its own bite with it, so the gaps between them stay the width they are however far into its arrival a disc has got. Animating the paint instead closes the gaps as the discs grow.
- A disc slides in from the right and never from the left, whichever way round the queue runs. Coming from the left it starts life inside the bite the disc in front has already taken out of it, so the first thing to arrive is a crescent under half a pixel across, which reads as a hairline flashing at the end of the mark. From the right it starts in open space and is only carved once it is big enough for the gap to be the gap.
- What is behind the mask while it is touched is a mesh, cool to warm across the whole width, so each disc is a window onto one field rather than three tinted discs. It is the app icon's glass splitting light, which is the one thing the mark can say in color without becoming a second brand. Nothing in the palette is an ink or a near white, the same rule the covers hold. At rest it is `currentColor` and nothing else, and the mesh only drifts while the pointer is on it.
- A disc springs past its own edge as it lands, so the live mark stands in a box with `ROOM` around it rather than in the mark's own box. Pinned to the mark, the overshoot is cut off flat at the viewport, and a circle cut by a straight line is a thin white bar standing at the end of the mark for the few frames the spring lasts. The box still clips, which is what keeps the light and the flash from painting past it, and the drawing is asked for by the height the mark should be rather than by a class, so the room never shrinks the mark. `BLEED` is the other half of it: the mask region and every rect inside it run wider than the box, so nothing runs out of paint on the way through.
- Nothing the mark paints may take the pointer, and it is never redrawn to replay. Overflowing paint is still hit tested, so the light crossing under the cursor read as arriving at the button and leaving it again for as long as it travelled, and remounting the drawing under a pointer already standing on it has React say it arrived once more, so between them a single hover fired forever. The button's own box is the whole of what can be hovered, `CrewLogo` restarts the three one-shot animations through `getAnimations` rather than through a `key`, and a pointer already inside is not an arrival.
- The sound is `crew.mark`, three bubbles panned the way the discs land and rising through A major, with the low one and the sparks arriving under the light. It is the same material as the music, so the voices it is built from stand above `STRIKES` in `sounds.ts` rather than below it.
- A build run from source wears a blueprint: vivid blue paper, ruled and lightly grained, with the same three discs on it lit like glass. It is the one place in the project that is blue, so a dev window is never mistaken for the installed app sitting beside it in the dock. Both dev icons stand on the same paper, so only the mark flips with the theme, black in dark and white in light, the way the shipping pair flips.
- A disc is lit from the upper left and built in layers: a body that falls away to the far side, a shade drawn in all round the edge, a light rim on the top where the light arrives, a softer one on the bottom where the tile throws it back, a specular where the light lands, and a shadow cast into the gap behind. The two rims are the same ring gradient held to opposite halves by a mask, which is what makes a flat circle read as a bead. Every gradient is in bounding box units, so one set of them shades all three discs the same way. Keep it gentle: a black edge and a blown specular turn glass into a toy.
- `src/main/from-source.ts` decides which of the two a window wears, from where the app is loaded. Never `app.isPackaged` for this: that only asks whether the binary is still called Electron, and `yarn dev` renames it to Crew, so a run from source claims to be packaged and wears the shipping icon. The one way past it is `CREW_SHIPPING_ICON`, which `yarn start` sets, so the shipping icon can be seen without installing. Nothing else loosens the path check.
- Each disc is cut only by the discs standing in front of it, one mask each. A single mask for the whole stack reopens the gaps, which is invisible on the filled icon and turns the blueprint outlines into crossing rings.
- `yarn icon` redraws all of it from `scripts/make-icon.mjs` into `resources/` (`icon.svg`, `icon-light.svg`, `icon-dev.svg`, `icon-dev-light.svg`, `crew-logo.svg`, `tray.svg`, `icon.icns`, `icon.png`), `src/main/icon-png.ts`, `src/main/tray-png.ts` and `src/renderer/src/components/crew-mark.ts`. That is the only place the geometry lives. Nothing there is edited by hand.

## Layout

- `src/shared` — protocol and event types, git exec helper, no dependencies
- `src/server` — host: ws server, session state, `.crew/` persistence, git sync
- `src/runner` — agent runner: CLI provider adapters, executes prompts, streams back, auto-pulls the repo while joined
- `src/main` — Electron main and preload, wires server and runner to the app
- `src/renderer` — React app
- `tests` — integration suites

## The toolbox

A panel of tiles under the top bar, holding the tools a crew builds for itself. They are shared the way todos are, so everyone gets the same toolbox.

- Nothing the app can do by itself is in it. The terminal, the project files, what has changed, the music and the games are all things the side panel holds, and `PANEL_OPENS` already lists every one of them in the panel's own menu, so a tile for each was the same list a second time in another shape, standing over the tools the crew really built. The huddle is started from the composer's own plus, where a call is one of the things a message can be. What is left is one panel that is only ever the crew's own work, and one place to look for the app's.
- A tool is one button: a name, a mark, and the single thing it does. It opens a page, runs a command, opens a file, a doc or a board, asks an agent, says something in the chat, adds a task, writes a line in a doc, puts music on, copies something, or runs several of the crew's own tools in a row. `cleanTool` in `src/shared/toolbox.ts` is the whole of what a tool may be, and one with no name or nothing to do comes back as null rather than as a button that sits there doing nothing.
- Three columns. A tile is the size it has always been and the panel is as wide as three of them and the padding, rather than the columns being cut to fit a width somebody picked: change the count and the width is worked out from the tile, never the other way round. The row ends on the empty slot that opens the builder, which is the way in and the empty state both, so the panel never holds a second way to do the same thing.
- The toolbox is a builder rather than chrome, so a tile is set on `rounded-tile`, squarer than the pills the rest of the app wears.
- A mark is one of the app's own drawings or one emoji, and `ToolMarkView` draws both, so the two are always the same size. The size is read off the class it is handed, which is where a glyph already reads its own weight from.
- **A tool is written on the card a schedule is written on, and it is one card rather than two that look alike.** What to call it, a rule, then what it does and the one field or list that kind asks for: `ToolDoes` in `src/renderer/src/components/ToolDoes.tsx` is that half, handed the kinds it may offer, and the toolbox hands it all of them where a schedule hands it the ones that can be put on a clock. Written twice they were already apart, a screen of rows in a 262 panel against a line of pills in a card, and the second one to move would have taken the two further. `cardParts.tsx` is the grammar both are set in, a line of pills, a rule that bleeds through the card's own padding, and the lists a choice is picked out of.
- It was a stack of screens inside the panel before that, and what it cost was the room. A panel 262 across cannot hold a paragraph, so choosing a mark and choosing what a tool does were each a screen you left the form for, and every field under them was a column narrower than the sentence going into it. A card is 520, so the choices are pills on one line with a menu under each, which is what the schedule already did.
- The card is raised from `state/toolBuilder.ts` and mounted once in `App`, the way the settings card is, and the toolbox goes as it opens. A floating layer is drawn above the dialog layer, so a popover left standing behind the card is a menu sitting on top of the thing it just opened, which is the one case the ladder cannot settle.
- The mark stays a control on that card where a schedule's is worked out from the kind. A schedule's is a picture of a decision nobody made, so it is nothing worth a row of the card, and a tool's is the face its tile wears and the only place it is chosen.
- `TOOL_KINDS` in `src/renderer/src/components/toolKinds.tsx` is the one table of what a tool can do. The pill that says which kind is picked and the menu it opens both read it, so the words a person chose from are the words they are shown afterwards. A row is its mark and its title and nothing else: a line under every row of a menu is a paragraph to read for one press, and it is the same second line the rule about headings already refuses. The `note` on each entry is what the table says a kind is for and nothing draws it.
- **A box of words grows with what is written into it and scrolls once it has had its room.** A tool is a sentence and a prompt is a paragraph, and a box that stands at two lines whatever is in it hides the whole of what a schedule will really say. `useAutoResize` is what does it, at a floor of two lines and a ceiling of 200, which is the same hook the composer grows on.
- The emoji grid is the one the reactions use. It is handed its columns, its size and its words, because the same grid reacts to a message in one place and marks a tool in another.
- A command written over several lines is several commands. The newlines are handed to the shell as returns, so each line runs in turn.
- A page opens in the side panel, or in the machine's own browser for a site that will not sit in a frame.
- An ask is a message in the chat, aimed at an agent by id so it cannot be lost to a rename. The agent a tool names may be offline on the machine pressing it, so whoever is here takes the work rather than nothing happening. Saying something is the same message without the agent, so a line written to name somebody still reaches them.
- Three of the kinds put words in the chat and each one is a different hand writing them. Start a thread hands the work over and the thread is there to watch, Send a message has an agent write the line and posts it under that agent's name with no thread anywhere, and Say something posts what you typed. With the notes off the rows the titles are the whole of what tells them apart, and Send a message is the one that does not say on its own whose hand it is: the row beside it is what answers it, since the two that name an agent carry the Ask pill and the one that is your own words carries nothing.
- A line written in a doc lands at the end of the page it names, after whatever is already there, and a page that has since been deleted is nothing rather than a doc written back from a tool. A task goes on the crew's list, for anyone or for the agent it names.
- Music is what everyone here gets, not what the machine pressing it gets. A list plays from the top and carries the list with it, so Next walks the rest of it. A huddle is not among the kinds a tool may be: a second way into the call would be a button that either does nothing or hangs up on everyone, depending on who pressed it.
- A word in braces is a blank the tool asks for when it is pressed, on a screen inside the panel the way choosing a mark is, so one tool covers every search, every branch and every ticket rather than one for each. What is typed lands in every place the tool named it, and it goes into an address as a query rather than as it stands, so several words are still one link. A brace with a dollar in front of it is a shell variable and is left alone. The one line in the builder that says any of this is written in braces itself, which is the whole of the explaining it needs.
- A chain is the crew's own tools, in the order they were picked, and it is built out of what is already in the toolbox rather than out of a second kind of tool nobody can see. It never names the tool being built, a tool it has already reached is passed over, so a pair that name each other is a short run rather than a locked window, and `STEP_LIMIT` is the end of it. Its blanks are asked for once across the whole run.
- Pressing a tool leaves you on the page you were on, whatever it opens. The panel stands beside every page, so a page, a file and a terminal open where somebody is already standing rather than carrying them to the chat to be read, and a tool pressed while reading a doc is a terminal beside that doc. A doc and a board have tabs of their own and go there. Copying, saying something, adding a task, writing a line and putting a track on open nothing at all, so `saidAfter` keeps the toolbox up and the tile says what happened rather than a panel closing over nothing.

## Scheduled tasks

A tool on a clock. It is a page under More in the sidebar, and what it holds is what the crew has asked to happen on its own: a standup message every weekday morning, an agent sent at the week's changes every Friday, a task on the list on the first of the month.

- **It is a `ToolAction` and nothing new**, which is the whole reason every kind of scheduled task worked on the day this landed. `cleanTool` already says what a tool may be and already validates all of it, so asking an agent, saying something, adding a task, writing a line in a doc, putting music on and running several of the crew's own tools in a row are the kinds a schedule has, and they are those kinds because they are the toolbox's. A second table of what a scheduled thing can do would have been the same list written twice, drifting from the first the moment either grew.
- **Only some of them can be scheduled, and the split is whether anybody is standing there.** A tool is pressed by a person, so it may open a page, a terminal or a file on the screen they are at. A schedule fires on the host at four in the morning with every window shut, so the ones that open something have nowhere to open it. `SCHEDULABLE` in `src/shared/schedules.ts` is that list and `cleanSchedule` refuses everything else outright, which is the rule `cleanTool` already holds about a tool with nothing to do: it comes back as null rather than as a row that fires into nothing.
- **The host fires it, the way the host walks the music on.** The clients read one clock, so a timer on the host is one run and a timer in every window is a run per window, which for a schedule that opens a thread is the same work started four times. It is refused from a runner the way the music controls and the leaderboard are, since an agent's machine is connected the whole time it is joined.
- One timer for all of them, armed to the soonest, the way `armMusic` arms itself to the end of the track that is playing. A timer each would be forty of them on a crew that has forty, all to answer one question.
- It is armed no further ahead than `CLOCK_MAX_MS` and comes round on its own. A schedule can be a month out, which is past what a timer will hold at all, and a laptop that slept through the moment it was armed for wakes with the timer still pending and the moment long gone. Coming round answers both, and what it costs is one pass over a handful of rows.
- **A missed run is skipped and never caught up.** A Crew shut over the weekend comes back to one run of each, not to the six it slept through, because six of the same run landing at once is worse than the five that were missed. `due` is what says so and it reads the last run rather than the wall clock behind it.
- Every cadence but the interval is a wall clock in the schedule's own zone, so it is worked out by walking candidate days rather than by adding milliseconds: a day is not always 24 hours long and a month never is. The zone is written down with the schedule, so nine in the morning is nine in the morning for everyone in the crew rather than nine where the host happens to be sitting.
- **The offset is read twice, at the guess and then at the answer.** The offset itself moves on the two days a year a zone changes, and read once both of those put the run an hour out. `instantOf` is that pair of reads and it is the one piece of arithmetic here worth a test of its own.
- What it runs on is said in plain words and never in a cron line: every so many minutes or hours, every day at a time, on the days of the week somebody picked, or on a day of the month. `sayCadence` is the one place a cadence becomes words, so the row says the same thing the card asked for.
- **Firing one by hand never moves when the next one is due.** A run somebody asked for is not the run the schedule was written for, so only the clock's own writes the time down. Without that, pressing it to see what it does quietly pushes tomorrow's run a day out.
- The agent a schedule names may be on a machine that is not here at four in the morning, so whoever is here takes the work rather than nothing happening. That is the rule the toolbox already holds about a tool that names somebody offline.
- A page a note was going to be written on may have been deleted since, and that is nothing rather than a doc written back from a schedule. The toolbox holds the same rule.
- **Asking an agent is two kinds rather than one, and the split is whether there is anything to follow.** Start a thread is the one that was always here: the work goes to an agent, a card stands in the chat, and the thread is there to read while it happens. Send a message asks an agent for a line and posts the answer in the chat under that agent's own name, and nothing else about the run reaches anybody. A standup every weekday morning is the case that asks for it, and as one kind it was a card in the chat saying a thread had started, with the standup itself an inch inside it: the one thing everyone was meant to read was the one thing not on the screen. Both are `post` and `prompt` in `cleanTool` and both are in the toolbox as well, since a tile that has an agent write a line into the chat is the same errand pressed by hand.
- **A message with no thread is the ghost machinery with nobody's window on it.** A ghost thread is already a thread whose events are kept in memory for one socket and never reach the log or anybody else, so a run that has to leave no trace is that with `ws` null on the `Ghost` record. Nothing about the run is written down, nothing rides in the snapshot, and no window is sent a step of it; what lands is the one message at the end. A second hidden-run path written beside the first would have been the same rule twice, and the two would have drifted the first time either moved.
- **The brief is the whole of what makes the message read as a message.** `POST_INSTRUCTIONS` in `src/shared/post.ts` is added to the prompt the way plan mode and voice add their own, and without it a model writes the answer it would write inside a thread: a heading, a preamble about what it was asked to do, and a sign-off. It is never a message in the chat, so the crew reads what the agent said and not what the app told it.
- A helper sent out by one of these is hidden too, and the message waits for it. `hidden` on `startThread` is what carries that down, since a hidden run whose children are not hidden is a hidden run that leaks, and posting while a helper is still out would drop the work it was sent for. A run somebody stopped says nothing at all, and one that fell over or answered nothing says so in the chat rather than leaving a schedule that quietly did nothing.
- The five events are `schedule.added`, `schedule.edited`, `schedule.removed`, `schedule.paused` and `schedule.ran`, folded off the snapshot's own event list the way the toolbox is. A schedule written months ago is still due tomorrow long after its event has fallen off the window, and what it did when it came round is folded onto its own row rather than being a line in the chat every time the clock struck. The work it produced is already in the chat as the thread it opened.
- Pausing is not deleting. A schedule somebody wants to stop for a fortnight is one they wrote and would have to write again, so it keeps its row and stands in its own section.
- It is a page rather than a row in the Tasks panel, and the two are a source and a destination rather than the same thing twice. Tasks is the work in flight: what is being worked on, what needs review, what is done. A schedule is a standing decision that produces that work, and the runs it opens land in Tasks the way any other run does. Put in the panel it would be a definition sitting in a list of live work with no way to tell the two apart, and the panel is 380 across where writing one needs a card.
- `tests/schedules.test.ts` holds the arithmetic with no server in the room, the clocks going forward and back included, and `tests/scheduled.test.ts` boots a real host on loopback from a log written as of a week ago, which is the only way to watch the clock strike without sitting through a real day.

## Settings

A card with a rail of pages down the side, opened by your own face in the top right. Everything that is yours alone and everything the crew shares is on it, so nothing about either has a tab of its own any more.

- The face is the whole way in. There is no menu between the press and the card: a list of the same things one press earlier is a list to read before getting to the thing, and it grew a row every time the app did.
- `SETTINGS_TABS` in `src/renderer/src/components/settings/tabs.ts` is the one table of what there is. The rail and the page it opens both read it, so the word somebody picked is the word at the top of what they get. `state/settings.ts` is which page is up, held outside the tree the way a toast is, so anything can raise it and the card never stands inside whatever opened it.
- Two groups, You and Crew. The first row wears your own face and your own name rather than a mark and a word, because the face is already what says who the page is about.
- Pages stand beside each other rather than in front of each other, so nothing travels between them: one is simply the one that is up, and it fades. A page that slides in says you went somewhere, and picking a row in a rail is not going anywhere. It cannot move either, since a page fills a scroller and eight pixels of arrival is a scrollbar coming and going for the length of it.
- The card is glass, so nothing on it is set in a solid grey. Every word and mark inside takes the foreground at an opacity, `Select` and the agent cards included, which is why those were turned over when they moved here.
- A page is `Page`, `Section` and `Row` from `settings/parts.tsx`, and a row is what it is called on the left and what you do about it on the right. A line under the label only where it says something the row cannot say on its own.
- A rail row has to say what you get, so there is no General and no Advanced, and a page is never one row on its own. This computer is what that rule folds into: whether the machine sleeps, what agents running here may do, and whether `crew` is on this PATH. Three switches that were three pages, and they are one page because they are one idea, what this computer does while Crew is open. Helpers came off the Crew side to reach it, which is also where it always belonged: a helper runs a real CLI on somebody's laptop and spends their tokens, so the group it stood in was telling people the wrong thing.
- Keeping this computer awake is off until somebody says otherwise, because a laptop that never sleeps is a decision nobody made, and the app held that assertion the whole time it was open before this: every machine in every crew was kept awake by nobody's choice, and the setting is that behavior turned into an answer somebody gives. It keeps the machine up and never the screen, since work carries on with the display asleep and a screen lit all night is a cost nobody asked for. `src/shared/awake.ts` is the rule and `src/main/awake.ts` is the hold, split the way the update pill is, so a suite drives it without electron anywhere near it. Several windows on one folder is one machine and several switches, so the hold stands while any window is asking and the last one to stop is what lets go, a window that died with it on included. It lives in that window's `localStorage`, is said again on every start because main holds no copy of its own, and is never sent.
- The crew command is one row on that page: the command ships inside the app and this is the press that puts it on this machine's PATH. A machine with nowhere to put it says so itself, `off` from `commandState`, and the row is left out rather than standing there greyed. That answer is the machine's own rather than the renderer reading what the browser calls the platform, which is a guess made in the one place that cannot know.
- Files is one row, how big a file may be, and it is on the Crew side because it is one number for everyone. The rail already says whose a page is, so the row needs no line under it saying so.
- The agents live here now. The way in stands at the end of the list rather than off in a corner of the page, so it is the empty state and the way in both, the way the toolbox ends its own row on the slot that opens the builder.
- **A row in that list is who the agent is, and every control on it is in the card the pencil opens.** It carried the model, the version and the thinking as pills of their own with an Advanced button beside them, so a list of four agents was a wall of dropdowns to read past before finding the one being looked for, and the name was the smallest thing on the row it names. What is left is the face, the name, what it runs on and whose it is. The card is the maker read back: the face and the name at the head of it, the plain settings as rows, and Advanced one press deeper, so setting an agent up and changing it later are one screen rather than two that drifted apart. Nothing on it is saved, since every row writes as it is changed and the name is written down once it is settled, which is the rule the address on the Scribe page already holds.
- **What an agent is set up on is one list, whatever it is made of.** A row for a model, a paragraph for its instructions and a group of switches all stand as siblings with a hairline between them, and a group's title stands on that list under the rule the row above it draws rather than in a block of its own. `SettingSections` is that flat list and `SettingRows` is the rows. Written as the settings pages are written, each group was a `Section` carrying a page's own rhythm, which is 28 points of air over a 14 point row and a rule dropped at the foot of every group, so a card of three groups read as three cards adrift with the rules only under one of them. A page has the room for that and a 460 wide card does not.
- Nothing here is a second way to do something the app already does elsewhere. The huddle is the sidebar's, and the music plays from its own panel: what the settings hold of the music is the one thing that is yours alone, which is how loud it is where you sit.
- What a working run says about itself is on the Appearance page: the count of its tokens, which is on, and what those tokens are worth, which is off. `state/prefs.ts` holds the pair the way `theme.ts` holds the theme, so they are yours alone and never sent, and the crew sees whatever each of them has chosen. Both numbers are gathered whichever way the pair is set, so turning one on says something about the run already going rather than only about the next one.
- A price comes from the CLI wherever the CLI works one out, because that is the real figure rather than an estimate of it: Claude prices its own turn against the model it really used, cached prefixes and all, and that stands in place of anything worked out here. Everything else is priced off `MODEL_RATES` in `src/shared/pricing.ts`, which is the one table of rates and the one place a model is added. A model with no row there draws no price at all rather than a zero: a wrong number costs more than a missing one, and a zero reads as a run that was free.
- What a call of a model cost is read once, by `usageFrom` in `src/runner/providers/tokens.ts`, because the CLIs disagree about one of the four numbers. Anthropic counts a cached prefix beside the input and OpenAI counts it inside, and read the wrong way round the same prefix is charged twice at full rate. The end of a turn is `total` and stands in place of the calls it is made of rather than beside them, or every token in it lands twice.
- Feedback is a row on the About page, since it is about the app rather than about you or about the crew. It opens the card a memory and an emoji are added on, because writing feedback and writing a memory are the same question asked once: one field, one press, and the words are never a second screen away. It is not a rail row of its own, since a page whose whole body is one field is a page that is one row.
- The card is the field and the two buttons and nothing else. Crew's version and the system go on the mail underneath what was written, and no line anywhere says so: a sentence explaining what is being attached is the machinery read aloud, and it stands over the one thing the card is for.
- There is nothing behind it, so it is a `mailto` and the machine's own mail app is what sends it. `src/shared/feedback.ts` is the whole of what one is, kept out of the card so it can be read without a window near it: the address, the subject, and the body.
- A machine with no mail app set up fails exactly the way one with a mail app succeeds, so `shell:openExternal` answers whether it really opened. Silence there is somebody's feedback that nobody ever receives, which is the same rule the music holds about a track that will not load. Refused, the card stays where it is with the words still in it, says the address, and copies the whole of it on one press.
- `FEEDBACK_LIMIT` is well under what a shell will read, because every space is three characters by the time the shell sees it and prose comes out around a quarter longer than it was written. The field stops taking words at the limit rather than the end being cut off on the way out, and anything past what a shell will take is caught by the copy either way.

## What a plan has left

The bars on an agent card: how much of the five hour window and of the week has gone. Every number on them comes from the provider, and none of them is worked out here.

- Claude's are read off the same endpoint Claude Code's own usage screen reads, with the sign-in the CLI already holds on this machine. Codex writes its rate limits into its session rollouts as it runs, so those are read off the disk.
- **An access token that has run out is not a machine that has been signed out.** It lasts eight hours and the CLI refreshes it out of its own refresh token whenever it runs, so a laptop nobody has run Claude on since the morning has an expired one sitting in the keychain while its agents work perfectly. Read as a sign-out, it stood the line "Claude sign-in expired. Run claude once to refresh it." over a working agent, which is both untrue and a job handed to somebody that was never theirs. `claudeSignIn` in `src/runner/providers/usage.ts` is the rule: the refresh token is the sign-in, and only that one running out is worth asking anybody to sign in again.
- A stale token is served the last good numbers with their own "updated Xm ago", which is what every other failure here already does. The poll comes round every minute, so the bars come back on their own the next time an agent runs.
- Nothing is fetched on a token already known to have run out. The endpoint rate limits hard, which is the whole reason for the backoff and the last good snapshot, and a request that can only answer 401 costs the same as one that can answer.
- The refresh is never done here. The credentials belong to the CLI, the refresh token rotates when it is spent, and a second thing spending it is one of the two ends holding a token the other has already invalidated: the cost of getting that wrong is somebody signed out of Claude Code altogether, against a few minutes of a stale bar.

## Toasts

A word from the app about what just happened, standing under the header on the right for a few seconds. `toast()` in `src/renderer/src/state/toast.ts` raises one from anywhere, in the renderer or out of a handler, and `Toaster` is the one stack, mounted once in `App` and standing in the body.

- It is for a moment, never for a record. Anything somebody has to come back to belongs in the chat, in a thread, or on the row it is about, and a toast that says what a screen already says is a row of type spent on nothing.
- The tones are plain, done, fail and busy, and each one brings its own mark: nothing, the check, the warning, the spinner. A busy one waits rather than running out, and the handle it hands back turns it over: `toast.busy('Sharing the session').done('Link copied')` is one row saying two things rather than two rows.
- `key` is one row per thing. Asked for again under the same key it is rewritten where it stands, so the same word said twice is said once. Four rows is the whole stack, and the oldest goes when a fifth arrives.
- The row itself is the way out, and a button on it is the button's own. Nothing runs out while it is being read: the pointer resting anywhere on the stack holds every clock in it.
- The card wears `glass glass-strong`, the same material as anything else that floats over whatever the app happens to be showing. It says its own `--glass-shadow` rather than the popover's: a popover is one card and throws a drop deeper than the gap between rows, so four of them pool into one dark box standing behind the stack.
- A row opens and closes on its own `grid-template-rows`, which is the one way a box as tall as its text can be animated, and nothing anywhere clips: a box that clips its card clips the shadow with it, and the card reads as standing in a hole. The card keeps its height the whole way out and the fade is what carries it off.
- The server has a word of its own for this, `notice`, which goes to the one socket it is about and is never written down. It is what says why a command did not happen, so nobody else scrolls past an answer to a question they never asked.
- A message the host would not take is a message that never happened, so the words go back in the box they were typed in. The composer is emptied on the way out rather than once the host has taken it, which is right, since a send that waits on a round trip is a box that hangs on a slow link: what makes that safe is holding the words, the chip and the files until the send is either taken or handed back. `unsent` on the notice is what says it never happened and `where` is the composer, a thread's id or a board's id and absent for the chat, the way typing names one. They go back only into a composer nothing has been put in since, because what somebody is writing now beats what is being handed back. Every refusal in `handleChat`, `setFallback`, `startAside` and `startFork` goes through `refuse` rather than `notice` for it, and the ones about an emoji name stay plain notices, since nothing was typed into a composer to lose.
- Being named and being answered are the same news, so a reply to something you wrote is a word in the corner the way a mention is. `memberReplyAlert` is that one, beside `memberMentionAlert`, and both are the same two clauses in front of them: never your own message, and never the thread you already have open. A message that mentions you and replies to you is one word rather than two, which the order they stand in settles rather than a rule of their own, and the mention wins because being named outright is the louder of the two.
- Anything worth a notification in the corner of the screen is worth a row in the app. There is one decision, `finishedAlert`, `memberMentionAlert`, `memberReplyAlert` and `questionAlert` in `state/alerts.ts`, and two places it is said: the row in the app and the banner from the system, both every time. Whether the window is the one being looked at decides nothing, because a toast is a few seconds and a banner is what is still there when somebody comes back to their desk, so the window having focus at the moment a run landed is no reason for the only lasting word about it to be missing. What the screen already says is left out by the rule itself rather than by the window, which is the clause about the thread somebody has open.
- What is already on the screen is not said again. A run finishing or a mention landing in the thread somebody has open says it there, which is the whole of what keeps this from being a toast per event. Nothing else about it is hidden: an agent finishing on another thread reads in the corner while you work in this one.
- `alertToast` in `components/alertToast.tsx` is what turns one into a row: the agent's pet or the person's face, the work under the title, Open as the way in, and a key of the thread's own so a second word about it is written where the first one stands. Opening a thread from anywhere goes to the chat, which is `App` watching `openThreadId` rather than every caller remembering to switch the tab.
- **A banner is only clickable for as long as something in here is still holding it.** Electron hands the native notification a JS object and nothing else keeps it: once the last reference goes, the collector takes it, macOS still brings Crew forward on the click because that is the system activating the app, and the thread never opens. `standing` in `src/main/alerts.ts` is that reference and it is the whole of why the banner works, so it is held on `globalThis` rather than in a plain module array. A plain array was what it was, and the bundler was right to delete it: nothing in the app ever read it, only a suite did, so the reads and the writes were a closed loop with no observable end and tree shaking took the lot. What shipped was a `showAlert` that posted a banner and dropped it on the floor, and every one of these suites passed, because the test imports `alertsStanding` and that import is what kept the array alive in the test build and nowhere else. Anything else here whose only job is to hold a reference is under the same rule: hold it somewhere the bundler cannot prove is unread, and check the built `out/main/index.js` rather than a suite.

## Typing

Somebody at a keyboard, said over the composer they are writing in: "Jamel is typing" and the app's own ellipsis after it.

- It is never written down. It rides in memory on the host and in `typing.room`, the way a call does, so an afternoon of half finished sentences is not something the crew scrolls past later. Nothing about it is an event and nothing about it reaches the log.
- The host holds it keyed by the connection writing, because two windows on one folder are one member and two people at two keyboards, and one person is one typist on the way out however many windows they have open.
