- A window says it once every couple of seconds while the box has something in it, and says it has stopped the moment the box empties or the message goes. A ping that says what was already said is only the clock being wound, so nothing goes out for it and a keystroke is never a message.
- A window that dies mid-word never says it stopped, so the host lets go of it on its own after `TYPING_TTL`. The sweep is armed off the oldest entry and only while there is one, rather than run on a clock that ticks through every quiet afternoon.
- A window arriving mid-sentence is told once, straight after the welcome. Typing is too short lived to ride in the snapshot and too quiet to reach a new window on its own, since a ping only goes out when it changes something.
- Nothing typed in a ghost thread is recorded at all, rather than recorded and filtered on the way out. A hidden thread whose typing is not hidden is a hidden thread that leaks.
- `where` is which composer somebody is writing in, a thread's id or a board's id, and absent for the chat itself. `typistsIn` is what a line asks for, and it leaves out your own keyboard and everyone writing somewhere else.
- The line stands under the composer, in the room the padding already leaves, so it covers nothing and nothing moves when somebody starts or stops. Above the composer it was a glass pill laid over the last thing anybody said, which is the one place on the screen everybody is reading. Under it there is a strip of the app's own chrome and no message ever goes there.
- It is the quietest thing in the column: the smallest size in the ramp, `fg-muted`, no surface of its own, and it fades in rather than popping. A half written sentence is somebody about to say something and not something that has happened, so it is read out of the corner of the eye or not at all. The row above the composer is left to the way back to the foot of the thread, which is a thing to press and has earned it.
- The ellipsis is the app's own: the same three dots on the same grid carrying the same wave a thought carries, from `THINKING_DOTS`. Standing still they read as a sentence that was abandoned rather than as one being written. They stand in front of the name rather than after it, so a glance lands on a mark that is moving instead of on somebody's name.

## Huddles

Voice, video and screen share, started from the Huddle row in the More menu. The host relays the handshake and nothing else. The media itself goes machine to machine, everyone to everyone, so there is no server to run and no stream passing through the host.

- The call is never written down. It rides in the session snapshot and in `huddle.*` messages, never in the event log, so no peer id, no handshake and nothing about the media is ever committed or replayed. `src/server/session.ts` holds it in memory, keyed by connection, because two windows on one folder are one member but two people in the call.
- The record of a call is written down, and it is a different thing: `huddle.started`, `huddle.joined` and `huddle.ended` in the log, carrying who started it, who came, and how long it ran. Nothing live goes in them, and someone who comes back to the same call is named once. The chat block folds the three back into one card, and it knows whether the call it names is the one happening now by matching the room's own `id`. A start with no end reads as live forever, so a session coming back up closes any call it finds still open.
- Whoever started a call can take its block out of the chat, and nobody else can. It is a tombstone the way a deleted message is, `huddle.deleted`, and `huddleRecordId` is what takes the three events of that record out together, on the host as it lands and again on the way back in, so it stays gone after a restart. Only a call that is over can go: while it is going the block is the way in, and removing it would take the way in off the screen of everyone who has not joined yet.
- Every connection has the same three slots, negotiated once at the start and in the same order: microphone, camera, screen. Turning a camera on swaps a track into a slot that already exists. Renegotiating mid-call is what makes calls drop, so nothing after the first offer does it.
- Only one end of a pair ever offers, and peer ids decide which. The other end adds nothing of its own and takes the three slots the offer makes for it. Both ends offering is a collision, and the rollback that settles one does not give the local slots back: the browser makes a second set for the offer it accepted, so each side ends up holding three slots nothing is ever sent on. The call reaches connected, every packet arrives, and the room is silent. A slot added by hand is never used for a line that was offered, so the answering side must not make any.
- `yarn media-check` is the only thing that can see this. It runs the real connection code in two Electron windows and looks at what comes out the other end: three slots each, and sound and pictures arriving on the ones the app is listening to. A fake cannot stand in for it, and the fake passed the whole time this was broken.
- Glare is settled by comparing peer ids, the standard polite and impolite pair, and it is a guard rather than a path anything takes. Only the side that offers restarts a connection that failed.
- Signals are handled one at a time, in order, and an address that turns up before the description it belongs to is held rather than handed over. Two of them in flight together, or one offered early, is how a connection ends up with nowhere to send anything and sits there ringing forever. `tests/helpers/fake-rtc.ts` is strict about this so the mistake cannot come back.
- A handshake that goes quiet is said again, a few times, until it lands. Only that first exchange ever repeats. Once the two ends agree, nothing renegotiates.
- Joining never waits on a device. Someone with no microphone still gets into the call, muted, and is told what to fix.
- Which microphone and which camera is asked from the control that runs it, by the notch on its corner or by right-clicking it, and `DeviceMenu` is that one menu for both. The choice is kept in `localStorage` and used by the next call, so it is a setting rather than something to pick again every time.
- A device is asked for as an `ideal`, never an `exact` one. A microphone that was chosen once and has since been unplugged would fail the whole request as an exact, which puts someone in a call with nothing at all rather than with whatever is there. What the machine actually hands over is what the menu ticks, read back off the track, because the two are not always the same device.
- Picking a device while it is live swaps the track into the slot it already holds, and the one it replaces is stopped only once the new one is in. Nothing renegotiates, and there is no gap in what the others hear. Picking one while it is off only remembers it.
- Tiles are widescreen everywhere, and a tile owns its own shape. Wrapping one in something that sets the shape from outside is how they ended up as slivers.
- A shared screen is zoomed the same way a picture is. Both are drawn inside `ZoomView`, which owns the pinch, the two finger scroll, the drag, the double click and the percentage in the corner, so the gesture means one thing wherever it is made. It is handed the size the content is really drawn at rather than the size of the box around it, since a screen is letterboxed inside its frame and panning into the black is not panning at all. For a screen that size is `videoWidth` and `videoHeight`, which `VideoStream` reports as it lands and again whenever the far end resizes what it is sharing.
- Marks like the ring on whoever is talking are painted inside the box they mark, with `InsetRing`. Anything drawn around the outside is cropped the moment the box lands in a scroller or a card that clips, which is where the dock, the rail beside a shared screen, and the screen picker all put it.

## Voice

Talking to an agent out loud, back and forth. It is a tile in the toolbox and `/voice` in the chat, and it opens a screen of its own with the orb on it. Everything that listens and everything that speaks runs on this machine and costs nothing: whisper for the ear, kokoro for the mouth, both fetched once from their own model repositories and kept by the browser after that. There is no service behind it and nothing said in a conversation leaves the machine except the words themselves, which go to the agent the way a typed message does.

- A conversation is an ordinary thread. What was said goes in the chat, the agent does real work on real files, and the crew reads it afterwards, so voice is a way into a thread rather than a room beside one. The thread wears `voice`, and the host adds `VOICE_INSTRUCTIONS` to every prompt in it exactly the way plan mode adds its own. Nothing about the brief is ever a message, so the chat holds what people said and not what the app told the agent.
- That brief is the whole of what makes a spoken answer sound spoken. Without it a model writes three paragraphs of markdown, and a voice reads out every asterisk in them. It asks for a sentence or two in plain words, and it is what teaches the agent to draw a card.
- Both models run in workers of their own. A turn of whisper is a second of solid arithmetic, and a second of that on the thread drawing the orb is a second of the orb standing still. Nothing heavy is imported anywhere but those two files, which is what keeps the runtime and its wasm out of the app's own bundle.
- **Both of them ask for the card and both fall back twice, and `hasGpu` in `models.ts` is the one place that asks.** It was written for the listener and the mouth was loading `q8` on `wasm` beside it, which is the whole of why an answer took three seconds to start: measured by `yarn mouth-check` on this machine, the first sentence of a turn was ready 2.95s after the turn opened, and on the card it is 0.18s. The rest of the turn moves with it, 13.37s to the last of three sentences against 0.81s. A second copy of the question would have drifted the first time either model moved, so the mouth reads the listener's own rule rather than a rule of its own.
- **What the card is handed is `fp32`, and the dtype is not a detail of that decision, it is the decision.** `speakBuild` in `models.ts` is the pair, `fp32` on the card and `q8` on the cpu, and the three that were measured beside it say why it is not a knob. `fp16` is fast and comes back with a whole sentence of `NaN` in the middle of a turn, which plays as silence between two sentences that work and is the one failure a peak alone does not catch. `q8` on the card is slower than the same `q8` on wasm, 2.62s to the first sentence against 2.50s, so the card without the dtype buys nothing at all. `q4f16` is as quick as `fp32` and is a quantized voice, and there is nothing in a peak or an rms that says how it sounds. `fp32` is what kokoro's own README asks for on webgpu and it is what came back closest to the wasm build it replaced, the same sample count on every voice.
- **What it costs is the load, and the load is the one part of this nobody waits on.** `fp32` is 325.5MB over four files against `q8`'s 92.4MB, and standing it up takes 8.99s against 2.79s. Both are paid at `mouth.load()`, which `start` calls as the voice screen opens, so they run under the microphone opening, somebody saying their first sentence, whisper reading it and the agent thinking, and the model has been ready for a while by the time there is a word to say. The 0.18s is paid on every sentence of every turn. The first turn after the load is 0.62s rather than 0.18s, which is the card compiling its shaders once.
- The second fall back is a real turn rather than a retry of a call, so what it replays is the words. A card that took the model and then could not run it throws in the middle of `tts.stream`, and the sentences already handed to the splitter have gone with it, so the turn keeps what was pushed into it and says it again on the cpu, skipping whatever was already spoken. The common failure is the first sentence, where nothing has been spoken and nothing is said twice. `tests/voice-speak-worker.test.ts` holds it, since a card that takes a model and then fails on it is not a thing this machine can be asked to do on purpose.
- The listener is told nothing about the language. Whisper's English-only model carries it in the model and refuses to be told again: a `language` or a `task` on one of those throws before it reads a sample, so every utterance failed on the way in, nothing was ever sent, and the orb sat working forever while the room said the words again louder. `askedOf` in `models.ts` is the whole of that rule. `yarn voice-check` is the only thing that can see it, because it hands the real model real speech and reads what comes back, and every unit suite passed the entire time this was broken.
- Nothing about a turn fails quietly. A listener that fell over and a room that said nothing are the same empty string on the way back, so `hear` hands back null for the first and the screen says so, or a fault in the one part nobody can watch reads as an agent that is ignoring you.
- The microphone stays open the whole time, the agent's turn included, and `echoCancellation` is what makes that possible. While the agent is speaking the gate asks for more before it believes it has been interrupted, or the agent talks itself to a stop. It is `ear.guarded` that arms that, off the sentence leaving the speakers rather than off the turn opening, because a turn opens while the agent is still working and the microphone is the room's until a word comes out.
- Cutting in stops the voice and nothing else. Whatever the agent is doing to the project it goes on doing, because an interruption is somebody talking over an answer and not somebody undoing work. What the model had already drawn and not yet played is dropped rather than let out afterwards.
- **Only a voice that can be heard is one worth cutting in on, and the mouth answers that as its own question.** `speaking` is whether the turn is over and `talking` is whether sound is really coming out, and they are two fields because a turn is opened before the model has drawn a word: `open` is called the moment the question goes out, so `drained` is false for the whole of the wait for an answer and `speaking` reads true across every second of it. Read off `speaking`, the gate opening anywhere in that wait was somebody talking over the agent, so the question, the run it had found and the reply being built were all thrown away, the mouth was sealed, and the answer landed in the thread with nothing left anywhere to say it out loud. A cough is the case that shows it, because the gate opens on one and `onEnd` hands back nothing, so the turn was destroyed by a noise that was never a word. The window is as long as the agent takes to think, which is the whole of why this reads as a voice that simply does not work rather than as a rare race. `talking` is `waiting > 0`, which is sound scheduled or playing and nothing else, and it is what the ear and the orb both read. `tests/voice-cutting-in.test.ts` holds the pair: an answer still spoken after a cough while the agent was thinking, and the voice still going quiet the moment somebody talks over it.
- A cough is not the end of the agent's turn either, so the phase goes back to whatever was really going on rather than to an empty room. `onEnd` with nothing in it read as listening while a run was still out, which is the orb saying the conversation is over on top of one that is still working.
- `VoiceGate` is where one thing somebody said starts and ends. A level alone cannot answer that, so it tracks the floor the room is sitting at and opens on whatever stands well above it for long enough. The floor only moves while nobody is talking: tracked through an utterance it climbs into the voice and closes the gate mid-sentence. A click is one frame and never opens it, a cough opens it and is dropped for being too short, and a microphone somebody left open lets go on its own.
- **A gap in the middle of a thought is not the end of a turn, and how quiet the room is cannot tell the two apart.** The gate waited out 620 milliseconds of silence and sent whatever it was holding, so somebody who paused to think was answered mid sentence and somebody who had plainly finished sat through a delay they could feel. Both are the same number doing two jobs. The conversation's own ear closes on `TURN_CLOSE_MS`, 420, and what was said is the other half of the answer: `stillGoing` in `turn.ts` reads the end of what whisper wrote, and a question mark is a finished turn, a comma, a dash or a trail of dots is not, and neither is a last word that never ends a thought, and, because, the, is, basically among them. Finished goes at once, which takes 200 milliseconds off every turn in the conversation. Unfinished is held by `HeldTurn` for `HOLD_MS`, and whatever is said next is joined onto it and asked again, so "So basically" and "the tests are slow" reach the agent as one sentence rather than as two runs of an agent answering half a thought.
- How long a gap has to be belongs to whatever is listening rather than to the gate, which is why `VoiceGate` is handed it and keeps `CLOSE_MS` as what it does when nobody says. Scribe holds the same gate to find the pauses in a dictation, and `tidy` places a mark from the silence after a word, so a shorter close there is not a quicker conversation, it is different punctuation in somebody's document. A number one feature wants is never taken out of the one they share.
- The two ways of being wrong do not cost the same, which is the whole of how that list of words is chosen. A hold nobody needed costs the length of the hold and then sends exactly what it was holding, and a turn sent too early is the agent talking over somebody and answering a question that had not been asked yet. So a word that usually ends a sentence and sometimes does not, `is` among them, is in the list, and the cost of that is 700 milliseconds on the sentences it gets wrong.
- Nothing about the hold slows an interruption down. The gate opening is what stops the voice, and it is read on the frame it happens rather than after whisper, so cutting in costs nothing it ever did. A hold standing when somebody starts talking again lets go of its timer and keeps its words, and every way out of the conversation, the agent being changed, the microphone being muted, a card being answered and the screen being closed, drops what was being held rather than sending it into a conversation that has moved.
- **The opener is the whole of what makes an answer arrive quickly, and it is a line of the brief rather than a word the app says.** `VoiceReply` already hands over each sentence as it lands, so a reply that opens on "Yeah." is out of the speakers a breath after the model's first tokens, where the same reply opening on a paragraph says nothing at all until the paragraph is written. Crew never speaks a filler of its own while it waits. One would be the app talking rather than the agent, and it would be guessing at an answer that has not been written yet, which is the same rule the app holds about never faking progress anywhere else.
- **The opener is a promise about the first token, so the brief also says when to write it: before reading a file, running a command or reaching for anything.** A turn that opens on three tool calls has written no first sentence, so there is nothing to speak until they come back, and the opener is worth nothing on the one turn it was there for. Beside it the brief asks for the answer out of what the agent already knows wherever it can. Neither is a rule about how much work a spoken turn may do, since it does the work it is asked to as usual.
- **A spoken thread is never told how to send work out, which is `!thread.voice` on `canSend` in `promptMessage`.** It is the same rule the aside already holds a line above it, for the same reason from the other end: an aside answers itself, and a conversation somebody is standing in front of waiting on has nothing to gain from a helper that comes back a minute later. It is also the fattest thing a voice turn was carrying, 2,602 characters of curl in front of a question that wants two words back, so it is a shorter read as well as a shorter run. What stays is memory, which every agent reads before it starts, and the board, which a voice thread only has if somebody asked for one with `/tickets` as well.
- Every sample is kept, through an `AudioWorklet`. An analyser hands back the last window it holds and nothing about the gaps between reads, which is fine for a ring around whoever is talking and useless for keeping the words. The few hundred milliseconds before the gate opened are kept too, or every utterance is missing the front of its first word and whisper guesses at it.
- The answer is spoken while it is still being written, and two things stop that being a plain hand-off of whatever just landed. A sentence is the smallest thing worth saying, so a part one is held back. And nothing past an unclosed fence is ever handed over, so a card is either whole or not there: `VoiceReply` is both of those, and the line it stops on can never fall inside a card, because a line ending inside one is still a line ending and the front of a card read out loud is a line of JSON.
- A card is the thing worth looking at rather than hearing. `VoiceCard` in `src/shared/voice.ts` is the whole of what one may be, five kinds and no more, and one with nothing on it comes back as null rather than as an empty box beside the orb. They arrive as the agent writes them rather than at the end, and the options on a choice are answered by tapping one, which goes in as a turn of its own so it reads back the way saying it would have.
- The orb is drawn the way the games are: `orb.ts` holds the numbers, `drawOrb.ts` holds the picture, and neither knows about the other. It is read off a real analyser hung on whichever of the two is making sound, never off a shape standing in for one.
- A circle that only changes size is a circle changing size. What makes one of these read as a voice is that the edge itself moves, and differently at different parts of it, so the outline carries a harmonic per band of what is being heard, low bands on the slow harmonics and high on the fast. Never the first harmonic: one lobe around a circle is the circle moved sideways, and the orb would wander about the screen while it talked.
- The light it throws is its own outline, stood a little further out and blurred. Light carries the shape of whatever throws it, so a round glow around a shape that is not round reads as a smudge the orb is sitting on. It is held inside the box it is drawn in, blur and all, and how far it stands out is worked back from `orbReach` every frame: paint that runs past the canvas is cut off flat at all four sides, which is a grey square standing behind a sphere, and a loud orb swells into that room without warning.
- The mesh is the agent's voice and nothing else. Your own turn leaves the orb in the one color the mark wears at rest, so who is talking is read off it without a word anywhere saying so, and the colors are the mark's own `MESH_COLORS` rather than a palette this invents.
- The renderer's CSP has to carry `script-src 'self' 'wasm-unsafe-eval' blob:` and `worker-src 'self' blob:`, or the worklet never loads and the runtime never compiles. Both fail quietly and both look like a microphone that does nothing.
- `tests/voice-listening.test.ts` holds the gate to all of the above, `tests/voice-reply.test.ts` feeds a reply in one character at a time and holds it to saying every word once and never a line of a card, and `tests/voice-mouth.test.ts` runs the speaker against a clock it winds by hand.
- **`yarn mouth-check` is the only thing that can see the speaking half, and it is the pair to `yarn voice-check` rather than a second version of it.** A suite winds a clock by hand and a fake hands back whatever it was written to hand back, so a model that returns a buffer of the right length full of zeroes passes every one of them, and what that reads as is an agent that answers and cannot be heard. It loads the real kokoro at the real dtype on the real device, hands it real sentences the way `VoiceReply` hands them over, and reads what comes back: how long the model took cold and again with the files already here, how long until the first sentence of audio is ready, the peak and the RMS of every chunk, and the rate the samples really carry. Every voice in `SPEAK_VOICES` is said as well, since one voice that yields silence looks exactly like the whole thing being broken.
- **It asks `hasGpu` and `speakBuild` rather than naming a device of its own, or it is a check of a thing the app does not do.** It named `q8` on `wasm` in its own page while the worker read the same two words a file away, which is a number that goes on being reported long after the app has stopped earning it. It says the turn twice for the same reason the card compiles its shaders once, so the first sentence of the first turn and the first sentence of every turn after it are two lines rather than one.
- It runs in a real Electron window rather than in node, and that is the runtime rather than a preference. Neither device it can pick is node's: onnxruntime there offers `cpu` and refuses `wasm` outright, and there is no adapter to ask for a card at all, so a check written in node cannot load the model the app loads either way. The page is served over http on loopback rather than opened as a file, since a module worker and a wasm compile both meet rules on `file://` that no window in the app ever stands under.
- The rate is the one number a suite could never have caught, because it is read on one side and assumed on the other. The worker posts the samples alone and `VoiceMouth.play` builds its `AudioBuffer` at `SPEAK_RATE`, so nothing in the app ever compares the two: a model that came back at another rate would play at the wrong speed with every part of the code still correct on its own terms. The check reads `sampling_rate` off the audio the model really returns and holds it against the constant.
- It says how it went on a line of its own and always exits nought, the rule `yarn scribe-check` already holds: the runtime leaves threads that abort while node is shutting down, and an abort is a signal, which reads as a check that failed after saying every word of it passed. A machine that cannot load the model says so on a line and checks nothing, the way `yarn local-check` does.

## Scribe

Hold a key, talk, and what you said is written into whatever you were typing in, anywhere on the machine. It is the same whisper Voice already runs, so there is no second model and no service behind it. Where Voice is a way into a thread, this never touches the crew at all: nothing is written down, nothing goes over the wire, and the settings live in `localStorage` the way a terminal is this machine's shell and not the crew's.

- The key is the whole feature and it is the one kind of key electron cannot register, because `globalShortcut` needs a real key in the combination and a bare modifier is not one. `scribe-keys.ts` listens under the window through libuiohook, bound over N-API the way node-pty is, so the binary loads in electron without ever being rebuilt for it. It is loaded through `createRequire` when it is first wanted rather than at the top of the file: a native module that will not load throws on the import, and an app that cannot start because dictation is unavailable is worse than dictation being unavailable.
- **Fn cannot be had, on any Mac, by any app.** macOS keeps it below the tap every app reads and it is not in the public hot key API, and libuiohook has no keycode for it either: `input_hook.c` reads four masks on `flagsChanged` and Fn is not among them. Right Option is the default instead, because it is on the built-in keyboard and Right Control is not. Never put Fn back in the list without a native addon of our own to back it.
- Four rules make a bare modifier bearable, and they are numbers in `scribeLatch.ts` rather than anything that owns a keyboard, the way the games hold their rules. It does not arm until it has been held alone, so reaching for a shortcut never raises the pill. Any other key pressed while it is down drops the take and lets the shortcut through, which is the whole of what makes plain Control safe to dictate on. A tap latches, so a hold is a hold and a tap is hands free, one rule rather than a mode to pick. And a press while it is latched is what ends it. A tap too quick to have armed still latches, or the key only works for people who are slow about it.
- There is a second way in that never goes through the hook, `Control+Command+Space` and `Control+Alt+Space`, registered permanently beside it. The Windows hook is known to go quiet after `getUserMedia` runs in a focused window, which is exactly what this does, so there is always a key that works and the settings page says outright when the good one is not being heard.
- The pill is a window of its own at `#scribe`, warmed at boot the way the tray panel is, and for a bigger reason: it holds whisper, and a model fetched at the moment somebody starts talking is a first dictation that arrives a minute late. It never takes focus. `showInactive`, `type: 'panel'` on macOS and `focusable: false` everywhere else, or the caret leaves the app the words were going to. `#root` wears `bare` there, since the glass is the whole of it and a surface behind that glass would be Crew's own color rather than the color of what is really back there.
- The pill is moved by taking hold of it anywhere on it, and it opens where it was left. Never moved, it stands at the bottom middle of the screen the pointer is on, which is the screen the app being typed into is almost certainly on. `scribe-spot.ts` is that placing as numbers and nothing else, the way `tray-position.ts` is, so a screen unplugged since and a drag that ran off the edge of one are both answered without a window anywhere near it. Two rules hold it still. It is placed once, when it is shown, and never again: placing reads the pointer, and the pointer has moved on by the time the renderer has measured itself, which is what made the pill land somewhere different every time. And growing is not moving, so a pill that grew a line keeps its bottom edge, which is what leaves the two buttons under the pointer already on the way to them, and writes no spot down, or a pill that grew once on one screen would be pinned to it by nobody's decision.
- A drag is a screen distance handed to main rather than anything the page does to itself, and it is measured against the screen and never against the window. The window travels under the pointer the whole time, so read the other way round every frame would move the pill by however far it had already moved, and it would run off the edge of the screen on its own. Main holds where it stood when it was taken hold of and works every move from there, so a drag that runs into an edge comes back off it as the pointer does. Where it lands is this machine's own, in `userData` beside the rest of what the app remembers for itself, and nothing about it is ever sent.
- Nothing can blur what is behind the pill, and that decides what it is made of. A backdrop filter samples the page it is drawn in, and behind a transparent window there is no page, so a heavier blur buys nothing at all and `glass-strong` is only a darker slab. `glass-pill` is lit instead, the way the app icon is: the tint falls from a lighter top to a darker bottom and a rim of light catches the top edge, which is the whole of what makes it read as glass with nothing coming through it but color.
- How far off opaque it may sit is decided by the words underneath. Nothing shows through at 0.97 and the pill is a slab laid on the screen; at 0.9 the line under it is a shape rather than a sentence, which is what frosted glass over text looks like; by 0.84 the words are legible through the pill and it reads as a smudge over the page. Dictation happens over text, so the ceiling is text and never a photograph, where more would only be prettier.
- The window is the pill plus `PILL_ROOM` on every side. A shadow is paint thrown past the box it comes from and a window ends where its bounds end, so a drop from a pill that fills its own window is cut off flat at all four sides, which reads as a pale oblong standing behind the pill rather than as a shadow under it. The drop is the pill's own and tighter than the popover's, written to land inside that room. Every rule in `scribe-spot.ts` is about where the pill may stand rather than where the window may, so the screen a spot is held inside is the real one grown by the same room, or the pill would rest a whole shadow's width in from every edge.
- That room is empty and swallows the clicks that land in it, and it is meant to. Electron does not pass a click through a transparent window on either platform, and the only way round it, turning the window's mouse policy over as the pointer arrives and leaves, forwards moves and never buttons: a drag in flight would lose its pointer up to the app underneath, which is the app the dictation is going into and the one thing this window exists to protect. So the margin wears `pointer-events-none`, nothing in the page acts on it, and a click there is lost for the few seconds a dictation is on screen.
- While somebody is talking the pill is the bars and nothing else. The one thing on it to press is the X, in a slot at its left that is shut at rest and opens under the pointer, so the pill is as wide as what it has to say and no wider: it stands over somebody else's work, and a button waiting there the whole time for the few seconds anybody reaches for it is their window spent on nothing. It opens from its own middle, which is what leaves it where it was put whether it is open or shut, and the gap in front of the bars opens with the slot rather than standing there empty. There is no way to finish on it: the key opened the dictation and the key is what ends it, and with the words already landing there is nothing left to hand over. A failure is the one state that opens itself, since it holds a sentence and a way out, and it is the widest the pill ever gets, which is what the window is sized from. The marks wear `STROKE_BOLD` rather than the weight a mark carries inside the app, because they are read at arm's length over somebody else's window.
- The bars are the whole of what the pill says, so they say the reading too. Once the key is up there is no voice left to draw, and a row sitting at its floor is a line of dots that reads as a microphone that has stopped rather than as work still going on, so a crest travels through the row instead, low to high: the same thing the thinking mark says with its three dots. `waveBands` in `media/bands.ts` is that reading and `tests/scribe-bars.test.ts` holds it to always having a crest in it. The bars grow off their middle here and stand on a floor in the music, and that is a word `Levels` is handed rather than a class, since `items-center` and `items-end` weigh the same and which one wins is decided by the order Tailwind happened to write them in.
- The mark is the pill's own bars. Voice wears the microphone and the two stand beside each other in the toolbox, so this one is what a voice does rather than what it is spoken into. Four of them and not five, since five across the same width close into a block at 16, and every one is centred on the same line: a set of bars standing on a floor is read as quantities beside each other, and one growing both ways off a middle is read as loudness.
- A long dictation is read while it is still being said. `take.ts` watches for the pauses and hands a finished stretch to whisper as somebody carries on talking, so by the time the key is let go only the tail is left. Every piece carries where it began, and the chunks come back shifted onto the take's own clock, because the gaps between them are half of what the marks are placed from.
- It is written as it is said, and that is the default. A stretch is a pause's worth of speech, which is a whole thought, so `ScribeFlow` in `scribeLive.ts` tidies each one on its own and writes it the moment it has been read, and every stretch after the first carries the space that keeps it off the end of the one before it. Never tidy the whole dictation again on each stretch and paste the difference: the mark at the end of a sentence is placed from the silence after it and a correction reaches back a sentence, so what is already on somebody's screen would change under words nothing here can take back. What has gone out has gone out, and a correction reaches back over what is still being held and no further.
- The store walks those stretches one at a time down a single chain, so two of them can never land the wrong way round however they were read, and a stretch that has been written is off both the pieces and the sound. Only what never landed is left for Try again, so a failure halfway through writes the rest rather than the whole of it. A walk still waiting on a read nobody is coming back for is left where it is rather than cancelled: it comes back to a list that is not the one it set out down and stands itself down.
- The paste is one thing at a time and the clipboard is borrowed once for the run. Two pastes closer together than `SETTLE_MS` and the second overwrites the clipboard the first is still on its way to read, which lands one stretch twice and loses the other, and giving the clipboard back between two of them puts somebody's own text in front of a paste already in flight.
- Nothing is pasted while the key is down. The modifier being held is composed into the keystroke, so the app on the other side is handed a shortcut nobody pressed, which is why main holds a dictation on Hold back to the end and writes it the way it always did. And the app must never hear its own paste: that keystroke goes out through the same hook the key is read with, so a dictation held on Command, or on Control where Control is what pastes, would be ended by the words it was writing on the way past. `ownKey` in `scribe.ts` is the whole of that rule.
- Writing as you talk is only ever a paste. A dictation on its way to the clipboard has one place to land and lands there once, so the row is left out rather than greyed when Copy is picked.
- Nothing spoken is ever a mark. `tidy` in `scribeTidy.ts` places them from whisper's own punctuation and from the gaps, so "the period after the war" is a sentence about a war. It also drops the fillers, collapses a word said twice, and cuts a correction back: one standing at the head of a sentence takes the whole sentence in front of it, which is how people really dictate. One sentence back and never two.
- A bare "hold on" is not a correction. "Hold on, let me check" is somebody asking for a moment, and taking the line before it away is the one mistake here nobody can see happening. "Actually hold on" is a retraction and is in the list.
- The reading runs on `device: 'webgpu'` where there is a card and falls back to `wasm` where there is not, which Voice gets for free. It falls back twice: once at load, and again if the first inference throws, because a card that took the model and then could not run it is the one failure no check before that can see.
- A failure keeps the sound. Nothing said twice, so trying again costs one press, and what goes to the worker is a copy rather than the buffer itself, since handing a buffer over empties the one here.
- A dictation started with the caret nowhere is held rather than lost. Scribe writes into whatever has the caret, so one begun with nothing focused was read, tidied and pasted into the desk, and the words were gone: the one failure here that costs somebody the whole thing they said. They stand on a card instead, on the pill's own window, with the line about clicking into a box, the words, Copy and a way out. `landingFrom` in `src/shared/scribeLanding.ts` is the rule and `askCaret` in `src/main/scribe-caret.ts` is the asking, kept apart so the rule can be read without a keyboard anywhere near it.
- The two ways of being wrong do not cost the same, and that settles every line of the rule. Held words that could have been pasted are on screen, one press from where they were going. Pasted words that had nowhere to go are gone. So a doubt is held, and only an outright refusal holds anything back: `unknown`, which is every machine that is not a Mac and every machine that will not answer, pastes exactly as it always did.
- **A missing focused element is two answers, and the focused window is what tells them apart.** That one extra read is the difference between the feature working and it saying "click into a text box" over an open composer. With a focused window it is a Chromium application that has not built its accessibility tree: it builds one only once something asks the way it is waiting to be asked, nothing here does, and so Crew, Discord, VS Code and Chrome all hand back `missing value` the whole time a caret is sitting in a box. That is a silence, and a silence is `unknown`, so it pastes the way it always did. With no focused window nothing in that application is open, which is Mail, Messages, Terminal and ChatGPT with every window shut, and there really is nowhere for the words to go, so that is the `none` the card exists for. Read without the window, every field anybody really dictates into was held back while nothing at all was caught.
- Nothing ever turns another application's accessibility tree on. `AXManualAccessibility` is somebody else's process and the tree costs them for as long as it is on, almost nothing honours the write anyway, and Chrome wants `AXEnhancedUserInterface` and up to several seconds to answer after it, which is longer than anybody waits to start talking. The one app whose answer is worth having is answered without asking macOS at all.
- Crew answers for itself, because it is a page and a page already knows what has the caret in it. A focused window is the whole of what says the app is frontmost, since Electron answers null for every window while somebody is in another application, so a null is the caret being somewhere only macOS can say. That makes our own windows the one place a `none` is really earned: the caret is in Crew, it is not in anything anybody can type in, and the words are held. The side panel is the exception, because it holds a real browser: the caret can be in a field on somebody else's site while the page around it sees only the box that site is drawn in, so anything holding a page of its own is the doubt it really is rather than a `none`. `landingInPage` is that rule and `FOCUSED_IN_PAGE` is what reads the page, kept beside it so `tests/scribe-caret-probe.test.ts` can put a real document through the pair. Editable is asked twice, off the property and off the attribute, because the property is what carries the answer down into a doc's own box and the attribute is what a box that says so itself carries.
- A role cannot answer this and the caret's own attribute cannot either. A box somebody wrote themselves comes back as a group in one app and as a text area in the next, so a list of roles to trust is wrong on the next app anybody opens, and inside a page `AXSelectedTextRange` is handed to buttons and to the page itself, so the one thing that reads as a caret is on things nobody can type in. Inside a page, which is what `AXDOMClassList` and `AXDOMIdentifier` say, `AXEditableAncestor` is the whole answer. Outside one, a role that names a field is taken at its word and the caret attribute is the fallback. `AXRole` is read as an attribute and never through System Events' own `role`, because the two disagree: a page with nothing focused comes back as a group from the property and as the web area it really is from the attribute, and that web area is the case the whole feature exists for.
- It is asked twice and neither ask is in anybody's way. Once as the key goes down, which is free because the whole wait sits behind somebody talking, and once more only when that first answer was a refusal, since somebody who has clicked into a box since is somebody who wants their words in it. The second ask only ever costs the case that was about to lose them.
- Every name in the AppleScript is short and plain because it has to be. AppleScript has a reference form for half the words a person would reach for, so `front`, `focused`, `role` and `named` are property forms rather than variables, and each one fails to compile in a way that reads exactly like the Accessibility permission being refused.
- The words are held in main and nowhere else, because main is the one place both ends can be reached: the card is drawn in the pill's window and Copy is the machine's own clipboard, which a window that never takes focus cannot reach on its own. Stretches join there as they arrive, so the card says the whole of what was said rather than a row for each piece of it.
- The card outranks the setting that puts the pill away. Whoever is looking at it has not copied the words yet, and a window that rested on the way past would take the only copy of what they said with it. Starting to talk again is what lets go of it, since words held behind a dictation already running are words nobody is coming back for.
- It waits for the dictation to be over. Stretches arrive while somebody is still talking and the bars are the whole of what says Scribe is still listening, so a card opening over them mid sentence takes that away. A failure comes before it for the same kind of reason: a read that fell over halfway has stretches held and a Try again for the rest, and the card drawn over that would be the words so far standing where the way to the rest of them was. Closing the failure is what shows the card, since the words are still held.
- `yarn scribe-caret-check` is the only thing that can see the rule. It asks real applications what they really answer and prints the branch each one came out of, without activating anything, because the one thing a suite cannot say is whether a real Chromium button really hands back a caret attribute. It does, which is how that case was found.
- `yarn scribe-check` is the only thing that can see any of this. It hands the real model real speech, reads the timestamps it really returns, and puts them through the real tidying, and it says how it went on a line of its own rather than in its exit code: the runtime whisper runs on leaves threads that abort while node is shutting down, and an abort is a signal, which is a check that says it failed after saying every word of it passed.
- `tests/scribe-tidy.test.ts` is the suite that says the feature works, and it runs without a microphone anywhere near it. `tests/scribe-keys.test.ts` drives the key by hand and `tests/scribe-take.test.ts` drives the microphone against a worklet that never really runs. `tests/scribe-landing.test.ts` holds the rule to real roles and real attribute lists, `tests/scribe-held.test.ts` holds what is done with the answer, and `tests/scribe-held-card.test.ts` renders the card and presses both of its buttons.

## Writing a dictation up

A dictation read a second time, by a model on this machine, so what lands is what somebody would have typed rather than what they said out loud. It is the Writing it up section of the Scribe page, off until somebody turns it on, and `src/shared/scribeEdit.ts` is the whole of it.

- **The tidying is rules and most of this is out of a rule's reach.** `scribeTidy.ts` places a mark from the silence after a word, which is the best a rule can do and is wrong in the one way people notice: a pause is not a full stop, so "I still need to review the data, update the presentation, and send everything to the team" came out as four sentences, three of them fragments. Everything else here is the same shape of thing. Four thirty is 4:30, a thousand two hundred forty seven dollars and eighty three cents is $1,247.83, quarter three is Q3, a spoken list wants its commas, an acronym wants its capitals, and a mishearing is only ever settled by the sentence around it. None of those is a gap in the rules that could be closed by more rules.
- **It runs where whisper runs or it does not run at all.** Scribe never touches the crew: nothing it hears is written down and nothing goes over the wire, and a second pass through somebody else's service would be the one thing in the feature that broke that. So what it reaches is an OpenAI compatible server on this computer, which is what Ollama, LM Studio, `llama-server` and vLLM all are, and the address and the model are the machine's own the way the key and the microphone already are.
- **No key anywhere in it.** A key belongs to the machine and is kept beside the app at `0600`, where Scribe's own settings live in the window's storage, so a key field here would be a token in `localStorage`. A server that wants one is a server Scribe leaves alone, and that is the honest end of it rather than a gap.
- **`EDIT_BRIEF` is the whole of what makes a dictation read as writing**, and it is added the way `POST_INSTRUCTIONS` and `VOICE_INSTRUCTIONS` are added. Without it a model rewrites what it was handed: it swaps the for a and their for the, it reaches for a more elegant phrasing of a sentence that was already right, and it answers the words instead of editing them. So the brief is mostly what never to do, and the one thing it asks for at the end is the text alone.
- **The guard is what makes this safe to switch on, and it is the load bearing half of the feature.** A dictation is pasted straight into somebody else's application, so `edited` reads every answer before any of it can land and the rules' own writing stands wherever there is a doubt. The two ways of being wrong do not cost the same, which settles it: a dictation that came out as the rules would have written it is a sentence somebody punctuates for themselves, and one that came out with a line of chat in front of it, or as a model's own answer, is somebody's document with words in it that nobody said.
- Three numbers are the whole of the reading. An answer may not grow much past what went in, which is what catches a preamble, a note on the end and an explanation. It may not invent words, which catches the same things from the other side. And most of what was said has to come back, which is what catches a refusal, an answer to the question, and a stretch quietly dropped.
- **A number word is allowed to go missing and that is why `LOOSE` exists.** "Nine thirty A.M." is four words and 9:30 a.m. keeps almost none of them, so read against the whole of what was said, every stretch with a time or an amount in it would be refused for losing what it was turned on to change. What has to come back is meaning, and a figure, a currency word and a spoken point are not.
- **A correction is the one place most of what was said is allowed to go missing**, since throwing a clause away is the whole of what the speaker asked for: "schedule it for Tuesday at three, actually make that Wednesday at four thirty" is one short sentence by the time it is written. The loss has to be licensed by evidence in what was said rather than by a lower floor everywhere, so a retraction word in the stretch is what drops it, and refusing to drop the whole of it is what the floor still holds. `yarn scribe-edit-check` is what found this: the rules were standing on the one case the brief has a whole paragraph about.
- **A model that thinks out loud has its thinking taken off the front.** A local reasoning model writes its chain into the content rather than into a field of its own, and read as the answer it is a page of deliberation where a sentence should be, so the guard refuses it and every dictation on that model quietly comes out as the rules wrote it. Everything up to the last closing tag comes off, the opener included or not, since a model that was cut off mid thought still closed nothing.
- **A preamble is cut where it can be and refused where it cannot.** A line with none of the dictation's own words in it is a line the model wrote about the work rather than a line of the work, so it comes off and the words under it are kept. Only a telling word counts as evidence for this: a line sharing nothing but "the" with what was said came from a model, which is why there is a second small list of the words that say nothing about where a line came from.
- **Nothing is ever lost to the model.** A server that is not running, one that answers a status, one that answers something the guard will not have, and one that has not answered by `EDIT_MS` all end in the same place, which is the writing the rules already produced going out. A stretch longer than `EDIT_LIMIT` is never sent at all, since past that the wait is worse than the writing is good.
- It is one stretch at a time, which is what keeps the live rule true. What has gone out has gone out, and a correction reaches back over what is still being held and no further, so a stretch is tidied, read again and written on its own and nothing already on somebody's screen is ever rewritten. The walk down the pieces was already serialized, so the order holds for free.
- The take can be cancelled inside the moment the model is thinking, so the phase is read again on the way back and nothing goes out for a dictation nobody is waiting on.
- `ScribeFlow.mark` is why there is a second way into that class. The space in front of every stretch but the first is one rule, and anything standing between the rules and the paste hands its answer back through it rather than working the space out again.
- The address is held by the field while it is being typed and written down once it is settled, because every write is cleaned on the way in and a cleaning that runs on each keystroke rewrites the address under the caret. That is the same shape as the agent maker, which keeps what was typed and asks `serverUrl` about it on the way out.
- The models are asked for again every time the page is opened rather than once, since what a machine will serve changes the moment somebody runs a pull, and a server that answers nothing says so on a row of its own rather than leaving a picker with nothing in it.
- **The addresses a machine might be serving on live in `src/shared/modelServers.ts` now.** The runner probes them to find an agent a model and Scribe sends a dictation to one, and the two would have drifted the first time either moved.
- `tests/scribe-edit.test.ts` holds every one of these, the guard against real answers a model really writes and the pass itself against a real server on loopback: the route asked, the brief carried, the answer whole rather than streamed, and every way a failure ends as the rules' own writing. `tests/scribe-edit-probe.test.ts` renders the page, and what it holds is the two rows standing only where there is something for them to say, the server asked again when the address changes, and the address written down once it is settled rather than on each keystroke.
- `yarn scribe-edit-check` is the only thing that can see the rest of it, and here that is the whole of the quality rather than a corner of it. Whether a small model on somebody's own machine really follows the brief, and how often the guard refuses it for trying, are not things a suite can answer: a fake hands back whatever it was written to hand back and every case passes. It runs the real dictations through the real rules and the real pass against whatever this machine is serving, prints what came back and how long it took, and says which of the brief's own promises held. A machine with nothing running says so on a line and checks nothing, the way `yarn local-check` does, and a failure is printed rather than made the exit code, since a model that writes 4.30 for half past four is the model rather than the code.

## The menu bar

A mark in the macOS menu bar, and a panel under it holding who is here: the people online and the agents working, the same list the presence popover in the app shows.

- The icon is the same three discs, drawn once by `yarn icon` into `src/main/tray-png.ts` as black art on nothing. macOS reads a template image by its alpha alone and tints it to whatever the bar is wearing, so one image covers a light bar and a dark one, and nothing has to follow the system theme by hand. Everywhere else the taskbar has no template images, so the tray keeps the dot it always drew.
- The count of tasks waiting for review rides beside the mark as the item's own title, in the system's digit face. A badge painted into the artwork would be a smudge: a menu bar item is one line tall, and the number is the thing being read. It is the same count the button in the app carries, through the same `badgeText`, and it moves the moment the app's own badge does.
- No context menu is set on the mac tray. One would open on a left click too, and the left click belongs to the panel. Right click pops the menu itself.
- The panel is a frameless window that loads the renderer at `#tray`. It never connects to the session: a second connection would be a second member. The window that is open publishes who it can see, main holds it, and the panel is handed it. With no window open there is nothing to publish, so the panel says to open Crew rather than showing a list that stopped moving.
- It is built and loaded before anyone clicks, the way a terminal is warmed, so the first click opens a list rather than an empty card. It measures itself and asks main for the height it needs, so the window is only ever as tall as what it holds.
- Two things about that window empty the dock, and both are one line each. `skipTaskbar` turns the app into an accessory on macOS, and `setVisibleOnAllWorkspaces` does the same transform unless it is passed `skipTransformProcessType`. Either one and the icon leaves the dock and does not come back. `createPanelOptions` in `window-options.ts` is where the first is kept out.
- macOS will not hand out the highlight it draws behind a menu bar item unless the item is opening a native menu, and Electron dropped `setHighlightMode` in version 9. A panel of one's own does not get it.
- A menu bar item is as wide as its image plus eight points either side, and as tall as the bar, which is 24 on a normal display and 33 or more on a notched Mac. The image is centred in that and never scales it, so nothing drawn into the artwork can line up with the item's own edges.

## What a window is called

The line in the dock's own menu, in the Window menu and under Mission Control. It is the project and then whatever that window is standing on: `device-os | Chat`, `device-os | The android build is failing on CI`, `device-os | Docs`.

- Every window said Crew, because `index.html` carries `<title>Crew</title>` and nothing ever wrote another one. Five windows open is five rows of the same word with a tick beside one of them, and the one thing a person is doing there, picking the window they want, is the one thing the list cannot help with.
- Nothing in main sets a title. Electron's own `page-title-updated` already moves the native title to whatever the page says, so `document.title` is the whole of it and there is no message to send and nothing to keep in step.
- `windowName` in `src/shared/windowName.ts` is the rule, and the project comes first because it is what somebody is looking for. What the window is standing on is cut at `SHOWING_LIMIT`, since macOS truncates a long title through the middle and the middle is where the project stops being readable. A thread title is already capped at 80 by the host, which is twice what a dock menu will show.
- The separator is a pipe. An em dash is what every other app reaches for here and the writing rule refuses it.
- `placeTitle` in `views/home/place.ts` is what a place is called, and `placesOf` reads it too, so the word in the window list is the word on the row in the rail, a name somebody gave it included. A crew joined over a link is its address rather than the folder work happens in, which is what the rail already says, and a shared project stays on its own folder: the key is what tells the two apart, since `link` is set on a host that is sharing as well as on a guest.
- A thread reads the way it reads in the rail rather than the way it reads inside itself, which is `threadName` beside `threadAsk`. `listTitle` is the one place the first letter is raised, so a list of windows and a list of threads never disagree about it.
- The chat that belongs to this machine is `PERSONAL_PLACE`, which is written down once and read by both main and the naming. It has no folder and no link to be named after, so it is named outright.
- Five windows and five callers: the app, the way in, a thread stood out on its own, the personal chat and a browser tab in a window of its own. Each one says what it is standing on and `useWindowName` says the rest, so nothing anywhere works the project out twice.
- `tests/window-name.test.ts` holds the rule with no window in the room and `tests/window-name-probe.test.ts` renders the app and reads `document.title` back off it, since a hook nobody calls is a title that quietly never changes.

## Design boards

Crew owns the board engine in `src/renderer/src/canvas`. It keeps the persisted record and snapshot format stable so existing boards, server messages, and agent operations need no migration.

- `canvas/index.ts` is the renderer-facing boundary. Design code imports canvas behavior from there and no external drawing engine sits behind it.
- Shapes are DOM elements under one camera transform. The overlay canvas paints selection chrome, resize and rotation handles, brushes, snap indicators, and shape handles in page space.
- The store separates document, session, and presence records. Remote changes never enter local undo history. Loaded version-2 schema metadata is preserved unchanged when a board is saved again.
- The canvas draws straight. Sans type, solid strokes, straight lines, no hand drawn wobble. `DESIGN_STYLE_DEFAULTS` feeds both `applyDesignDefaults` and `designops`.
- Defaults are applied on mount and after every snapshot so the next shape is styled consistently.
- Nothing wears a text outline. `shapeUtils.ts` turns it off for text, geo, and arrow labels.
- A frame is white in both themes unless its `meta.background` says otherwise. `frameFill.ts` owns the fill and contrasting outline.
- Artwork colors never follow the app theme. The editor stays in one color mode while Crew chrome and the surface around the artwork may flip.
- A board mention wears `FrameGlyph`. `BoardImage` renders its preview through the synchronous owned SVG exporter and holds a `Skeleton` over the image until it has painted.
- The left panel is layers when nothing is selected and the inspector when something is selected. The right panel is board chat. Boards already serve the role of pages, so there is no page switcher.
- Both panels and the canvas run to the top of the window, and the board has no bar of its own. The one it had was 40 pixels of chrome under a 70 pixel header, so a board opened on 110 pixels of furniture before any of the work was on screen. What was on it lives where it belongs instead: the board's name and the view controls in the app header, which is the row a page already gets, and everything about a panel on the panel itself.
- **The header is a page's own row rather than one fixed grid, and a page reaches both ends of it as well as the middle.** `headerSlot.ts` holds four places, a backdrop and then left, centre and right, and `HeaderSlot` is handed which one it is filling. It was one cell in the middle, which is why everything a board had to say was crammed into the middle of a bar with empty ends. The board's name stands at one end and the history and the zoom at the other, and nothing about that is Design's own arrangement: the toolbox and the tasks came off this bar, so what is left of it belongs to whatever page is up.
- **In Design the header paints no ground at all, because the page is really under it.** It was `ink-900` across the top of an `ink-950` canvas first, then three bands standing in for the columns underneath, and both were a strip of chrome where the artwork should have been: the one thing the panels running to the header was for, a canvas that reaches the top of the window, was cut off an inch down either way. So the page has no room held for the header, each panel carries `TOP_BAR_H` of its own, and their surface and their border run to the window's edge, which is what the bands were drawing. The canvas takes the rest of it, so the board is behind the header rather than beginning under it, and a shape can be moved up there and read.
- What keeps the name and the view controls legible over artwork is `.design-scrim`, the page scrim's own ramp painted in `--design-canvas`. It is drawn in `DesignStage` rather than in the header, since it belongs to the canvas it stands over and the panels want none of it, and it is `--design-scrim` deep, which is more than the bar's own height so what stands in that row has ground under it whatever the board is doing. It wears the `design` class, or the token resolves to nothing outside the canvas tree. `scripts/design-header-look.mjs` reads back that the fade covers the canvas column exactly and wears the canvas's own colour, since a fade of the wrong colour is a stain nothing else can see.
- **A control in the header stands at the canvas's own edge, and the room the app's chrome takes is the floor under it.** The board's name lands on the left edge of the canvas and the zoom on the right edge, so the layers band and the chat band are the panels' and the faces stand over the chat where people belong. Neither number is written down: the window corner and the app's own controls both measure themselves into `headerSlot`, since the corner is a mark of a width nobody here picked plus a rail button plus 64 points of traffic lights that are not there in full screen, and a constant for it is wrong on the next Mac anybody opens. Pinned, the corner stands over the rail and takes nothing, which falls out of the same sum rather than being a case of its own. `headerBand.ts` is that arithmetic and nothing else, so a suite reads it with no window in the room.
- **What stands at that edge is the name's own letters rather than the pill they sit in.** The last thing at the other end of the bar is a face, which is a circle with no padding anywhere on it, so it really does sit the header's own 24 from the window. The name carried its pill's 14 on top of that same 24 and stood 38 in, which reads as a bar heavier at one end than the other. `nameInset` carries the pill's room and `namePull` takes it back off, so with a panel open the pill still lands on the panel's border and none of those states move at all.
- The pull is capped at the room that is really free, which is `namePull` asking how far the name stands from the window corner before it pulls. Unpinned, the corner's own box ends exactly where the name begins, since `cornerRoom` takes the header's 24 off precisely because the corner's `px-6` has already paid it, so a pull there tucks the left of the pill under a band that is `app-drag` and `z-[55]`: a button that moves the window rather than opening the boards. It pulls where the header's own edge is the floor and nowhere else.
- The boards open on the name's own left edge rather than centred under it. Centred was right while the name stood in the middle of the bar, and at the edge it hung the card out past the button on both sides, so the one thing the header is placed by, the left edge, was the one edge nothing lined up on. What lands there is the card's edge on the pill's edge, which is `align="start"` and what every other menu in the app already does. The words in the rows sit the menu's own 5 inside the name's, since a row carries a card's padding and the pill carries its own, and closing that would put the card's edge past the pill's for the sake of a line of type: a stacked pair of boxes is read by its edges before its letters.
- The room the app's own controls take is measured with the gap in front of them folded in, which is why that gap is padding on the group rather than a `gap` on the row it stands in. Read without it the zoom lands 8 pixels short of the canvas edge, which is the one failure here that reads as a rounding error rather than as a rule being wrong. `yarn design-header-look` stands the header up in a real window at six panel states and reads back where every control really landed, because that 8 was invisible in every suite, and it says outright whether the name's letters and the face are level once the panels are away and the rail is pinned.
- The zoom is the reading and the way to change it in one button rather than a minus, a figure and a plus: the steps, the fit and the selection are all in the menu it opens, and a canvas is zoomed by pinching it anyway. One rule stands between the history and the zoom, and none in front of the history: a rule is what separates the name from the tools, and the name is at the other end of the bar now.
- A panel is put away from its own top right corner and asked back from a circle standing beside the tool bar, on the side it comes in from. That is the rule the side panel already holds, which is one control for the state you are not in: nothing stands there while the panel is up, since the close inside it is already on the screen.
- The circle wears what is behind it rather than a mark for a panel. The layers is the sheets, drawn the way the copy mark is with the one behind it left as an open corner, and the board chat is the pet of the agent on it, which is a real face rather than a drawing of a conversation. A board nobody has asked about yet wears the ask mark, since that is the whole of what is behind the button until somebody has.
- The sticky note is not a tool on the bar. Agents still make notes through the design API and the ones already on a board are untouched, and what came off is the button and the letter it answered to.
- The inspector follows Figma's section order: Position, Layout, Appearance, Typography, Fill, Stroke, Effects. Unsupported fields are omitted.
- Every number field supports arrow keys and horizontal scrubbing. `useNumberField` marks and squashes the whole scrub into one undo entry.
- Typography lives in `meta`, except alignment in shape props. `TextUtil.tsx` uses the same style for measurement and painting. `DesignTextUtil` measures again when a web font arrives.
- The contextual rich-text toolbar supports bold, italic, code, links, lists, and highlighting. Stored ProseMirror documents and marks render unchanged.
- There is one inspector for every shape kind. `nodeView.ts`, `designView`, `frameView`, `textType.ts`, and `paletteView` map each record into it.
- Shapes made with the shape tool are Crew design nodes. Their outlines live in `nodeShape.ts`. A rectangle alone supports corner radius and auto layout.
- Hidden fills, strokes, and effects remain in the record with `visible: false`. Every renderer and exporter honors it.
- `ColorPicker.tsx` owns saturation, hue, alpha, hex, eyedropper, and Crew swatches. `.design-picker` in `styles.css` sizes the picker.
- `wholePixels.ts` rounds position and box edges after create, move, and resize. Lines and freehand strokes only round their position.
- `SelectionOverlay.tsx` adds the size readout and Crew node radius handles. The owned overlay canvas provides the standard selection, brush, handle, and snapping chrome.
- Board cursors live in `cursors.tsx` and use `--crew-cursor-<type>`. `applyDesignCursors` writes them on the canvas container, while `applyToolCursor` updates the active placement cursor.
- Remote cursors are Crew's arrow plus a glass name tag, agent pet, or human avatar. Presence records still carry remote selection state.
- A cursor leaves when the pointer leaves the board while the person's selection remains until their page becomes null.
- Point and gap snapping are both live during translation and resize. Indicators are collected again after the snap so every resulting alignment is shown.
- Every canvas action lives in `DESIGN_COMMANDS`. Context menus, Actions, and keyboard shortcuts read the same catalogue.
- A mask reparents the rest of the selection into its bottom shape and clips them to that shape's sampled outline.
- Asking an agent is available from the context menu or `⇧⌘A`. The ask bar stays inside the stage and grows away from the selection.

## Tickets

`/tickets` opens a thread with a board beside it, in the side panel where the plan stands. It is what the work is broken down into, what is being worked on now, the questions the agent answered for itself, and what it has finished and wants a look at.

- The board is Crew's own, and that is the whole of why it works. It was read off the task list each CLI keeps for itself once, and one vendor renaming its own todo tool took the columns out overnight: the list stopped arriving as a list and started arriving one task at a time, so there was nothing to fold. The harness lives here now. `/tickets` on the host's own http is the way in, exactly as `/agents/spawn` is, so it is a curl line in the preamble rather than a feature of any one CLI and every provider gets the same board at once.
- Four calls and no more: put the tickets up and get an id for each, move one, name a decision, raise a question. `ticketPreamble` in `src/shared/tickets.ts` is the whole of what an agent is told, and it is written on the machine running the agent rather than on the host, the same reason the helpers and the design board write theirs there: only that side knows the address it reaches the session at. Every call names the promptId of the run asking, and the host takes it only while that prompt is one it is running right now, so a board is reachable from the thread it belongs to and nowhere else.
- Nothing about a board is state the host holds. Each call is written down as one event on the thread, `ticket.added`, `ticket.moved`, `ticket.decided` and `ticket.asked`, and `boardOf` folds the whole board back out of them, so it replays for free and is the same on every machine.
- They are left out of the chat's own events and carried in the snapshot as a list of their own, the way the shelf and the todos are, and the window holds them in a slice of its own for the same reason. Filtered out of the snapshot and nowhere else, a board is whatever has happened since the window last connected, which reads as every board emptying on a reload.
- The list a CLI keeps for itself is still read, and it is the fallback rather than the board. A thread that never put a ticket up gets rough columns off `stepTodos` in `src/runner/providers/detail.ts` for nothing, and the moment the agent puts its own tickets up those win outright. Never let the two run together, or the same work stands on the board twice under two names.
- The one line a step says about a list is still narrated, "Drawing the rows", because that is what a live step reads like. A ticket is the same item named rather than narrated, "Draw the rows", which is why `activeForm` wins in one place and nowhere else.
- A question never stops the work. The agent raises it, answers it for itself, and carries on, and the row says what it assumed. Answering costs one press, and what answering late would cost is on the row as the number of files that have changed since, so interrupting is a number rather than a feeling.
- A decision hangs off the ticket it names, or off whatever is on doing when it was written, so an agent that names nothing still lands it somewhere true.
- Which files a ticket touched is the ones that changed while it was the one on doing, which is why the steps and the events are read as one run of time rather than one after the other.
- In review is the column that pays for the rest. Everything else is already readable in the thread, and this is the agent saying a slice is finished. Both ways out are real messages: a sentence goes back as a steer aimed at that slice, and picking the ticket up again is what the board reads as the way out of review.
- What you have dealt with is yours rather than the crew's, so a question you answered and a review you have looked at are held in the window, in `state/board.ts`, and never written down. Two people each get their own review queue, which is what In review means.
- The columns run in the order they want you, not the order work travels: a review, then what is being worked on, then what is next, then what is done. The panel is 480 across and a ticket is a sentence, so they are sections down the panel rather than columns across it. Four columns at 480 is 105 pixels each.
- Claude and Codex take a steer mid-run, so anywhere else an answer joins the queue.

## Memory

What the crew has learned, in one list every agent on every machine reads before it starts. It is three events in the chat log, so it syncs for free, replays for free, and nobody scrolls past the moment a fact landed.

- The two halves are deliberately uneven. Reading is free: the host puts the whole list on the prompt itself, beside the tickets and the helpers, so every agent has it in front of it before it does anything and nothing has to be fetched, remembered or polled. Writing is a curl on the host's own http, the way a ticket and a helper are, so every provider gets it at once rather than one CLI's own tool. A store an agent has to remember to open is a store most runs never open, which is the whole reason the reading half is not a call.
- Every memory is in every prompt, so the list is a budget rather than a store. A line is `MEMORY_TEXT_LIMIT` and the list is `MEMORY_LIMIT`, both in `src/shared/memory.ts`, and anything longer than a sentence is a doc. A full list is refused with the move to make, "Take one out before adding another", rather than the oldest being dropped for somebody: a fact quietly falling off the end is worse than a call that failed and said why.
- One list, not one per agent. Cross agent is the whole point of it, and a scope would be a second thing to explain on every screen and in every preamble for a distinction nobody asked for.
- The same fact twice is one memory, keyed on the words the way `askKey` keys a question, so the second call comes back with the id the first one got rather than writing a second row that says the same thing.
- An id is six hex characters rather than a uuid. It is written out beside every line, sixty times over, in front of every agent on every run, and 36 characters of noise a row is a paragraph of nothing.
- A ghost thread is refused in words. Its events never reach the log, so a memory written from one would either vanish with the window or leak the hidden thread into the crew's own history, and there is no third answer. Refused, the model reads why instead of watching a call quietly not appear.
- A write over http is refused unless the promptId names a run the host has going right now, which is the credential the board and the helpers are already reached on. A write over the socket is refused from a runner, the way the music controls are: an agent's machine is connected the whole time it is joined, and the door it writes through is the live prompt.
- The three events fold off the snapshot the way the toolbox does, so the memory rides in `SessionSnapshot` as a list of its own and a fact learned months ago is still a fact after its event has fallen off the window.
