- Whoever wrote what is there now owns the line, so rewriting one puts the new name on it. The row wears `AgentIcon` for an agent and `Avatar` for a person, which is the one thing a list of sentences cannot say about itself.
- The page is under Crew in the settings, since it is everyone's. The way in stands at the end of the list, so it is the empty state and the way in both, and adding and editing are one card because they are one question. What comes back from a refusal is said on that card rather than somewhere the person is not looking.
- The line under the heading is the one page that has earned one. A list of sentences and a button cannot say that every agent reads them before it starts, and that is the whole of what makes this different from a notes page.

## Docs

A page of the crew's own writing, block by block, in a BlockNote editor crew draws all the chrome for. Every panel BlockNote ships is turned off in `DocEditor` and replaced: `DocSlashMenu` for what `/` opens, `DocToolbar` for a selection, `DocSideMenu` for the gutter, `DocEmojiMenu` for `:`. They are crew's own glass menus, the same rows the mention menu uses.

- **The writing takes the middle of the page, and it only gives that up to stay clear of the list.** `docLeft` in `docsLayout.ts` is the whole rule: the column is centred in the page, and where centring would put it under the list of pages it stands a gap after the list instead and the slack all falls to the right. Centred in what is left over beside the list, which is what it was, the writing was pushed into the right corner of the window by a rail and a list that together take 520 of it, and the wider the window the further over it went. The other way round, holding it symmetric by padding both sides by the list's own width, buys that symmetry with the measure: at 1440 it takes 96 off the line length, and the measure is the whole of what a writing column is for.
- The list of pages is a list in the page rather than a second rail, so it wears the app's own row: `rounded-xl`, lit as `bg-fg/[0.08]`, quiet at `text-fg/70` with the mark at `text-fg/45`, which is `NavRow`'s grammar written at the one place a doc row needs a chevron and an add beside it. It lit its own row in `ink-800` before the rail existed, and `ink-800` is what the rail is made of, so the page you were reading read as a piece of rail adrift in the page.
- Where you are in the tree is the page's own row in the header rather than a line over the writing. It stands at the writing's own left edge, `trailInset` against the room the window corner takes, so it sits exactly over the title it belongs to and is never pulled back under the corner. Written above the title it moved the whole page down by the depth of the page you happened to open, and the header over it said nothing at all. A page with no parents says nothing there, since the title an inch below it is not news.
- `tests/docs-layout.test.ts` holds the numbers, `tests/docs-columns-probe.test.ts` holds the trail landing in the header and the lit row, and `yarn docs-look` is the only thing that can see the rest of it: it stands the real page up in a real window at six widths and rail states and reads back where the list, the writing and the trail really landed.
- A doc is a markdown file the crew syncs, so the editor only offers blocks markdown can carry. `DOC_BLOCKS` in `docBlocks.ts` is that list, one entry holding the title, the mark, the aliases and the shortcut, and both the slash menu and the turn into menu read it. A toggle list is left out on purpose: it comes back from markdown as a bulleted list, and a block that quietly changes on the next sync is worse than one that was never offered. `tests/doc-blocks-probe.test.ts` puts every entry through a round trip, so nothing can be added that the file cannot hold.
- The marks are drawn in `docGlyphs.tsx`, on the same grid and the same 2 stroke as the rest of the app's icons. `glyph()` takes the weight, so a mark in this set can be drawn lighter where it has to be: the numerals beside the headings are, because a figure standing next to a letter is a smaller drawing than the letter is.
- The menu reads as its three groups or it reads as nothing. The text blocks are letterforms, the lists are rows with a different marker down the side, and the blocks are objects. Every mark has to be told from every other at 16, which is what decides these: quote is the quotation mark rather than a bar with lines, because a bar with lines beside it is what a list is; the numbered list carries two rows rather than three, because a numeral small enough for three of them is a smudge; and code is two chevrons with nothing between them, drawn closer than brackets would sit so the middle of the box is not empty.
- Letters share one cap height and one baseline, `CAP` and `BASE` in `docGlyphs.tsx`. The pilcrow, the three headings and the marks the toolbar wears are one face, so they are drawn to those two lines rather than each to its own, and the family sits under the box keyline on purpose: a letter grown until it fills a 17 box towers over the row it is read in. That is also why the set is not audited by `yarn icons`, which measures objects. The numbers are still printed.
- Punctuation is solid. A quotation mark is filled the way it is in every face it is set in, and a counter that small closes up at 16 regardless, so filled is the honest drawing of what it becomes.
- Code blocks are lit by the same shiki crew already uses for a diff, through `docCode.ts`. It hands BlockNote a highlighter that always asks for both themes at once, so a block carries `--shiki-light` and `--shiki-dark` and flips with the app instead of being highlighted again. The languages, their names and their aliases all come from `highlight.ts`, so a fence written as `ts` or `bash` lands on the same grammar the rest of the app uses.
- Three backticks open a code block on the third one, in `docFence.ts`, rather than on the space or the return after it. BlockNote's own rule wants one of those two, so somebody who typed the three and carried on was writing in a paragraph, which is what everybody does and what nothing on the screen says. What it costs is the language written inline: `ts` typed after the backticks is the first word of the code now, and the language is picked on the block's own header, which is where it is read back from anyway. Only at the head of a block, so backticks written into a sentence and backticks written inside a code block are both left as they were typed.
- Placeholders, the language names and every label are crew's words, held in `docSchema.ts` as a dictionary over BlockNote's own.
- **A bullet is drawn rather than set.** The three the editor writes are characters, `•`, `◦` and `▪︎`, so each one is whatever the face happens to carry: three sizes, three weights, and the first of them a speck beside 16 point type. What stands instead is a shape, and the cycle is Notion's, a filled circle, then a ring, then a square, and it starts over, since the one thing a marker says is how far in the row is. `--doc-bullet` is how wide it is and both margins are worked back from it, so whatever size it is drawn at the marker sits on the middle of the 24 the gutter has always been and the words start where they always did. The square is under the circle on the ruler, the same reason a circle is not 17 on the keylines.
- The character is painted out rather than taken away. The editor writes it from three rules of its own and the deepest of them is heavier than anything worth writing here, so at the fourth level of nesting `▪︎` is still what wins: the box keeps it, `color: transparent` is what hides it, and the shape is drawn in a background and a border, which nothing in there ever sets. Written as a fight over `content` it worked at the first level and quietly failed at the rest.
- It stands on the middle of the line rather than on a share of the font. A doc says 1.7 and a block sets 1.5 inside it, so half an em of the one is a pixel and a half off the other and the marker rode low on every row. `0.5lh` is half of the line the marker really stands beside, and it stays right whichever of those two is ever changed.
- Nothing runs down the left of a sub-bullet. The line is the editor's own and it is turned off rather than restyled, since where a row sits is already said by the indent, and a hairline for every level of it turns a list into a diagram of itself.
- `yarn doc-bullets-look` is the only thing that can see any of this, because it stands the real editor up in a real window and reads back what each of six levels really drew: the shape, the gutter, and how far off the line it landed. Every one of those was green in a suite while the marker was 1.6 low and the line was still there. `tests/doc-bullets.test.ts` holds the ladder and the arithmetic with no window in the room.
- **A numbered list is lettered a level in.** The ladder is a number, then a letter, then a roman numeral, and it starts over, the same three step shape the bullets hold and the one Google Docs and Word both settled on. Numbered the whole way down, a list cannot say how far in a row is: a `1.` under a `1.` under a `1.` is three rows that read as one row three times, and the indent is left as the only thing telling them apart, which is the least legible half of a list.
- The count is the editor's own and is never counted again here. BlockNote works out where a row really stands and writes it on the block as `data-index`, a list interrupted and resumed included and one told to start at three included, so what the marker does is read that number and spell it another way. `counter-reset: doc-order attr(data-index type(<integer>), 1)` is the whole of that and `counter(doc-order, lower-alpha)` is the spelling, and it is why nothing here runs a counter of its own down the page: a second count would have to answer every one of those questions again and would be wrong the first time either the editor or a doc moved. It is said twice, off `data-index` and off `data-prev-index`, since a block changing type carries the index it had a moment ago and the marker is drawn from that while it travels.
- **Which marker a list wears is its depth and nothing else, and that is the markdown rule again.** Markdown has one ordered list and one unordered one, and `a.` is not a marker in either: a list somebody set to letters by hand comes back from the next sync as a paragraph, and one set to numbers at a depth the ladder letters comes back lettered. That is the same reason the toggle list is left out, a block that quietly changes on the next sync is worse than one that was never offered. Turning a list between numbers and bullets is markdown's own distinction and stays on the Turn into menu, where it already is.
- `yarn doc-numbers-look` is the only thing that can see any of this. It stands the real editor up in a real window with a list nested six deep and reads back, level by level, which counter style the row asked for and what number it really took off the editor, because a marker asking for letters and reading nothing is a row of empty gutters that no suite over the stylesheet can see. `tests/doc-numbers.test.ts` holds the ladder with no window in the room.
- An emoji in a doc is the app's own picture, the same sheet the chat draws from, so a face is the same face wherever it is written. What is written down is untouched: the character stays the character, because a doc is a markdown file and what somebody typed is what the file holds. `docEmoji.ts` is a decoration over every emoji in the document and the sheet is painted over the glyph rather than in place of it, so the caret lands where it always did and a selection paints the band under it. Code is quoted as it was written, a fence and a run of inline code both, which is the rule prose is already drawn under. The crew's own `:name:` emoji are left as the words they were typed in: a name is ten characters and a picture standing on it would cover the rest of the line, which is the same reason the composer leaves them alone.
- Tab takes the row standing under the pointer, which is the first until somebody moves. It is caught on the way down so the editor never sees it, or a Tab written inside a list indents the row instead of finishing the word, and a Tab written after `/tab` lands a tab character in the sentence rather than the table it is pointing at. It belongs to both menus rather than to the emoji one, which is what `useDocMenu` is: the Tab and the selected row being scrolled to are the same two things in the slash menu and in the emoji menu, and written twice one of them was written once.
- **What is typed is ranked rather than filtered, or the row Tab lands on is whichever block happens to stand earliest in the table.** BlockNote's own filter is a plain `includes` over the title and the aliases and it keeps the order it was given, so `/ta` put To-do list first, on the strength of `task`, with Table underneath it. `slashMatches` is the order instead: the whole name, then a whole alias, then a name that starts with what was typed, then an alias that does, then the two that only contain it. It is the rule the path menu in the composer already holds, that a name starting with what was typed beats a run of letters scattered through one.
- A group is never split down the list. The rank decides which group comes first and the order inside it, and the rows of that group are then kept together, since the heading is drawn wherever the group changes from the row above: interleaved, one query draws Lists twice with Blocks in the middle, which reads as a menu that has lost its place rather than as an order.
- The plus and the drag handle line up on the first line of the block, which centers them on a block that is one line tall and holds them near the top of one that is not. BlockNote anchors the gutter to the top of the block and nothing else, so `blockAnchor.ts` measures the block itself: the first line box of its text, or half the block's own height capped for something with no text to read, like a divider or a picture. A table of paddings per kind of block was what it was before, and it was wrong on the first block of a doc, on a divider, and on anything the table had never heard of.
- A block being dragged is a small card, not a copy of the block at full width. `.bn-drag-preview` in `styles.css` is the only handle on it, because BlockNote clones the block into the body where nothing else can reach it.
- The room under the last block is one number, `--doc-tail`, and one of two things carries it rather than both. Where the writing ends on a line to write in, it is the editor's own padding and a click there puts the caret in that line. Where it does not, it is the trailing block, and a click anywhere in it writes a new line at the foot of the doc. It was the padding alone with the trailing block standing 30 pixels in the top of it, so everything below those 30 fell to the nearest text position: a doc ending in a code block took the caret into the snippet from anywhere on the empty page under it, and there was no way to write after the code at all. Both carrying it is a doc that ends on twice the gap it asked for, which is why the padding is said with `:not(:has(.bn-trailing-block))`. `tests/doc-tail-rule.test.ts` holds the pair and `tests/doc-tail-probe.test.ts` holds the click.
- **A table is drawn as a grid rather than as a card with rows inside it.** The outline is on the cells: the corner cells carry the radius, and the first row and the first column carry the top and left hairlines. A box around them with `overflow: hidden` rounds the corners in one line and it also clips the two pieces of chrome that really are children of the cells, the column resize handle standing a pixel past the last column and the drop cursor standing three above the first row. On the cells there is nothing anywhere that clips, and the outline still holds when a wide table is scrolled inside its own box.
- **The room around a table is BlockNote's, and it is the whole of what makes the two add bars reachable.** The wrapper holds 9 pixels at the top and left for the handles, which stand half over the table, and 22 at the right and bottom for the bar that adds a row and the bar that adds a column. Crew set that padding to nothing once, and what it cost was not the handles: those are drawn in the container rather than in the wrapper and were never clipped. It was that the only way to reveal either bar by reaching past the table is hovering wrapper pixels below it or right of it, and with no padding there are none, so a table could only ever gain a row from inside the last cell in it. The room is given back with a negative margin on each side and a width that grows by the same, so the table's own edges land on the writing's edges and the bars stand in the page's margin rather than inside the measure.
- **The bar that adds a row is capped at the measure.** It is sized to the table it hangs off, and a table wider than the writing column scrolls rather than squeezing, so on a wide one it was drawn at the table's full scroll width and ran off the side of the page. `--doc-measure` is the writing column written down in CSS, and `tests/doc-tables.test.ts` holds it to `DOC_MAX_W` less both gutters, since a stylesheet cannot import the number the layout owns. It is the same reason the disk image's icon positions are written down twice.
- **The handles are Crew's own, the way every panel BlockNote ships already is.** Each one is a bar rather than a card with a grip drawn in it: 4 across and 16 down for a row, the same bar turned for a column, quiet at `fg/25` and lit under the pointer. It stands in a box 20 by 24 and that box is the whole of the aiming, because BlockNote places a row handle 10 pixels over the table's left edge and a column handle 12 over its top, so those two numbers are what put the bar on the border rather than beside it. What it opens is Crew's own `Popover`, so a table's menu reads like every other menu in the app.
- **A doc table offers only what markdown can carry, which is the rule the toggle list is already left out under.** A header row survives. A header column does not, and it is worse than lost: written out and read back it comes home as a header row, so the one thing it changes is the wrong one. Cell colour, cell text colour and merged cells are all off for the same reason, which is also why the cell handle never draws at all. What is left is add above and below, or left and right, the header row on the first row alone, and delete, which stands last under a rule because it is the one press nobody can undo.
- **A column width is written down beside the table, because markdown has nowhere else to put it.** `<!-- crew:cols 200 90 -->` stands one blank line above the table it belongs to, a dash for a column nobody sized, and `docTables.ts` is the whole of reading and writing it. A comment is what carries it rather than the frontmatter the title already uses, and the reason is a hand edit: the frontmatter would have to key on the table's position in the page, so deleting one table silently hands every table after it somebody else's widths, where a comment sitting on the table goes when the table goes. Every other markdown reader draws nothing for it, which is the point.
- **The blank line under that comment is load bearing.** Written straight above the table with no line between, the comment does not annotate the table, it destroys it: what comes back is three paragraphs and no table at all. That is measured rather than feared, and it is why the pair is written and read in one place and never spelled out by hand.
- **A mark is never left in what anybody reads.** It is stripped on the way into the editor, and also by `docExcerpt` and by the doc text the host puts on a prompt, so nobody's hover card spends a line on it and no agent is handed a comment it has no way to read. BlockNote drops the comment on parse anyway, so an unstripped one would be erased by the first edit, which is the other half of why the editor re-adds it on every save rather than trusting the round trip.
- **Nothing is written for a table nobody touched.** No comment and no colon, so opening a doc and saving it changes not one byte, which is what keeps a git history of docs from filling with churn nobody caused.
- **Column alignment is markdown's own and BlockNote loses it in both directions.** It never writes the colons, and handed `|:---|---:|` it reads every cell back as left, so opening an aligned doc and touching nothing stripped the alignment out of the file. Crew reads the delimiter row itself and writes it back, so the column menu offers left, centre and right: it is per column rather than per cell because that is the whole of what the file can say. `left` is written as no colon at all, since it is also the default and writing it would put colons through every table in every doc for nothing.
- **A line break in a cell used to break the table open, and that was the worst of the three.** A cell holding two lines serialized to a backslash and a real newline in the middle of the row, so a two by two came back as a three by two with the data shifted down and a stray backslash in it. Nothing had to be typed to reach it, since `<br>`, which is how GFM writes a line break and what any hand written table uses, parses into exactly that. `mendDocTableRows` is the rule: inside a table every line ends with a pipe, so one that does not is a row split in half and is joined back with `<br>`.
- What is still lost is what the menu still refuses. A header column comes home as a header row, cell colour goes, and a merged cell is worse than dropped: it comes back as a blank cell the table never had. Those are BlockNote's own losses through GFM, they cannot be carried in a comment without inventing a table format nobody else reads, and the answer stays what it was, which is not to offer them.
- **A table sets its own line.** The writing runs at 16 on 1.7 and a table runs at 14 on 1.5 with 7 and 11 of padding, so a row of one line stands 36 tall rather than 45 and a screen holds the whole of what is being read. An empty cell is held open at exactly one line, `1lh`, which is the cell's own line rather than a share of the font: BlockNote asks for 1.5rem, which is taller than the line a table is set on, and it leaves an empty row standing above its neighbours.
- **The row under the pointer says so and the header never does.** A table is read across rather than down, and 652 pixels of four columns is a long way for an eye to carry one row, which is the whole of what the wash is for. It is `ink-hover`, the app's own, and it is said on `td` alone, so the header is the one row that never moves.
- `yarn doc-tables-look` stands a table up in a real window at both themes, hovers the six places the chrome is revealed from, and reads back where every bar really landed, because a bar drawn past the edge of the page is invisible in a suite and looks perfect in the numbers. It also drags a real column border and follows the width the whole way, into the markdown and back into a fresh editor, which is the one part of this no suite can see: a suite hands the save synthetic widths, and what a drag really produces is the question. **The drag has to be driven with `sendInputEvent` and `leftButtonDown` rather than with a `MouseEvent`.** prosemirror-tables opens its move handler with `if (!event.which) return`, and a synthetic event has `which: 0`, so the drag ends on its first move and lands a zero width change that reads as a pass. `tests/doc-tables.test.ts` holds the rules and the room with no window in it, and `tests/doc-tables-probe.test.ts` puts a header row, a header column, a column width, an alignment and a line break in a cell through a real round trip, and holds a doc nobody touched to coming back out byte for byte.

## Talking to Codex

Codex runs on `codex app-server`, a JSON-RPC service inside the same `codex` binary everybody already has. Nothing extra is installed and the desktop app is not needed: the app-server is a subcommand of the CLI, and `codex --version` is the whole of what has to be there.

- `codex exec --json` was the door crew knocked on before, and it only ever says what has already happened. Reasoning arrives as one finished block after the fact, an answer arrives whole, a file change carries no diff at all, and a shell call carries a raw string with a login shell wrapped round it. Live thinking, a streamed answer, real diffs and a named command were four names for one thing, which is the transport, and they closed together the moment it moved.
- A run that has to answer what it reads is a `Dialog`, in `types.ts`. It says what goes out at the start, what to write back for each line that arrives, and what a steer looks like, and having one is what makes a provider steerable. Claude has one too, which is where the message shape it writes to stdin now lives: a file that spawns every CLI in the app has no business knowing one vendor's JSON.
- The handshake is four steps and each one waits on the answer to the last: `initialize`, then the `initialized` notification with `thread/start` behind it, then `turn/start` once the thread has an id. `codex-app.ts` is that walk and nothing else, so a suite drives it by hand with no process anywhere near it.
- The sandbox is said twice and in two different shapes. `thread/start` takes `sandbox: "danger-full-access"`, a plain word, and `turn/start` takes `sandboxPolicy: { type: "dangerFullAccess" }`, a tagged one. Handing either the other's shape is refused outright, and the refusal arrives as an ordinary response nobody was reading, so the run simply never starts.
- The app-server does not exit when a turn ends, so `turn/completed` is what ends the run rather than the process going. Closing stdin is what makes it exit, which is the same grace window a Claude run already had, and it is what leaves a steer already in flight somewhere to land.
- A steer is `turn/steer` with `expectedTurnId`, a precondition the server checks, so a message aimed at a turn that has already finished is refused rather than lost inside the next one. The turn id is let go of the moment `turn/completed` lands, so a message written a breath too late is queued for a run of its own instead of being sent into nothing.
- What a command was is Codex's own reading of it. `commandActions` is on the item at `item/started`, already parsed into `read`, `listFiles`, `search` or `unknown` with the shell wrapper taken off, so a step says "Read notes.txt" rather than "Ran /bin/zsh -lc 'cat notes.txt'". Those four are named so `toolActions.ts` already knows them, which is the whole reason nothing was added to that table. Only what is named `Shell` keeps what it printed, which is the rule a file read would otherwise break by pouring itself into the log the crew syncs.
- The diff is on the item at `item/started`, so a change draws the moment it begins rather than after it lands. There are no counts anywhere in the protocol and they are walked out of the diff itself. An update arrives as a unified hunk and is passed through as it is, and an add or a delete arrives as the file's own body with no markers at all, which draws as nothing until every line is marked, so those two are marked up here.
- Text sits on block 0 and the two reasoning channels stand above it on lanes of their own, a summary on the odd numbers and the raw chain on the even. Nothing may share a number, because closing a block closes every kind at that index: reasoning landing on 0 would take the answer down with it, and a `summaryIndex` and a `contentIndex` are counted separately on the one item, so read as plain numbers the first heading and the first paragraph land on the same block and close each other. Lanes are what keep that impossible however many of either arrive, where a base picked to sit above the summaries is only a guess at how many there will be.
- What Codex says on the way is not the answer. `phase` is `commentary` while it works and `final_answer` at the end, so the running commentary streams into the thread where it belongs and is kept out of what gets posted as the reply. A turn that ended without a final answer falls back to the commentary rather than to nothing.
- That commentary is the thinking, and it draws as one. It is the only prose channel Codex has: the reasoning summaries are bold headings and nothing else, while commentary is whole sentences about what the model is about to do, arriving token by token as it works. Read as message text it was the agent talking over its own answer, so `aside` on the block is what says which rail it belongs to, and `cli.ts` is where that is answered rather than in the parser, because the block ids the asides are kept under already live there. The bookkeeping stays on the text stream, since that is what the protocol calls it and what keeps it out of the reply; only the step that reaches the screen is a thought.
- The token count is the whole thread each time rather than the last call, so it stands in place of what has been counted rather than being added to it. The app-server says the four numbers in camelCase and counts the cached prefix inside the input the way the rest of OpenAI does, and `usageFrom` is still the one place any of that is read.
- An approval is accepted and anything else is refused in words. With the policy set to never none of them fire at all, but a server request nobody answers is a run that hangs forever, and a wrong answer to one that asks about somebody's account is worse than saying crew cannot answer it.
- The ceiling on the reasoning channel is the account rather than the transport, and it is measured rather than assumed. Every summary that channel has ever returned here is a bold heading, a median of 47 characters across 478,287 of them, and the raw chain comes back only as `encrypted_content`, which is there so the reasoning can be handed to the next turn and is readable by nobody. `item/reasoning/textDelta` is real surface and Crew draws it, on a lane of its own, proven against a provider that emits it, but only open weight models ever populate it. Codex never asks for raw reasoning and there is no parameter that would: the request carries `effort` and `summary`, and `include` is hardcoded to `reasoning.encrypted_content`. OpenAI's own terms say extracting it another way may cost the account, so nothing here tries.
- `reasoning_summary_format` is not a way out, and it is the opposite of what it reads as. It is dead client side, absent from the config schema and read by no code at all, surviving only as metadata per model, and `experimental` is the heading style rather than an escape from it. Forcing it on through `model_catalog_json`, which is the one lever that can rewrite that metadata, changes nothing, because nothing reads it. The one model marked otherwise is refused outright on a ChatGPT login, so the prose form of a summary is reachable only on an API key and a model that is not Codex tuned.
- The desktop app is not holding anything back, and its own bundle is what says so. `encrypted_content` appears in it nowhere at all, so nothing there decrypts the chain and nothing there is even handed it: the Rust core keeps the blob and posts it to the next turn, which is the whole of what it is for. What that bundle does hold is the list of notifications the app asks for, and it is the list Crew reads, `item/reasoning/textDelta` among them and every legacy `codex/event/*` name switched off beside it. Both ends listen on the same channel for the same thing, and the model is what does not send it. Its prose is the commentary, turned into steps by a detail level rather than by a channel nobody else gets.
- Never set the thinking effort from Crew's own default. The Model field offers Default and falls through to whatever the machine is configured with, and the Thinking field did not, so an effort was sent on every turn and `model_reasoning_effort` never applied to anybody. Both fields carry the empty option now, and it is the same rule twice rather than a special case.
- `tests/codex-app.test.ts` holds the handshake, the steer and every shape above, and `yarn codex-check` is the only thing that can see the rest of it: it runs a real turn against the real CLI and reads back whether reasoning arrived while the work was going, whether the answer streamed, whether a command was named and whether a diff came through with anything counted in it. Every one of those can be green in a suite and dead in the app.

## Talking to Kimi

Kimi runs on `kimi acp`, the Agent Client Protocol server inside the same `kimi` binary everybody already has. Nothing extra is installed, the same way nothing extra is installed for Codex: the server is a subcommand of the CLI, and `kimi --version` is the whole of what has to be there.

- `kimi -p --output-format stream-json` was the door crew knocked on before, and a whole turn came back as six lines of it. No reasoning at all, an answer whole at the end, a tool call whose arguments only arrive once it has already run, and not one token counted anywhere. Live thinking, a streamed answer and a named tool with real arguments were four names for one thing, which is the transport, and they opened together the moment it moved.
- The handshake is `initialize`, then `session/new`, then the settings one at a time, then `session/prompt`, and each step waits on the answer to the last. A setting sent alongside the turn would race the turn it is for.
- There is no flag to start a session on, so what a run is set to is said over the wire after the session exists. `session/set_config_option` takes `configId` rather than `id`, and `mode` and `model` both go through it. A setting the CLI will not take carries on to the turn rather than stalling it, since a model that has left `config.toml` is not worth losing a run over.
- Both file capabilities are declined, so Kimi reads and writes with its own tools. A client that offers them and answers with nothing gets a model that gives up on the file. The terminal is offered and served by `acp-terminal.ts`, because Kimi Code 0.37.2 will not run Shell without it. A command starts as the executable and argument list Kimi handed over, keeps its working folder and environment, holds only the requested tail of valid UTF-8 output, and reports its exit before Kimi releases it. Every process still alive when the run closes is killed with the run.
- Everything is approved before the turn starts, which is the posture every other CLI here runs under. The permission requests are still answered, because a request nobody answers is a run that hangs forever.
- The words arrive as a plain run of chunks with no block number on them, so the lanes are counted here. A block is a stretch of one kind, switching kind or reaching a tool closes the one that was open, and nothing reuses a number, because closing a block closes every kind standing at that index.
- The name a tool started under is the one that says what it is. Every update after the first carries a narrated title instead, "Reading math.js" where the tool is Read, so the first is kept and the rest are read for what they add. `rawInput` only lands on the last update before the tool completes, which is why a call draws as soon as it starts and fills in as it goes.
- **Kimi takes a steer, and it is the turn standing down and going again rather than a message pushed into one already running.** A second `session/prompt` mid-turn is refused outright as `turn.agent_busy`, in ten milliseconds, and discarded rather than deferred, so nothing is ever sent into a live turn. `session/cancel` is the way, a notification rather than a request, and the turn it stops answers its own prompt with `cancelled` in a few tens of milliseconds. Everything that turn had already done is still in the session: what it read, what the tools gave back, what it had said. So the prompt after it carries on with the work intact rather than from nothing, and the model knows it was interrupted and says so.
- The cancelled turn has to be let go of before the next one is asked for. A `session/prompt` written in the same breath as the cancel is swallowed by it and answers `cancelled` itself, doing nothing at all, with no error and no output to say so. That is the whole reason a steer is two moves rather than one: `steer` stashes what was typed and asks the turn to stand down, and the answer to that turn is what opens the next. Two written in one breath arrive together as one prompt, so nothing is said twice over.
- A turn cancelled to make room for a steer is not the end of the run, so the parser says nothing about it. Every other stop reason still is.
- What it costs is the tail of the turn that was interrupted, which a steer into a live turn would have kept. That is the trade, and it is the same shape as what a steer already means everywhere else in the app: somebody talking over an answer rather than undoing the work behind it.
- A dialog that answered the very line a turn ended on has opened another one in the same breath, so the run is not winding down and `cli.ts` holds `TURN_END_GRACE_MS` back for it. Without that the grace closes stdin under a turn that has only just started, which is the race a steer landing as the turn was already ending falls into: the cancel arrives too late, the turn ends of its own accord, and the message that was written gets three quarters of a second of a run to land in.
- **The local server is the other door, and it is the one the steering in the agent core was written for.** `kimi server` puts the same binary behind http, and its web UI sends every mid-turn message as `POST /api/v1/sessions/{id}/prompts` followed by `POST /api/v1/sessions/{id}/prompts:steer`, with no gesture of its own: a prompt written while a turn is going comes back `queued`, and the steer is what moves it in. Nothing is cancelled and no turn is started, the message is buffered and folded into the context at the next step, and a steer that arrives as the turn was about to stop extends that turn rather than ending it. ACP reaches none of it: it calls launch, which is what answers `turn.agent_busy`.
- It is still not the door to use, and the reason is when a steer lands rather than what it costs. That one folds the message in at a step boundary, which is a few seconds while tools are running and the whole of a generation when one is not: measured at 3.5 seconds against a run reading files, and at five minutes and twenty seconds against a run writing one long answer. Standing the turn down lands in tens of milliseconds every time. A steer nobody can feel is worse than one that costs the tail of a turn, and the tail is the part being talked over anyway. The rest of the price is the shape: a crew runs a CLI per run over stdio, and that is a daemon on a fixed port behind a bearer token, one for every run on the machine, with a websocket to read instead of a stream of lines.
- **Nothing about what a turn cost is on the wire.** Not a notification, not the prompt's own answer, not a field anywhere in a stream of two hundred messages. Kimi writes it down instead, in a log of its own beside the session, and `kimi-usage.ts` is the whole of reading it: `usage.record` lines under `~/.kimi-code/sessions/*/session_<id>/agents/main/wire.jsonl`, found by the session the handshake already knows rather than by working out the folder's hash. They are written live rather than at the end, so the count moves while the work is going.
- One record is one call of the model, so they are summed, and only the ones scoped to a turn are counted or the session's own total lands twice. Kimi counts the cached prefix beside the input the way Anthropic does, which `inputOther` says outright, so it is handed to `usageFrom` in that shape and the prefix is never charged twice at full rate.
- No price is drawn, and that is right rather than missing. There is no rate for these models in `MODEL_RATES`, nothing in the CLI or its config knows one, and the account this runs on is a subscription rather than a per-token bill. A wrong number costs more than a missing one.
- An error before the session exists ends the run and says why. Nothing has been asked for yet but the way in, so no turn is coming to end it, and a machine that never logged in would otherwise sit there for the whole ten minute idle timeout saying nothing.
- `tests/kimi-acp.test.ts` holds the handshake, the lanes, the tools, the stop reasons and the steer, the last of those against a real process: a fake CLI that ends its turn only once the cancel has arrived, which is the race, and the run is held to still saying what the steer asked for. `tests/kimi-usage.test.ts` holds what a turn cost and where it is read from, and `yarn kimi-check` is the only thing that can see the rest of it: it runs a real turn against the real CLI and reads back whether thinking arrived while the work was going, whether the answer streamed, whether a tool was named with its arguments, whether the edit really landed, and whether the tokens were on disk before the turn ended. Every one of those can be green in a suite and dead in the app.

## Talking to Gemini

Gemini runs on `gemini --acp`, the Agent Client Protocol server inside the same `gemini` binary everybody already has. Nothing extra is installed, the same way nothing extra is installed for Codex and Kimi: the server is a flag on the CLI, and `gemini --version` is the whole of what has to be there. It is the second CLI here on ACP, so the protocol half is shared rather than written twice.

- **`acp.ts` is that shared half, and the split is the transport against the reading.** The handshake, the client capabilities, the permission answers, the MCP servers, the cancel that carries a steer and the lane counting are the protocol's, and they are one file both providers read. What each CLI does differently is what it says over that protocol, so `kimi-acp.ts` and `gemini-acp.ts` are the parsers and nothing else. Written a second time instead, the two would have drifted the first time either vendor moved.
- **Both doors were opened and the loser is worth naming, because it is not the one Kimi's was.** `gemini -p --output-format stream-json` is a real streaming surface rather than the six lines Kimi's was: the answer streams token by token, a tool is named with its real arguments at the moment it is requested, results come back, and the end of the turn carries prompt and candidate tokens per model. Two things it has no channel for at all. There is no reasoning in it, and it is one shot, so there is nothing to steer. Those are the two the transport decides and they are the two worth having, which is the same call Codex and Kimi already made.
- What that costs is the token count, and it is the honest price rather than an oversight. ACP has a `usage_update` in its schema and Gemini emits it nowhere, so a run draws no tokens and no price. `MODEL_RATES` carries the rates, so the figure is right the day the CLI starts saying it, and until then a missing number beats a wrong one.
- **The kind is what names a tool, and the title is not.** `tool_call` carries no arguments at all, which is the one place this is thinner than Kimi. What it carries instead is `kind`, an ACP tool kind, and `title`, which is the tool's own narrated description: `notes.txt` for a read, the command itself for a shell. Read as a name the title is a different tool on every call and none of them is in the table a step is drawn from. Read as a kind it is already Crew's own words: `execute` is in `SHELL_TOOLS`, so a command gets its terminal card and keeps what it printed, `read` keeps a file out of the log the crew syncs, and `edit`, `search` and `fetch` land on the rows a Claude run already draws. `TOOLS` in `gemini-acp.ts` is that map and nothing was added to `toolActions.ts` for it. The title is the detail, which is what it really is.
- The diff is the tool's own before and after rather than the arguments it was called with, and that is the better half of the trade. It arrives on the tool's content as `{type: 'diff', path, oldText, newText}`, so what draws is what really landed on the file instead of what the model asked for. `editStrings` in `detail.ts` reads those two spellings beside the three it already read, which is what that helper is for.
- There is no plan channel, so no list is folded out of a run. The tickets board is Crew's own http and arrives for Gemini the way it arrives for everybody, which is the whole reason that harness lives here rather than in each CLI's own todo tool.
- What a run is set to is a flag rather than something said over the wire, so nothing stands between the session and the turn. The model is `--model` and approval is `--yolo`, where Kimi has to say both as config options once the session exists. That is why `acpDialog` takes its settings as a list that is allowed to be empty.
- The Model field offers Default and falls through to whatever the machine is configured with, the same rule Codex's thinking effort is held to. Never send Crew's own default in place of somebody's config.
- A machine that has never signed in is refused at `session/new`, in a few milliseconds, with an ordinary JSON-RPC error. That is before a turn exists, so no turn is coming to end the run, and the refusal ends it and says why rather than leaving an agent apparently working for the whole ten minute idle timeout. It is the same rule Kimi holds about an error before the session exists.
- `tests/gemini-acp.test.ts` holds the handshake, the lanes, the kinds, the diff off the content alone, a command keeping what it printed while a file read does not, the stop reasons, the refused session and the steer. `yarn gemini-check` is the only thing that can see the rest of it: it runs a real turn against the real CLI and reads back whether thinking arrived while the work was going, whether the answer streamed, whether a tool was named with a title and a kind, whether a real diff came through and whether the edit landed on disk. Every one of those can be green in a suite and dead in the app, and this one was written on a machine with no `gemini` on it at all, so the check is the first thing to run once there is one.

## Talking to a model on this computer

A provider called Local, so somebody can use a model running on their own machine instead of Claude, Codex, Kimi or Grok. Everything above `src/runner/providers` is untouched: threads, columns, ghost threads, mentions, plan mode, the review panel, the toolbox and the voice all read a provider as a name and a `Provider` object, and the memory, tickets, helpers and `/page` preambles are appended to every prompt by the runner before it reaches one, so all five arrive the moment the model has a shell tool. `/fallback` works both ways, which is the direct answer to instead of: a cloud agent that has run out can hand the thread to the model on somebody's own machine and the work carries on.

- **There is no agent CLI to spawn, so the harness is ours.** `ollama run` is a chat box: nothing to parse, no tools, no file editing. The loop, the tools and the rounds live in Crew, the way the tickets board does rather than reading each CLI's own todo list. That is the whole of the work, and it is why `local.ts` is written directly rather than through `makeCliProvider`.
- **It is Local rather than Ollama, and that is a bet about who to be tied to rather than a name.** The floor is OpenAI compatible chat completions with tools, which LM Studio, `llama-server` and vLLM all speak, so Ollama is the default and the one Install installs rather than the thing this is built on. Owning the engine underneath was considered and refused: an inference runtime is per GPU backend, it lands on every machine in every crew including everyone who never runs a local model, nothing here is ever rebuilt from source, and it would take away the models somebody has already pulled. The lesson the tickets board taught was not to own everything underneath you, it was not to stand a feature on one vendor's private surface. A GPU kernel is not a private surface.
- The picker says Ollama and the provider is still `local` underneath, which is two decisions rather than one changing its mind. What somebody installed, pulled a model with and would go and fix is Ollama, so that is the word on the row, the same rule the review panel holds about git's own words: where Crew has a hand on somebody else's tool, the tool's own name is the plain word. The key an agent is written down under never moves with it, or a rename nobody asked for orphans every agent already made.
- **The run sink is what made any of this possible without a second copy of the app's own reading.** `cli.ts` held the transport and the sink in one closure, and only the transport is about a child process. `run.ts` is the sink now, `makeSink(cwd, hooks)`: block ids, the thinking rail, the task ledger, the shell-output rule and what a turn cost. `cli.ts` keeps spawn, timers, dialog, kill and exit, and how a run ended stays with it, so `out.error` and `out.turnEnd` are read in the transport while every step goes to the sink. A provider that owns its own loop reads the same sink, which is what keeps the block ids and the token maths from existing twice and drifting.
- **The tool names are the app's own names, and that is the load bearing decision in the whole feature.** `fileChanges` in `detail.ts` builds the diff a step draws from `file_path` with `old_string` and `new_string`, and from `file_path` with `content` on a tool whose name matches write or create. `isShellTool` matches `bash`, which is what gives a command its terminal card and keeps a file read out of the log the crew syncs. `stepTodos` folds the list out of `todos` with `content` and `activeForm`. Speak Read, Write, Edit, Bash, Grep, Glob, LS and TodoWrite and every step, fold, diff, review row and ticket is the row a Claude run draws, with no renderer change anywhere. Invent a name and all of it has to be taught it.
- Relative paths are read from the project and absolute ones are followed as given, which is what every other CLI here already does. A tool that fails comes back as a sentence rather than a throw, because the model reads that text and tries another way, where a throw takes the run down.
- Grep and Glob read the project the way the Files pane does, which is git saying what it is made of, so `.gitignore` is honored for free. `listRepoFiles` moved to `src/shared/repoFiles.ts` for it, since the runner may not import from main.
- **How the models are known is decided by `fields()` being synchronous.** It is called to build the picker, so by then there is nothing left to wait on, which is why `kimiModels` is a file read and `codexModels` is a file read. An HTTP call cannot go there. So there are three sources and the async pass is `detect()`, which asks whoever is answering and leaves the list in the module for `fields()` to hand back. That is also why `capabilities()` in `crews.ts` awaits `detect()` before reading `fields()`: read together, a list warmed by detect lands a beat too late to be in the picker.
- What a running server says it has is the best answer there is, because it is the runtime saying what it will really serve. What is on disk is the fallback, the manifest folder under `~/.ollama/models`, so a first open draws a real list rather than an empty one while the address is still being asked. Neither is the note case.
- **The picker is shorter than `ollama list`, and that is the tools filter rather than a bug.** `/api/show` says what a model supports, and one that says outright it cannot call tools is left out, since a model that cannot call tools cannot be an agent here at all. One that says nothing stays in: a missing field is not a refusal. The calls go out in parallel in the same async pass and are cached against the digest `/api/tags` already returns.
- The maker asks again every time it opens rather than once per mount. A CLI's models change about never and a local one changes the moment somebody runs `ollama pull`, which they will very often be doing because the card just told them they had none.
- **Installed is not the whole of whether an agent would run, which is what `note` is for.** A runtime up with nothing pulled is neither installed nor not installed, and without the note it is an agent that is made, mentioned, and fails on its first word. It stands in the card and Create stands down while it does.
- **Thinking has to be asked for and cannot be asked for safely.** Ollama takes `think: true` and refuses the whole request on a model that has none, so the first turn simply never happens. It is asked once, the refusal is read, and that model is remembered as one that does not think. The OpenAI compatible runtimes are never asked at all and say it as `reasoning_content` or `reasoning` or not at all, so all three names are read.
- **The context window is the difference between working and looking broken.** A tool loop reads a file, gets a result back, reads another, and a small window is gone by the second one. So Context is a real field rather than a knob, and `fitWindow` trims as the conversation nears the ceiling: the brief and the first thing the person said stay, and what goes is the oldest work. It goes in whole rounds, because a tool result with no call in front of it is a conversation the server refuses outright. A run that quietly forgets what it was asked reads as a bad model and is usually a full window.
- Steering and stopping fall out of owning the loop rather than being built. A steer is a message put in front of the next call, so it lands in the tens of milliseconds between one round and the next, which is better than every CLI here manages. A stop aborts the stream and stands down between rounds with the work already done left where it is, which is what stopping means everywhere else in the app.
- No price and no usage, and both are right rather than missing. There is no row in `MODEL_RATES` for a local model and there should not be one, and there are no rate limits on your own machine. The default is already tokens on and price off, so the default screen is the same one everybody else gets.
- The server is started from `start()` and never from `detect()`. Starting somebody's server the moment Crew opens is a decision nobody made, and by the time a run begins they have made a Local agent on purpose. The address is asked again there too, since one that was up when the picker was drawn can be down by the time somebody says something.
- `tests/local-agent.test.ts` runs the whole provider against a real server on loopback streaming real shaped frames, and holds the thinking, the answer, the tokens, the diff off the arguments alone, a command keeping what it printed while a file read does not, the result going back to the model, a steer landing between rounds, a stop keeping the file that was already written, and the window trimming. `tests/local-tools.test.ts` runs the real tools against a real folder and `tests/local-models.test.ts` holds the three ways of listing and the tools filter.
- **`yarn local-check` is the only thing that can see the rest of it, and here it is closing a real gap rather than a theoretical one.** There was no Ollama on the machine any of this was written on, so the manifest path, the field names on `/api/tags` and the capability key on `/api/show` were read off the documentation. Every one of them degrades to an empty list rather than to a throw, which is the right way to be wrong, but a fake will happily hand back whatever shape it was written to hand back and every suite would be green while the picker was empty on a real machine.

## A server somebody writes down

The Server field is written in as well as picked from, so a Local agent can run on any OpenAI compatible server there is rather than only on one this machine found for itself. It is the same provider and the same loop underneath: what a written address changes is where the loop sends its rounds.

- **The address is the agent's and the key is the machine's, and that split is the whole of the feature.** An agent's settings ride in the pool the crew shares, so a key written into one is a key handed to everybody who ever clones, which is the rule the plugins already hold. The address goes in the settings the way a model name does, and the key is kept beside the app in `model-servers.json`, at `0600` because of what is in it, read at run time by the machine that runs the agent and never sent, never returned to the renderer, never in the log.
- **A written address keeps its path, and that is what makes it work at all.** An address like `llm.example.com:39862/v1` is a base that already names where the models are, so `serverUrl` keeps the path exactly as it was given and `openaiUrl` adds `/v1` only where there is none. Dropped, as the address the picker found was dropped, every route lands one segment off and a server that is up answers 404 to everything.
- The one address there is anything to start is Ollama's own on this machine, its own port or wherever `OLLAMA_HOST` says. Anywhere else is somebody else's to start, and `ollama serve` handed a foreign host would try to bind it here. A port on this machine that nobody pointed Ollama at is no better: what stands up there is a second Ollama nobody asked for, on a port that was silent for a reason. That rule is also what decides the words: "Start it and say that again" is only offered where there is really something here to start.
- A server that turns the key away is a server. Read as silence, the words say nothing answered there and send somebody to check an address that was right all along, so a 401 or a 403 comes back as the key being wanted or the key being turned away, and those two are different sentences because they are different mistakes.
- What runs on a port is only worth naming where the port is this machine's. A server somewhere else on 1234 is not LM Studio, it is whatever somebody stood up there, so a written one is called by its own host.
- It is added from the picker, since that is where somebody stands when they find their server is not in it, and the list ends on the way to one that is not there yet, the way the toolbox ends its own row on the slot that opens the builder. The same screen is where one is taken back out, or a mistyped address stands in the list forever.
- A written value is only kept because the field says it may be. `resolveSettings` drops a choice its options do not carry, which is right for a model that has left a CLI's config and wrong for an address, so `free` on the field is what says the writing is the point of it.
- `tests/model-servers.test.ts` holds the address rules, `tests/model-servers-store.test.ts` holds what the machine keeps, and `tests/model-server-run.test.ts` runs the provider against a real server on loopback that stands under a path of its own and turns away anything with no key on it: the route asked, the key carried, both refusals in their own words, and the address standing in the picker under its own host.
- `yarn local-check` is still the only thing that can see a real one. `CREW_LOCAL_URL` is the address to write down and `CREW_LOCAL_KEY` is what it wants, and the check writes the pair into a store of its own the way the app does, so what runs is the same reading, the same routes and the same key. A server nobody here can reach says so on its own line and the check carries on against whatever this machine is serving, since the point of the line is the address rather than the turn.

## What an agent did

A step in a thread says what happened in plain words, wearing a mark of its own.

- Thinking has to be asked for. From Opus 4.7 on, a model keeps its reasoning to itself unless the request says otherwise: the blocks still arrive, signed, with an empty string where the words would be, so a run appears to think at length and say nothing. The interactive CLI asks for the summary and a headless one does not, and crew is headless, which is why the thread was silent for so long. `--thinking-display summarized` in `claude.ts` is the whole of the fix. Never drop it.
- A thinking block with nothing in it is not a step. `cli.ts` waits for the first word before it opens one, and closes only what it opened, so a model that is not showing its reasoning reads as working rather than as an empty card. That guard is also what leaves the complete block at the end of a message free to stand in, on a CLI that only sends it that way.
- Claude and Kimi stream their reasoning word by word. Codex streams a heading a stretch: OpenAI encrypts the raw chain of thought and sends down a short summary of each piece of work as it starts it, which is the same thing its own app shows, so crew posts each one as it lands and that is the ceiling. Grok hands back whole blocks at the end of a turn.
- `toolActions.ts` is the one table that turns a tool name into a mark and a phrase: "Reading" while it runs, "Read" once it is done, "Read files" when a run of them is folded up. Every CLI is read through it, so `Read`, `read_file`, `ReadFile` and `view_file` all land on the same line, and an mcp tool is named after the tool rather than the plumbing around it. A name nobody has heard of is set in words rather than shown raw.
- The marks are drawn in `toolGlyphs.tsx`, outlined on the same 24 grid as the design glyphs. They are sized by eye rather than by their box, so a page, a magnifier and a sparkle carry the same weight in a row. Nothing wears a container or a badge, with one exception, and it earns it: `ShowGlyph` is a tile, because showing a page is the one step that puts something on the screen outside the thread, and a tile is what an app is drawn as everywhere else. It is `TILE` in `keylines.ts` rather than `SOLID`, since a tile carries a mark cut out of it rather than its whole box as ink: it stands in a column beside the thought's ring, which paints its stroke outside itself and arrives at 19, and a square reads larger than a circle at the same measure, so 17.5 is where the two sit level. The arrow is cut out rather than painted on, or it would be one fixed color over a surface that changes under it, and what comes through it is whatever the row is standing on, the hover wash included.
- A live step lights its mark white and pulses it. Only `RunStatus` spins, at the foot of the run, so a long thread is not a field of spinners.
- A thought is the one mark that moves on its own. `ThinkingMark.tsx` draws it on the same 24 grid as the rest: three filled dots with a wave travelling through them while the model is thinking, and when the thought lands they gather into the middle as a ring blooms out of them and the check strokes itself in, which leaves the mark standing as `DoneGlyph`, the same check in a circle used everywhere else. The word turns over with it, "Thinking" to "Thought". The dots, the radii and the check path live in `toolGlyphs.tsx`, so the still glyph and the moving one are the same drawing.
- The ring's growth is a CSS transition and the dots' exit is an animation, and it has to be that way round. A transition does not fire on the first render, so a thought that was finished before it was ever on screen is simply drawn done rather than landing again as the thread is scrolled. Taking an animation off an element drops its values where they stand instead of easing them, which is why what the wave holds cannot be handed to a transition: the landing is keyframed, and it starts on the value the wave rests at so there is no jump. Only a mark that was watched thinking wears `data-landing`, or every old row would land again on the way past.
- A thought is set in italic, and a blank line between two of them is a paragraph rather than an empty row: `StepRow` splits on it and spaces the paragraphs instead, so a long thought reads as prose rather than as blocks pushed apart.
- Three or more of the same tool in a row fold into one line that opens: a pair is not clutter and stays where it can be read. Every step in the group keeps its own row and its own diff.
- An edit opens into the file it touched: the path and the counts on a bar, then the diff, red where lines went and green where they arrived, with the words that actually changed picked out. `diffRows` and `codeLine.tsx` do that work for the browser panel too, so a diff reads the same wherever it is shown.
- A command opens into a terminal: what was run after a `$`, then what it printed under it on the same surface, each with a copy button of its own. Every CLI hands back the result of every tool, so `cli.ts` keeps it only for a command, because a file read or a search would pour the whole file into the log the crew syncs. The result arrives without the name it was called under, so the name the tool started with is what decides.
- What a command printed is cut down by `output.ts` before it is ever sent: the colors and cursor moves a terminal would have eaten are dropped, and a long run keeps its head and its tail and loses its middle, since a failure is at the end. The card scrolls inside its own height rather than counting off the lines it is not showing, because unlike a diff there is nowhere else to go and see the rest.
- What a step says it did is what it did, not the summary the model wrote of it. `detail.ts` reads `command` before `description`, so a shell step shows the command itself.
- The panel a step opens carries no rail or bracket. It is a card, or it is plain text, sitting under the row that opened it.
- A row is drawn again only when it reads differently, and that is what keeps a thread usable once it is long. A thread worked in all day is three thousand rows, and every one of them was drawn again on every letter typed into the composer: the draft is held in the store, so a keystroke re-renders the view that holds the rows. Worse, a run streaming rebuilds every item in the thread on every delta, so the whole of it was drawn again several times a second while an agent worked. `ThreadItems` is memoized on the list it is handed and each row is memoized on `sameItem`, so typing draws nothing in the thread and a step landing draws the row it landed in.
- Nothing in the thread may be handed an inline callback. `onReply` is a `useCallback` in `ThreadView`, or a new function every render undoes the memo on every message under it, and the same goes for anything else passed down to a row.
- `sameItem` in `thread.ts` names every field rather than walking the object, because a field left out of it is a row that quietly stops updating, which is the one way this can be wrong. What the store owns is compared by its reference, the files on a step and the attachments, mentions and reply on a message, since those objects are only replaced when they really change. What `buildThread` makes fresh each time is compared by what is in it: the pages a run showed, the helpers it sent out, and the reactions on a message. `tests/thread-item-same.test.ts` types its fixture as `Required<ThreadItem>`, so a field added to the interface fails to compile until it is added here too, and it changes each field in turn to hold every one of them. `tests/thread-render-probe.test.ts` is the other half, and it types five letters into a thread of two hundred steps and reads back the count of what was drawn again, which is nothing.
- A hook that merges the page read back out of the log into the window's own events holds the two halves apart. `useThreadRead` had one memo over both, and a step lands about once a frame, so every frame re-ran the merge and handed back a fresh events array: every memo under it, `eventsOfThread`, `buildThread`, `stepsOfThread` and the two walks below, rebuilt a thread that had not changed, and the whole column drew again around it. Split, a step landing moves the steps and leaves the log where it is.
- A selector handed to `useCrew` runs on every write the store takes, once for each component subscribed, because that is how zustand decides who has to draw again. So a selector that walks the log is that walk times the rows on screen times the frames in a run: one screenful of reply quotes and helper rows read 399,060 events for a single step landing. `eventIndex.ts` is the answer and the shape to copy, one pass over the array held against the array's own identity, so every row on screen shares it. A selector is a field read, and the walk behind it belongs in a `useMemo` or in that index. The count on the review badge is the one to remember, since `App` and the top bar both read it and both are always standing: it asked `threadWorking` about every thread and that question walks every thread, so the badge was the square of the crew's threads twice over on every write. `workingThreads` answers for all of them in one pass, and the badge and the tasks panel read it.
- Memoizing the rows is half of a keystroke, and what the view around them does on every render is the other half. `ThreadView` read the run it is showing and the last one that ended straight out of the thread's own events, unmemoized, and a keystroke re-renders it because the draft it reads lives in the store: two walks of the thread's whole event list a letter, growing with the thread. The live case is the worst of both, since `lastEnd` finds no end while a run is going and walks all of it, and a `find` going forward reaches the run that has just started only at the far end. Both are held in a `useMemo` now and `lastStart` searches backwards beside `lastEnd`, so a run is found where it really is. Anything read off a list on every render of that view is the same mistake wearing another name.
- A box that grows with what is typed in it reads its own height once, never twice. `useAutoResize` writes `height: auto`, reads `scrollHeight`, and writes the height back, and it used to read `scrollHeight` a second time to decide the overflow. A read after a style write is a synchronous layout, and the composer stands in the same column as the thread, so that second read was a second full layout of every row on screen for a number the first read already answers.
- **A box that measures itself inside a scroller holds that scroller still while it does.** For the length of the read the box is one row tall, the browser clamps the scroll to the shorter content it now has, and writing the height back does not write the scroll back, so what is left is a view that has walked up by whatever the box was worth. Nothing is ever painted in between, which is why this reads as the page moving on its own rather than as a box collapsing: a message being edited in the middle of a thread pulled the view up on the first keystroke and on every one after it. `useAutoResize` finds the scroller once, on the first measure, and puts the top back if the read moved it. A composer with no scroller over it gets nothing and pays nothing.
- The box a message is edited in is the message, so it is never capped and never scrolls. A cap is a box shorter than the words it stands in place of, which moves everything under it and puts the rest of somebody's own sentence behind a scrollbar in a thread that already scrolls. Uncapped, `useAutoResize` sets the overflow to hidden itself, so the bar down the side, which offered somewhere to go that was not there, is gone. Focus is taken with `preventScroll`, or opening the box carries the view to it, and the caret lands at the end of what is already written rather than in front of it.
- A thread draws its tail rather than every row it holds. A window holds far more than the five hundred events `EVENT_LIMIT` names, because a step is not counted against that limit and one `agent.start` standing inside the window drags every step of its run in behind it: at rest that is seven thousand events, and a thread worked in all day is thousands of rows of them. `useDrawnTail` is the whole of the rule. Four hundred items to start, another four hundred as somebody comes up on the top, and what arrives lands above where they are standing rather than under them, so the line being read does not move. It grows by whatever lands at the end, or a step arriving would push the oldest drawn row off the top under somebody who had just reached back for it, and a tail too short to fill the scroller reaches back on its own, since scrolling is the only other way it would ever be asked.
- Two things draw the whole of a thread, and both are somebody asking for the whole of it. A search is one, because the find bar walks the text nodes that are really there and a row that was never drawn is a row it cannot reach, and it stays drawn after the field is cleared rather than taking the rows away under whoever just found something in them. The way to the head of the thread is the other, and it draws first and lands instantly rather than travelling: a smooth scroll to the top of seven thousand rows that arrived above you mid-flight is a scroll that never seems to end.
- A thread the window has scrolled past is asked for on its own. What a window holds is the tail of the log, and a thread worked in a month ago has none of its own events left in it, while the rail still lists the row, the chat still holds the card and the plan still rides in the snapshot: opening one drew a header over an empty column. `thread.history` names one thread and the host hands back what that thread is drawn from, the events naming it and the two a helper raises on its behalf, the tail of them where a thread has been worked in all year. It goes to the one who asked, and a ghost is answered for the window that opened it and nobody else, or a hidden thread leaks to whoever guesses its id.
- What says a thread is worth asking for is its own beginning. A thread starts once, so a window holding that event holds everything the thread has done since, and one that does not is a row with nothing under it. Asked once per thread per connection, which is what keeps opening the thread that just ran free, and a welcome empties the record of what has been asked since the session has come back up.
- What comes back is kept beside the window's own events rather than folded into them. The chat feed is drawn from the window, so a thread read back would stand a card from a month ago at the head of today's, above the way back into the history that would have filled in around it. `readEvents` and `readSteps` are that slice, and the window's own copy wins wherever the two hold the same event, since the window trims and this does not. `useThreadRead` puts the two together, and asking is part of reading rather than a call anybody has to remember, so anything that shows a thread gets both halves by naming it: the column, a helper, a board's chat and the work beside a thread.
- Steps arriving live are held for a frame and land as one write, because every write walks the thread again. The host already throttles one step id to a message every eighty milliseconds, so a burst is several step ids and several runs at once rather than one step saying itself twice: four messages in a tick were four rebuilds of the whole thread. `stepBuffer.ts` holds them in the order they arrived and arms a frame and a sixteen millisecond timer together, whichever comes first. Anything that reads the steps exactly flushes before it reads, which is every event, and a rebuild drops what is waiting rather than letting a stale delta land behind it.

## Music

Something to put on while the crew works. It is a tab in the side panel called Music, opened from the panel's own New button, and what is playing is playing for everyone: one loop, one place in it, and anyone can press anything.

- Crew plays two things and nothing else. Its own tunes, which are numbers rather than files, and a track somebody in the crew added themselves. There is no service behind it and nothing to fetch from the internet.
- A tune is written out as rows of steps in `songs.ts`, read by `tunes.ts`, and struck through the same bubble the rest of the app's sounds are made of. A note is its name, a dot is a rest, a dash holds the one before it, an x is a drum. `voices.ts` is the whole kit: the low one, the tune, the spark, and three with the tuning taken out of them for time.
- A row shorter than the loop is laid down again inside it, so a drum is written once and a melody is written out. A note nobody can spell comes back as nothing and is dropped, which is silence rather than a wrong note, so `tests/music-player.test.ts` spells every token of every song.
- What is playing is never written down. It rides in the session snapshot and in `music.room`, the way a call does, so a morning of skipping tracks is not something the crew scrolls past later. A session coming back up has nothing on.
- The shelf is the other way round. A track somebody added lasts, so it goes in the log as `music.added`, is replayed on the way back up, and is left off the shelf if its file has gone. Both kinds are trimmed out of the snapshot's own event list, the way the toolbox is.
- The file itself is kept beside the session in `.crew/music` and served over http by the host, exactly as an attachment is. Everyone plays their own copy from `/music/<file>`: nothing streams from whoever added it, and there is no second copy of the audio in the log.
- A picture in a message is drawn by a tag, which takes it from anywhere. A track is read with `fetch`, and the answer to a fetch is not handed to the page unless the host says who may read it, so `MEDIA_HEADERS` in `src/server/routes.ts` says so. Without it a run from source, where the renderer is served on a port of its own, is refused the file and plays silence, while the same build installed reads it from `file://` and is fine. Only the two routes a member reads from carry it. The ones that write stay shut, or a page in somebody's browser could reach a board by guessing its name.
- A track that will not load says so, on the line under its name. Silence that looks exactly like music playing is the worst of both, and the fetch and the decode were both swallowed, so nothing anywhere said the file had never arrived.
- A playlist is a list somebody made out of what is already on the shelf, and the panel holds it on a tab of its own beside Songs. It keeps ids rather than tracks, so it is the order it was written in and nothing else, and a track that has left the shelf is read out of it rather than left as a row that plays nothing. Everyone sees every list and anyone can play one, and only whoever wrote it can write in it, which is the `by` on the list and `isMine`. A list lasts, so it goes in the log as `playlist.added`, `playlist.track`, `playlist.renamed` and `playlist.removed`, and those are trimmed out of the snapshot's own event list the way the shelf is.
- The tab is one list of every list there is, the app's own first, then yours, then everyone else's. Whose a list is rides on the row, beside the count, rather than in a heading over a section of them: sections spent a row each on saying a thing the rows themselves can say, and a crew of four turned a handful of lists into a page of titles.
- A list can be named again, by whoever wrote it. A name blanked is refused rather than taken, since `cleanPlaylistName` would hand back Untitled and that is the one way a rename loses something. Naming a new list and renaming an old one are the same question, so they are the same card: `PlaylistName` is it, and what is already there arrives selected.
- Whoever asks for a list names its id, in `playlistId` on `playlist.add`, so the window that made it opens it the moment it lands and a track can be filed into it in the same breath.
