The host takes the id it is handed only if it is a plain uuid nothing else answers to, and names the list itself otherwise, so an older host and a junk id both come back as a list rather than as nothing. `makePlaylist` waits for the list to really arrive before it hands the id back, since a list is not somewhere to stand until the host has written it down.
- Making a list goes all the way through. It is named on a card of the app's own, it is written down, and it opens on the list itself, so there is somewhere to be once it exists. Nothing is made by wandering off, and nothing typed is lost by it either. A new list asked for from a track's own menu takes that track with it, rather than putting the track down and sending you to another tab to write the list you were about to fill.
- A track put on from a list carries the list with it, in `playlistId` on the room, so Next and Back walk the list rather than falling back into the shelf after its first track. A list nobody has heard of plays the track and belongs to no list rather than being refused outright, and deleting the list that is playing leaves the track on.
- `MUSIC_SETS` is the app's own lists, made of its own tunes. A crew's lists are theirs and are written down as they are made, and these belong to nobody, so they sit in `src/shared/music.ts` rather than in the log, they are the same for every crew, and there is nothing about one for anyone to change. `by` is empty on them, which is the whole of what keeps the delete and the take out off the page: a list with no owner is never yours however you are named. `isMusicSet` is how the host knows to play from one, since a set is not in the lists it holds.
- Crew is the one set there is, and it is the whole of the slow tunes. They run sixteen bars rather than eight, two of them to a chord and none of them past seventy, so each is about a minute round rather than half of one, and the kit stays out of the way where there is one at all. Anything added there is written the same way, or the list is a minute of ambient followed by half a minute of something else.
- What is playing floats at the foot of the panel, and only while something is on. Nothing playing is nothing to say, so the tabs sit at the top of a list that has the whole panel, with no rule under them, and the bar arrives when a track does. It is glass with a margin round it and the list runs on underneath, so nothing is set in a solid grey on it: `barButton` and the words beside it take the foreground at an opacity. Volume goes with it, off the speaker on the bar, since there is nothing to be loud about when nothing is on.
- The bar is lit by the cover of whatever is playing, `meshOf` at a fifth of its strength behind the glass, so the foot of the panel carries the color of the track rather than a second grey. The list is given room to scroll past it and the fade under it is taller than the ordinary one: the rows have to be gone by the time they would show in the margin the bar floats in, or a row reappears under the bar a moment after passing behind it.
- A track plays on to the next one when it ends, and the host is what walks it: the clients read one clock, so a timer on the host moves the room the way a press of Next does, along the list it came from or along the shelf. Loop is the crew's setting rather than a window's, it rides in `music.room`, and it is the one thing that leaves a track to come round on its own. Nothing else about the end of a track is written down.
- A row has one menu, at the end of it, in a slot that is held whether or not the pointer is there. A button per action grew the row under the pointer and pushed the time along with it. The playlists are a screen inside that menu rather than a menu hanging off it: one card is one thing to aim at, and a list is picked by its cover and its name, the same row the Playlists tab holds. `ScreenSwap` is what carries one screen to the next, travelling the way you are going and taking the card's height with it.
- One screen of the panel giving way to the next is a movement rather than a cut, and it is the same `ScreenSwap` the row menu uses in a card, on `fill`. Songs and Playlists stand beside each other and a list stands inside them, so where a screen travels from falls out of the order they are named in: sideways between the two tabs, in and out of a list, and back out on its own if somebody deletes the list while you are reading it. The header goes with it, the tabs giving way to the way back. A card carries a change in its own height and a panel has none to give, so there the screen being left is held on for the length of the movement and watched out, quicker than the new one arrives, and the box it all happens in is clipped, since a screen on its way in stands past the edge of it while it travels. A panel is the size of the window and the few pixels that read as a movement in a card are no movement at all across one, so how far a screen travels is `--screen-travel` on the box it happens in.
- Every screen scrolls on its own rather than through one scroller the panel shares. That is what lets two of them stand one over the other while one gives way to the other, and it is what lets each keep its own place: coming out of a list puts the shelf back where you were reading it, and a list you have not opened before starts at the top of itself. The place is held by the panel, not by the screen, since the screen is taken down whenever you leave it.
- Searching is a mark until it is asked for. A field standing open across the top is the loudest thing on a page that is mostly covers, and most of the time nothing is being looked for. Leaving it empty puts it away and takes the search with it.
- Nobody is told a moment on somebody else's clock. The host says where in the loop it is right now, and each machine takes it from there on its own audio clock. A machine already within a third of a second of the crew leaves its music alone rather than jumping, or every message that changed nothing would be a hiccup in everyone's speakers.
- Volume is yours alone, and so is mute. Both are in `localStorage`, neither is ever sent, and the player keeps its place with everyone else's whatever you do to them.
- The controls belong to the people here. `music.set`, `music.off`, `music.loop`, `music.add` and `music.remove` are all refused from a runner, since an agent's machine is connected the whole time it is joined.
- The bars are the music's own loudness, band by band, low on the left and high on the right. Where it is playing they are read off an `AnalyserNode` hung before the volume, so turning it down where you sit does not stop them dancing. Where it is not, they are drawn from `levels.ts`, which works the same curve out of the notes themselves, so a machine with the sound off watches what everyone else watches. The analyser's window has to be wide: a narrow one measures in steps of a few hundred hertz and the lowest band is fifty across, which leaves the bass bar empty.
- Bars are written straight onto the elements in `Bars.tsx` rather than held in state. It runs every frame, and a render a frame would cost the whole panel.
- A cover is a photograph of a few petals held right up to the lens: a sky, a handful of shapes standing at different distances in front of it, and a light off one side. `coverSeed.ts` works the whole scene out from the track's id, so it is the same picture on everyone's screen and no two tracks look alike, and `coverArt.ts` photographs it in a fragment shader. A track somebody added takes a palette from `paletteFor`, so it has a cover too.
- It is shapes rather than a stack of gradients, and that is the whole difference between a picture and a tint. Gradients average, and the average of two colors is a third color, so a picture built out of them has no objects in it: every edge is both of the colors that meet there and neither one is in front. A petal hides what is behind it, carries its own color right up to its own edge, and is blurred by its own distance. All three of those are per pixel, which is why this is a shader and not CSS.
- The blur is the depth of field, and it belongs to the petal rather than to the picture. Every cover is spread end to end through its depth rather than rolled, so there is always something near the lens and something far from it, and they are laid down blurriest first. One blur over the finished picture is a soft photograph of a flat thing.
- Nothing in a palette is dark. `colors` is three petals, the light along their lit edges, and the sky they stand against, and the darkest place in a picture like this is a color in shade, never an ink. A near black anywhere in the five lands as a bruise in the corner of the tile, and since every palette used to carry one, every cover had the same bruise and they all read as the same picture in different colors.
- Nothing in the first three is a near white either, which is the same mistake at the other end. Those three are the things in the frame, and a thing has a color: pale enough and there is none left in it, so it stops being the light on a petal and becomes a hole cut in the picture, brighter than everything around it and made of nothing. Every palette carried one as its middle color, one of them a plain `#ffffff`, which is why a cover's pale shape read as belonging to a different picture. The fourth is the light itself and is meant to be near white.
- A petal is not one color. It is a curved surface, so it turns away from the light at both flanks and falls off toward each tip, and a normal drawn in the plane of the picture gives every part of it its own angle to the light. Lit, it goes toward the palette's light; turned away, toward the sky rather than toward an ink, because a leaf in its own shadow is still lit by the sky above it. Both are hung off the petal's own color so the middle of it stays what it was given. Flat, the same shape is a band of paint.
- A petal is thin at its rim and at both tips, so it lets go of what is behind it there rather than cutting it off. That is what takes the hardness off a near edge without blurring it: the outline stays where it is and stops being a wall. The thickness is measured against the petal's own width, never against its blur, or a far petal, which is a wide soft thing, never counts as thick anywhere and the softest object in the picture becomes the faintest.
- A lit edge never arrives whole. Let all the way through, the brightest part of a rim is the light itself and nothing of the petal, which draws a pale line around the shape and is the one thing that tells the eye an outline is there. The sky takes two scales of noise rather than one, for the same reason: one field over the whole frame is another ramp, and it is the quiet half of a cover that needs something to look at.
- Something in the frame is always a different color from the sky. A green leaf on a blue sky is what makes one of these read as a photograph; all five colors from one arc of the wheel is weather rather than a picture. The third petal is the one that carries it.
- The odd color out is always the furthest thing away, and the nearest petal is always the one that belongs. Given the nearest slot it is the hardest edge in the frame wearing the most unlike color, and it reads as a sticker however good the rest of it is. The colors are handed out furthest first and stretched across the depth with both ends pinned: cycled, a picture holding more petals than the palette holds colors comes back round to the odd one and hands it to the sharpest petal there is, and taken as a plain share of the way through, one holding fewer never reaches the quiet end at all.
- A thing further off is seen through more of whatever the sky is made of, so a petal takes on the sky's color as it goes back. That is what settles a leaf into a photograph instead of leaving it sitting on one, and it is why a far shape does not carry its color at full strength.
- A petal has to end inside the frame. Run long enough that its taper happens off the edge, what is left in the tile is a stripe of one width crossing corner to corner, which is the one shape that says at a glance that a picture was made rather than taken. One tip is enough, since a leaf that runs out of the picture at one end is what a photograph taken this close looks like.
- The length that does it is worked out rather than searched for: the frame is a box and the spine is a line, so the two places the line crosses the box are the whole answer, read the longer way along itself. The bend is the half that cannot be solved in one line, because it carries the tip sideways the whole way out and by more the further out it goes, so a petal with plenty of bend leaves the picture long before its own length says it should. It is walked down instead, and that ends because straightening one always brings it back to the length that was solved for. Shrinking the length instead is what does not end: both tips draw in toward the point the spine passes through, and on a spine passing outside the frame there is nothing there to draw in to. Every spine is held inside the picture for the same reason.
- The cover on the panel breathes while its track plays, and a picture that moves has to be bigger than the box it moves in. It is laid in past all four edges and the drift stays inside that margin. A turn is the dearest thing it can spend: a square turned four degrees has to be scaled by 1.067 before it covers its own box again, so a small rotation and a scale that never drops under one is what it is, and the tile showed the panel behind it when the two were not weighed against each other. Said as a size and a corner rather than as four insets, since a canvas is a replaced element and takes its own pixel size wherever its width is left to itself.
- Noise is drawn once, above the loop, never inside it. A driver unrolls a loop that reads a uniform by its counter, so one noise call written inside is really one per petal, and on a Mac that is enough to take the Metal shader compiler down with it: the whole panel comes back as empty tiles, and the only thing that says why is the console. The loop is handed three fields, coarse to fine, and mixes them.
- The shader counts the same number of petals every time, because a card cannot be handed a shorter loop, so an empty slot still runs. Fill one with numbers that mean something rather than leaving it at nought: a petal blurred by nothing asks the card to fade an edge from a point to the same point, which has no answer, and what comes back is not a number and spreads through everything it touches.
- `yarn covers` draws every cover on one page. A generator like this cannot be judged from its numbers, and `tests/music-cover.test.ts` only holds the rules above, never the look.

## Games

Something to play while you wait. It is a tab in the side panel called Games, opened from the panel's own New button, and it holds Tetris and Flappy Bird with room for more. What lasts is the score.

- `GAMES` in `src/shared/games.ts` is the whole list of what there is to play, so the panel, the leaderboard and the host all read the same table and a new game is a row in it plus the two files that draw and play it.
- A game is numbers. `tetris.ts` and `flappy.ts` hold the whole of the rules and neither one draws anything or listens for anything: a move is a function from one game to the next, which is what lets `tests/games-play.test.ts` play both of them through without a canvas anywhere near it. Anything that has to be shuffled or has to fall takes its randomness and its step as arguments, so a test steps them exactly.
- The drawing is beside the rules rather than inside them. `paint.ts` is the kit both games are painted with, the colors, the block, the pipe and the bird, and `drawTetris.ts` and `drawFlappy.ts` are each game's own picture. The row a game is opened from is painted with the same kit, so a row is a still of the game rather than an icon of one.
- A game is a picture, so its colors are pinned the way the design canvas pins its palette. The field is the same deep blue in a light window as in a dark one, and everything drawn over it is set in white at an opacity rather than in the theme's own foreground, which would be black text on a dark field in a light window.
- Every game stands on a field of one shape, `FIELD_RATIO`, whatever shape its own world is. It is the shape of the room the panel leaves, so the side panel is filled rather than nearly filled, and one game does not resize the panel's furniture on the way to another. A game whose world is another shape is centered in it and sinks its own well into the field, which is what Tetris does: a board left to be guessed from where the grid happens to stop is not a board.
- The field keeps its own shape whatever the panel is doing. `Field` measures the box it is given and works the game's size out from that ratio, because a canvas told to fill a box it does not have the shape of comes out stretched and the games are drawn in world units.
- The field is nothing on its first render and its real size a beat later, and a game that is not running has no loop to paint it again. `Field` says when its size changed and the game repaints, or the board keeps the one pixel it was first drawn at and is stretched over the whole field.
- The field takes focus as it arrives and eats the keys it is played with, or the arrows scroll the panel out from under whoever is playing.
- Nothing stands beside the field. The score for the round rides on the panel's own header line, beside the name of the game, and the board of high scores rides on the overlay that is over the field between games. Under the field, the board changes the size of the field the moment a game starts, and a table of scores beside a game being played is something to read instead of the game.
- A frame is worth what it really took, capped at a twentieth of a second. A window left in the background hands back one enormous step when it comes round again, and a bird moved by a second of gravity in one go is already in the ground before anyone can press anything.
- The leaderboard is one row per person per game, their best. A score that beats nothing is a round somebody played rather than something the crew keeps, so the host only writes one down when it beats that person's own, and `game.score` is trimmed out of the snapshot's event list the way the shelf is: a high score is a row on a board, not a moment to scroll past in the chat.
- Reading the log back keeps the last score for each person rather than the highest, because they went in in the order they were played and each one already beat the one before it.
- The board is the crew's own, so `game.score` is refused from a runner the way the music controls are. An agent's machine is connected the whole time it is joined.

## Attachments

Anything the crew has to hand goes on a message: a picture, a pdf, a log, a spreadsheet, a zip, a track, a clip. `src/shared/attachments.ts` is the one table of what a file is, and both sides of the wire read it.

- A picture is kept under the type it says it is, since that is the one thing every browser reports the same way twice. Everything else is kept under the extension it arrived with, because the type a browser reports for a csv is a guess and for plenty of files it reports nothing at all. The type written down is the type it will be served as, so what the app reads off the record is what really comes back off the wire, and a file with no extension to go on is handed over as a download rather than guessed at.
- Nothing a browser would run is ever served as itself. A page and a vector are markup, and an attachment is served from the host's own address, so one carrying a script would be reading the session from inside it. Both go over as text, which is still there to read with nothing left to run.
- Both are still drawn, and neither is drawn where it is served. A page is read out of the attachment and stood up in the app's own folder through the same `Previews` a file in the project is previewed with, so what somebody sent is a page in a window rather than a script inside the session, and it is handed no folder to read from since there is no file behind it and nothing beside it to reach. A vector is drawn as the picture it is, in an image tag, which runs nothing and reaches nothing. `markupOf` in `src/shared/attachments.ts` is what says which of the two a name is, because the type on both says text on purpose and the name is all that is left.
- A file written to be looked at is offered both ways, the thing it is and the words it is written in, the way a file in the project is. It opens on the page or the picture, since that is what it was written to be, and the switch stands in the file's own header wearing the mark of what it is when it is not text, so the mark holds still while the two are swapped and lights while that is the way it is being read. `bothWays` is which files those are, markdown included, and `tab.preview` is which way it is being read, the same flag a file in the project carries.
- A face is a picture and nothing else. `savePhoto` is the one way one is set, and the window that sends it already turns everything else away: the host asks again, because taking a window's word for it is what put a pdf on somebody's avatar the first time this was widened.
- The name somebody uploaded is a label and never a path. What is written down is a uuid and an extension, `isAttachmentFile` is the whole of what may be read or written under `.crew/attachments`, and the runner asks it again about a name that arrived over the wire, since that side is handed a name rather than making one.
- What an agent gets is a path per file and a line saying which of them are pictures. It reads them off the disk itself, so a log or a csv is work it can do rather than something described to it. A thread's own transcript says `[image: name]` or `[file: name]`, so a later turn knows which it was.
- Six to a message, the same for a picture and a file. How big each may be is the crew's own, on the Files page in the settings, and it starts at 10MB. Everything lands in the folder the crew syncs, so a big one is a big one in the history too.
- That number is one number for everyone, because the host is what turns a big one away: a window that raised its own would watch the host refuse the file anyway. It is written down as `attachment.limit` and folded off the log the way the toolbox is, so it rides in the snapshot, replays for free, and is refused from a runner the way the music controls are. `cleanAttachmentMb` is the whole of what may be asked for, and a junk number leaves the one standing where it is rather than being taken. Both ways in ask the host's own limit before they keep a byte, the http upload and a message's attachments, and nothing anywhere reads a constant of its own.
- A file left behind for being too big says so where it was dropped. It went quietly before, which reads as a drop that did nothing.
- An image draws where it stands. A file is a row that says what it is, what it is called and how big it is, and pressing it opens it in the side panel where the app can show it and hands it to the machine where it cannot.
- The preview of a picture waiting to be sent is built once and kept. It is the whole file written out as a data URL, so a ten megabyte picture is thirteen megabytes of string, and it was built again on every render of the composer, which is every letter typed while one is attached: two milliseconds a keystroke. `previewSrc` keeps it against the attachment's own id and `keepPreviews` lets go when the attachment goes. Never hand back an object URL in its place: `copyImage` in main reads a `data:` and an `http` address and answers false to anything else without saying so, so a blob would quietly take Copy image off a picture nobody has sent yet.

## GIFs

The plus button in the composer opens a menu, upload a file or pick a GIF, and picking one sends it straight out. `AddMenu.tsx` is the menu and `GifPicker.tsx` is a screen inside the same popover rather than a popover of its own.

- Klipy is the only service left to build this on. Google closed the Tenor API to everyone in June 2026 and Giphy stopped being free, so there is one choice and `gifs.ts` reads it.
- The key is a client key rather than a secret, and it sits in `CREW_KEY` in `gifs.ts` so it rides in the repo the crew already shares. That is what gets everyone who joined GIFs without being handed anything of their own, the same way they get the toolbox and the songs. `VITE_KLIPY_KEY` overrides it for a machine that would rather keep it out of the project.
- The grid takes the small copy and the message takes the large one, and both fall back down the sizes rather than coming back empty, since a GIF the service only holds one copy of is still a GIF.
- The message carries the picture itself rather than a link to it, the way every other image in the chat does, so it is still there when the service is not. The CDN answers a cross origin fetch, which is the whole of what makes that possible.
- A GIF is attached and sent in the same breath, so every send guard reads the attachment count live rather than off the last render. Read from state it is a render behind, and the send is refused for having nothing in it.
- A GIF that fails to send leaves the grid where it is. Only the send failed, so it is said on the tile that failed rather than in a line under the grid: the picture keeps its place, wears a ring in `danger` and the warning mark over it, and is cleared the moment another is picked or the search moves on. A message under the grid named nothing and cost a row of the panel to say a thing that is almost never true.
- The picker is a search and a grid, and nothing else. The search is a flush row with a hairline under it, the way the Actions palette is built, rather than a pill floating in its own padding, which is the shape a search takes in a panel. A popover whose whole content is the result of that search says so with the row rather than with a box drawn around it.
- There is no way back to the menu, because there is nothing to come back for. The two rows it was opened from are one press away again, and a chevron in front of the field is a control that spends the width of the search on going somewhere nobody goes.
- The picker clips to the corners the popover is wearing, `overflow-hidden rounded-[inherit]` on its own box. With a bar under the grid the tiles never reached the bottom of the panel; scrolling under a square corner is what showed once the bar came off.
- `SearchField` is that row and it is the app's search field, wherever one is wanted. It holds the magnifier, the field and a way to clear what was typed. It takes focus when it mounts, since it is the field of a screen that just opened. Seven other places still draw one of these by hand.
- A popover stands in the body, and its clicks still bubble through the React tree to whatever rendered it, which for anything in the composer is a shell that focuses the message box on every click it sees. Typing in the search field was thrown back to the composer by it. `clickToFocus` is that handler everywhere it appears: a click is the box's own only if it landed inside the box, and one that came out of a popover did not.
- The grid's padding and the gap between tiles are the same number, so the gutter reads evenly all round rather than as a frame of one width holding a grid of another.
- The scrollbar is off. A grid two columns wide is narrow enough that ten points of it comes out of the tiles, and the bar sits over the pictures at the one edge where they are already tight.
- Nothing is drawn around the outside of a tile. A GIF is a picture with any color at its edge, so what marks the one under the pointer is an `InsetRing` in white, and it turns up hard enough to be seen over a photograph. Growing the tile instead is what it was, and a tile that grows in a masonry column grows underneath the one after it.

## Emoji

Two things, and they belong to different people. The row of reactions that opens over a message is yours, and the emoji the crew drew themselves are everyone's. Both are set from the settings, on the Reactions page under You and the Emoji page under Crew, which is the rail saying whose each one is without a word anywhere on either page saying so.

- Your row is kept where you sit, in `state/quickReactions.ts`, and nothing about it is ever sent. Ten is the cap, because a row of one-tap reactions past ten is the picker with extra steps and the tray is as wide as what it holds. None is a real answer, so it is written down as one: nought and the default are the same absence otherwise, and the four it ships with would come back the moment somebody cleared the last one.
- A row of none still has a tray. The divider only stands while there is a reaction in front of it, or a rule with nothing on one side of it is a line at the edge of the tray, and the way in is the picker that was always there.
- A row of ten is wider than a message in the side panel, so the tray wraps and nothing in it gives up its own width for that: a button squashed to fit is a smaller thing to aim at than the one beside it. Measured in a real window, the old classes put a row of ten 388 across in a 280 message and hung it 108 past the left edge, because a nowrap row keeps its buttons at their own size and simply overflows.
- Wrapped, it is pinned by its bottom edge and grows upward, the way the ask bar on a board grows away from what it is asking about and the Scribe pill keeps its bottom edge when it gains a line. Anchored at the top, a second line reaches down over the message the tray is about, which takes away the one thing on screen it refers to.
- Holding shift turns the More button into a delete button, so taking a message back is one press instead of a menu and a row inside it. It is the same slot rather than a button beside it: the tray is as wide as what it holds and a row that grows a button under the pointer moves everything left of it, so the press somebody was already aiming at lands somewhere else. The menu keeps its own Delete, since holding a key is a thing a pointer does and the menu is the way there without one. It says More rather than Message actions: the old words named the row of buttons it was already standing in rather than saying what pressing it does.
- The swap stands down while the menu is open. A popover hangs off its button's own box and reads it every frame, so a button that goes to nothing under it leaves the menu standing over the message with nothing to point at.
- Held shift is an attribute on the root rather than state, `data-shift` written by `state/shift.ts` and read by the `shift:` variant in `styles.css`. Every message on screen draws a tray of its own, so held in React a capital letter typed into the composer would redraw every row in the thread twice, which is the one thing the thread is written to never do. Nothing re-renders for it at all. A window that loses focus mid-hold never hears the key come up, so a blur lets go of it, or it comes back with shift held by nobody.
- The picker opens on what you actually use, so `emojiRecents.ts` seeds itself from your own row rather than from the four in `reactions.ts`.
- An emoji the crew added is a picture with a name, written `:name:` wherever an emoji goes: in a message, as a reaction, on your own row, and in the picker for everyone. `shared/customEmoji.ts` is the whole of what one may be. GIFs are the point of half of them, so a GIF is first among the types rather than the exception, and one is drawn as it is rather than through anything that would take a still of it.
- The picture is kept beside the session in `.crew/emoji` and served over the host's own http with `MEDIA_HEADERS`, exactly as a track is, so everyone draws their own copy and nothing streams from whoever added it. Only the two routes a member reads from carry those headers, and this is now the third.
- It lasts, so it goes in the log as `emoji.added`, `emoji.renamed` and `emoji.removed`, and those are folded off the snapshot's own event list the way the shelf is: an emoji is a picture in the picker rather than a moment to scroll past. One whose file has gone is left out on the way back up, since a name that draws nothing is worse than no name.
- A name means one thing for the whole crew, so a name already answering to something is refused rather than shadowed. It is said rather than dropped, because whoever asked is holding a picture and a field, and it is said twice: the window checks before it sends and the host checks again, since taking a window's word for it is how the wrong thing gets written down.
- What somebody typed is taken rather than refused. The case, the colons they wrapped it in, the spaces and dots in the middle are all read as the name underneath, and only a name with nothing left in it once the characters that cannot be in one are gone is nothing. `cleanCustomEmojiName` is that rule and it is the only place a name is decided.
- `isCustomEmojiFile` is what keeps a name off the disk, and the store asks it again on the way in and on the way out. The name written down is a uuid and an extension, so there is nothing in it to walk out of the folder with.
- A reaction may be one off the sheet or one of the crew's own, and a `:name:` nothing here answers to is refused. That is the one rule that means a reaction can never stand for a picture that was never there.
- Naming one again keeps the picture where it is, and what was already reacted with under the old name still says the old name. That is the honest record of what somebody pressed, and rewriting the log to match a rename would be the app deciding it knows better.
- A megabyte is its own number rather than the crew's file limit. That one is about what people send each other; this one is a folder of pictures every machine pulls, drawn at 22 across.
- They are the crew's, so `emoji.add`, `emoji.rename` and `emoji.remove` are refused from a runner, the way the music controls and the leaderboard are. An agent's machine is connected the whole time it is joined.
- The sheet is looked up rather than handed down. `customEmojiSheet.ts` holds the crew's own and the address to read them from, fed by the store off the snapshot and off every `emoji.set`, because a picture drawn from a name has to be reachable from a DOM walk over rendered markdown and from a component a dozen callers deep. Threading the list and the address through every one of them to draw one picture is the plumbing that file exists instead of, and it is the same shape `emojiData.ts` already has for the sheet the app ships with.
- That sheet says when it has changed, and it has to. What draws a name is memoized on the words, and adding an emoji changes neither the words nor any slice a message subscribes to, so a name already on screen would have stayed the four characters it was written as until the message it sits in was unmounted. `useCustomEmoji` is that one hook, taken in `Emoji`, in `EmojiText` and in `Markdown`, which are the three funnels every drawn emoji comes through. It only says so when the list or the address really moved, since the store says it on every change it makes.
- A name the crew does not have is left as the words it was written in. `:shipit:` in a sentence is a picture where the crew has one and four characters of text where they do not, which is also what a reaction to a picture somebody has since removed reads as.
- A name the crew does have beats the sheet's own shortcode for it. Somebody who called their picture `tada` had it eaten on the way in and turned into 🎉, because the composer swaps a closed `:shortname:` for a character as it is typed, so that swap asks the crew's own first. The host cannot settle this, since the sheet lives in the renderer and it is the one place that knows the name is taken, which is why the rule is a lookup in front of the swap rather than a name refused at the door.
- Somebody's own words are drawn as pictures wherever they are read back, and not only in the message they were written in. A line cut down to a row is still the line they wrote, so the thread's own header, the row in the rail and the two lists in the Tasks panel all go through `EmojiText` the way a reply quote does. Left as text, one heart is the system's own glyph in the header and the app's picture an inch below it in the thread, which reads as two different apps on one screen.
- `quiet` is what holds the name back on one of those, and it is for the rows that already open something of their own. `EmojiText` wears a `Tooltip` per picture so somebody can learn what to type, and the header's ask line exists to open a hover card, so hovering the one emoji in it opened a name over the card it had just raised. It is the same rule as a tooltip standing down for the popover its button opens, and it is said on the caller rather than worked out here, since the picture never knows what it is standing in.

## The side panel

Everything the app can stand beside the chat: a web page, a terminal, the project files, what has changed since the last commit, the music, the games, and the thread's own plan, board and helpers. `SidePanel` holds it and `BrowserPanel` draws it.

- It is a place rather than a side effect of a tab existing. `open` on the browser store is the whole of whether it is standing, so it can be open on nothing, and putting it away keeps what is in it rather than throwing three tabs away for one press. Read off `tabs.length` it could not do either.
- Putting something in it opens it, and that rule is written once, in the `set` the store's actions are given. It counts what arrived rather than what is there, or a page that finished loading behind a panel somebody had just put away would bring it back on its own.
- The last tab out takes the panel with it. That is `settle`, and it covers a tab that goes because you moved, a plan that came with a thread you have left, and every tab closed by hand: nothing left in it is nothing to stand on. Opening it again on nothing is what Start is for, so closing the last thing never loses the way in.
- It is put away by dragging its own edge, and dragging is the whole gesture rather than a shortcut for a button. The edge stops at `MIN_WIDTH`, and carried on past `MINIMIZE_AT` the panel goes altogether, back the other way it stands up again, so putting it away and bringing it back is one drag that can be turned around mid-flight. Nothing snaps back at the end: where the pointer was when it was let go is what stands. The floor and the threshold are 120 apart on purpose, since an accidental collapse costs somebody the row of tabs they were reading and there is no gesture to undo it.
- What is put away is minimized rather than closed. The tabs are all still there, which is what `closePanel` already meant, and the only thing that really goes is the room it was taking.
- `PanelToggle` is the way back, and it stands while the panel is away and there is something to come back to: what is already in it, or what the thread you are in has of its own. It wears `PanelRightGlyph`, it is the last thing in the top bar, past your own face, so it stands at the end of the row hard against the edge the panel comes in from. Pressing it puts the panel back and takes the button off the row in the same moment: there is one button here, and it is the one for the state you are not in.
- A plan, a board and the helpers a thread sent out are that second half, and the button wears a `Badge` with no number in it while there are any. No count, because the question it answers is whether the thread has anything in there rather than how much, and a three is a figure nobody acts on. `Badge` handed nothing to say is the same disc a count stands in with a smaller circle of the surface in the middle of it, so a badge saying there is something and a badge saying how many are one object at one weight rather than a badge beside a speck. It reads the thread rows of `PANEL_OPENS`, so what the badge stands for, what the plus menu offers and what Start lists are one table.
- Pressing it asks for all of them, so a panel opened on a thread that has been working lands on the plan with the board and the helpers beside it rather than on Start. They go in backwards, which is what leaves the first row of the table standing.
- A plan and a board come with the thread rather than being put in the panel, so neither one stands it up. They arrive in the row and wait, and the panel is where it was left, which is what makes a plan written mid-run something to go and read rather than something that takes half the screen while you are reading the thread. Everything else opens the panel as it lands, and the presses that really are presses open it themselves: the row in the menu is already inside it, and the alert's own Open says so on the line after it.
- Nothing stands there while the panel is up, and that is the point rather than an omission. The way to put it away is the edge and the close in the panel's own corner, both of which are already on the screen, and a third control in the top bar saying the same thing is a row of chrome standing there all day for a press somebody makes twice.
- It stands beside whatever page is up. A build running in a terminal, the file somebody is reading and what has changed since the last commit are all things somebody is in the middle of, and a panel that goes with the page is that work taken off the screen for pressing Docs. Nothing about the panel is the chat's: it is a column of this machine's own things standing beside the app, so the page it stands beside is whichever one is up. The way back is in the top bar for the same reason, on every page rather than on one.
- Design has a right panel of its own wearing the same `PanelRightGlyph`, so the two are told apart by where they stand rather than by the mark: the board's is in the middle of the bar beside the board's own name, and the app's is at the end of the row past your own face. Never put a second control for either one anywhere near the other.
- A thread in a window of its own has no top bar, so the same button stands in that window's own drag band, at the right of it.
- It was one button per thing the panel could hold, the plan, the board and the helpers, and then one in the header of whichever thread was focused. Each of those was a control that came and went with what was under it, and none of them was on the screen from the chat with no thread open, which is where somebody who had closed the panel was left with no way back into it at all.
- `PANEL_OPENS` in `panelOpens.tsx` is the one table of what it can hold. The menu the plus opens and the Start tab both read it, so the words somebody picked from are the words they are shown either way, and there is one place to add to.
- The thread's own things stand first and come and go with the thread you are in. The rest never move, so nothing under the pointer travels as you go between threads. `scope` on a row is which of the two it is, and the menu draws its one divider off that pair rather than off the order alone.
- Start is that table as a list, and nothing else. A line over it naming what the rows are would be the screen read back to whoever is already looking at it, and no row promises a key, because nothing in the app listens for one. It wears a tab of its own, so what is on screen is always a tab, and it has no close: closing it is closing the panel, and that button is an inch to the right of it.
- The close in its own corner puts the panel away and keeps the tabs, the same as the button in the thread.
- A tab wears the site's own mark, and a mark that never arrives is the globe. A page announcing a favicon is a page saying where one should be rather than one that is there, so an address that answers with nothing, or with bytes no picture can be read out of, left the row carrying the browser's own broken image: a torn page where every other tab has a mark, and the one kind of hole a row of pills cannot absorb. `Favicon` draws it and stands the globe in on the first error, which is the mark a tab with nothing announced already wears, so a site with no icon and a site whose icon will not load read the same. It is keyed on the address, so the state belongs to the picture rather than to the tab: a page that broke and then announced another draws the new one, where a flag held across the change would leave the globe standing on a mark that loads perfectly well.

## Several threads at once

The chat holds up to ten threads side by side, as columns, and any one of them can be stood out into a window of its own. `openThreadIds` on the store is the row, left to right, and `openThreadId` is the one the caret is in.

- Opening a thread never closes one. It joins the row, or it is the column it already stands in, and a row with no room left says the move rather than dropping whichever column somebody was reading. That is one rule wherever a thread is opened from, a card, the rail, a task, a toast or a fork, so the way in to a second column is opening a second thread and there is no gesture to learn.
- `src/shared/threadViews.ts` holds the whole of it as numbers, the limit, what joining a row does, what closing one leaves, and where the caret goes afterwards, so `tests/thread-views.test.ts` reads the rules with no window anywhere near it and `tests/thread-columns-probe.test.ts` reads the columns in a real render.
- The way out of a column is the button that was the way back to the chat. One thread open is a way back and several is a close, so nothing was added to the row to say the same thing twice, and the chat is what is left once the last column goes.
- A right click on a thread says where it opens. `threadMenuActions` is that rule and it answers off the row rather than off the thread: nothing open is Open, something open is Open beside, one already in the row is a window of its own or Close, and a full row is left with the window alone, since a row that says Open beside and then refuses is a press that does nothing. A window of its own is always there, which is what makes it the way to read an eleventh thread.
- The thread itself is one of the things that can be right clicked, and that is where a window of its own is asked for now. It was a button in the header, standing beside the way out and the way to the panel in every column at once, for the one press somebody makes on a thread a day. The menu already said the same thing in words, and a right click is what says it everywhere else a thread is listed, so the row is two buttons rather than three and the gesture is the one already learned. A window standing on one thread says nothing of its own, since it is already the answer, which is what `alone` decides.
- What already answers a right click keeps it. Nothing is offered over a link, a picture, a field or anything editable, and nothing is offered over a selection, because that is where Copy lives and the words are the reason somebody is there. `ownsMenu` is that rule and `selecting()` is the other half, and left out the app would hand back its own menu in place of the one with Copy on it.
- The row ends on the way to another, the way the toolbox's own row ends on the slot that opens the builder. The chat mark stands at the right edge and pressing it stands the chat itself in the row as the last column, lit while it is there and pressing it again is what puts it away.
- It only stands while the pointer is near the edge it hangs off, `nearRight` and `NEAR_RIGHT`. A control for a move somebody makes now and then, standing in every thread all day, is a strip of the screen spent on nothing, and it was a column of its own before this: 44 points of empty gutter down the side of the work. Near the edge it arrives over the far right of the thread rather than beside it, which is room nobody has to pay for the rest of the time, and it comes from the edge rather than growing, the way the rail does. It is glass, so nothing on it is set in a solid grey. Without it the only list of every thread there is was the chat, and the chat is exactly what the columns take off the screen, so the first column closed the door behind it: the rail was the whole way to a second one, it holds a handful of live threads and none of the finished ones, and from the chat there was one thread anybody could ever open. It reads as opening a thread the ordinary way, which is what it was.
- The chat in the row is the chat, composer and all, and a card in it opens beside rather than in place, which is the same rule everywhere else. It goes with the last column, since the chat standing beside nothing is the chat, and the slot goes when the row is full, since there is nowhere left to put what it opens.
- What is shared is the menu rather than the row, the way the one in Files is: `ThreadOpenItems` is the rows, for a card that already has a menu of its own to stand them in, and `useThreadMenu` is the whole card, for a row that had none. The doing stays in the menu and only the opening is handed in, since a thread in the rail is opened by going to its project and a thread in the feed is opened where it stands.
- A column never goes narrower than `COLUMN_MIN`. It holds a composer, a diff and a terminal card, so past that the row scrolls sideways rather than squeezing them: a column too narrow to read is a column nobody opened on purpose.
- There is one side panel, so no column holds a control for it. The way back into a panel that has been put away stood in the focused column once, which kept a row of them from being several buttons for one box, and a control that moves from column to column as you click about is still a control that moves. It is in the top bar now, where it never moves and never has to say which column it means.
- The same goes for `⌘F`. Every column draws a find bar of its own, so the shortcut belongs to the column you are writing in, which is `listens` on `FindBar`. A bar already open still answers it, or the way to close the one in front of you would depend on which column was last touched.
- The panel's own thread tabs are read against the whole row rather than the one thread, which is `leaveThreads`. Read the old way, opening a second column took the first one's helpers off the screen.
- What is already on the screen is not said again, and with a row of them that is every thread in it. `finishedAlert`, `memberMentionAlert` and `memberReplyAlert` take the row, or a thread being read in the second column raises a banner about itself.
- A thread in a window of its own is the same session seen through one thread. The window is opened by main, looking at the crew the window that asked was looking at, and the thread rides in the hash so it survives a reload: `threadWindowHash` writes it and `main.tsx` is where a window with one in it becomes `ThreadWindow` rather than the app. It boots the way the app does and joins nothing extra, since two windows on one folder are already one member.
- It pins the store's row to its own thread, which is what makes everything downstream true there: a page an agent shows opens in that window, the panel holds that thread's things, and nothing raises a banner about the thread being read.
- Popping out takes the column with it. Standing in both places at once is two composers on one thread and nobody asked for the thread twice.
- A thread of another project pops out too, and that is the one way a thread from somewhere else is read without leaving where you are. The window is opened on the crew the thread belongs to rather than on the crew that asked, which is `popOutTarget` in `src/shared/popOut.ts`: no key named is the crew the asking window is in, a key named is taken only if that crew is running, and nothing opens otherwise rather than a window landing on the way in with a thread named in its URL. The rail only ever lists the threads of crews that are running, so the refusal is a guard rather than a path anything takes. `popped` is keyed by the crew and the thread together, or two projects could hold one window between them.
- A column cannot do the same, and the reason is the store rather than the layout. `useCrew` is one session whole, built on one socket, and `BLANK` is that whole session wiped in one go when a window changes crew: a row of columns from two projects is two live stores in one window, which means the toolbox, the design channel, the pending map, the helper prefs and the custom emoji sheet all stop being able to answer which crew they mean. Every one of those answers for free today because a window is in one project. A window of its own answers it for free too, which is why that is the way in.
- It closes for real. On a Mac the app's own window is put away rather than taken down, and a window standing on one thread wearing that close would be hidden with the thread still spoken for, so the next press would open nothing.
- Nothing this machine says about itself is said by a popped out window: the presence, the dictation settings, the awake switch and the dock's icon are the app's own window's, and the dock has one icon however many windows are open.

## Find

`⌘F` opens `FindBar` over a doc and over a thread. It walks the text nodes of whatever it is handed and paints the hits through `CSS.highlights`, so nothing anywhere is re-rendered to mark a match.

- A search reaches what a step is carrying, not what the step is drawing. A folded run of reads shows one line, a thought shows the word Thought, and a command shows what it ran and not what it printed, so a search that reads the screen misses most of a thread. `find.ts` is that rule: `stepText` is everything a step holds and `stepHidden` is the part of it that is closed, and a row or a run whose hidden part is what was typed opens itself.
- It sets the default rather than overruling. `open ?? (found || live)` is what every step wears, so a row somebody has already opened or closed keeps what they chose, and clearing the search puts the thread back the way it was.
- The query is published from the field's own handler, never from an effect. An effect runs after the commit, so the bar would walk the page a pass before the rows that had to open for it, count them out and say nothing was found. Both land in one commit, and the counter is what says so in `tests/thread-find-probe.test.ts`.
- The bar stands inside the column it searches rather than in the window, so a thread with the side panel open does not draw it over the panel. Whatever mounts one holds `relative`.
- It is glass, so nothing on it is set in a solid grey.

## Terminals

A terminal is one of the things the side panel can hold, beside a web page and a file. The New button asks which. It is the real thing: a pty running the login shell, started the way Terminal and Windows Terminal start it, in the project folder.

- `node-pty` opens the pty and xterm.js draws it. node-pty is built on N-API, so the binary in the package loads in Electron and in plain node alike and is never rebuilt for an Electron version. That is what lets the tests drive a real shell.
- The package hands over its `spawn-helper` without the execute bit, and without that every terminal fails to start with "posix_spawnp failed". `scripts/ensure-pty.mjs` puts it back on every install, and `asarUnpack` keeps node-pty out of the asar so the helper stays a file the system can run.
- Sessions live in `src/main/terminal.ts`, one `Terminals` per window, closed with the window and on quit. None of it is written down or shared. It is this machine's shell, not the crew's.
- A shell is only spoken for while it is the one standing under its name. React mounts a view twice while developing, so a tab is opened, closed, and opened again under the same name in one breath, and the first shell's exit lands after the second has taken the name. Striking the new one off on the old one's word leaves a terminal that prints but never listens, and prints "[Process completed]" before its own first prompt.
- The pty is named after the tab, so nothing has to be handed back before the terminal is wired up, and the first keystroke has somewhere to go.
- xterm measures itself against the box it is in, and a box with no size yet throws from deep inside it. The fit is left to a `ResizeObserver` rather than done at mount, which is also what tells the shell its new size when the panel is dragged wider.
- The palette is in `terminalTheme.ts` and follows the app theme. Red and green are the app's own danger and positive.
- Copy and paste on a Mac are the app menu's, and xterm answers the events they raise. Everywhere else it is Ctrl+Shift+C and Ctrl+Shift+V, because plain Ctrl+C has to reach the shell.
- A shell that ends says so and the tab stays open, the way Terminal does, so whatever it printed on the way out can still be read.
- One shell is always kept ready, so a tab opens on a prompt instead of a blinking cursor. A login shell reads the whole profile before it says anything, which is over three seconds cold on a machine that loads a version manager, and none of that wait belongs to the person who just asked for a terminal. `warm` starts the spare, `claim` hands it over, and every `open` starts the next one. Main decides when, in `warmTerminals`: once a window has loaded, and again whenever a session is started, joined or resumed, because that is when the folder a terminal would open in is known. The renderer is not told any of this and has nothing to call.
- A spare is only ever handed to a tab in the folder it was started in, and a shell that ended before anyone claimed it is passed over. Both fall back to starting a shell the old way, so a stale spare can never put a terminal in the wrong place.
- What the spare printed is held and replayed to the tab that takes it, through `replayable`. It is never replayed raw: zsh pads its prompt line out to the width it believes it has and then returns to the start of the line, so bytes drawn for an eighty column spare leave the end of line mark stranded on screen in a tab of another width. Only what follows the last carriage return of a line was ever meant to be read. Nothing here may assume the spare and the tab are the same size.
- The spare counts for nothing in `count()` and goes with `closeAll`, so a window that never opened a terminal still leaves no shell behind.
- **A terminal says what is going on in it, or five of them are five tabs called Terminal.** What names the tab is the foreground process on the pty's own slave device, read off one `ps` for every terminal in the window at once and matched by tty, with the shell's own pid left out: idle, the shell is the foreground and there is nothing to say. `foregroundOn` in `src/shared/terminalName.ts` is that reading and `commandName` is the tidying beside it, since `node /Users/x/.nvm/versions/node/v26.3.0/bin/yarn start` is the interpreter's line rather than the work's and reads as `yarn start`.
- **A shell standing at its prompt is still the terminal somebody opened to run something**, so the last thing it ran is what it goes on being called until it runs the next one. `ran` keeps `RAN_KEPT` of them, newest first, and the pill takes the head of it the moment nothing is running. A title the shell set itself beats both, since that is somebody's own configuration speaking.
- Nothing is read while nothing is open, and the next read is armed once the last has settled rather than on a clock, which is the rule the sync loop already holds: a machine that answers slowly would otherwise be handed a queue that only ever grows. A reading that has not moved is never sent, so a terminal sitting at a prompt costs one `ps` and no messages at all.
- Windows has no `ps` and no slave device to match on, so nothing is read there and the tab falls back to whatever it was opened to run. It is left out rather than guessed at.
- **The whole of it stands up on hover, which is the rule a thread's own ask line already holds.** The pill is 180 across and a real command line is longer than that, so `browserTabCard` stands the name uncut, where the tab really points, and everything else that terminal has run. It is measured rather than guessed, off the name span being cut off, and a card with none of those to say is nothing at all rather than the pill read back. It is the tab's card rather than the terminal's, so a web tab hands over its whole address and a file tab its whole path, and it stands down while the tab's own menu is open the way a tooltip does.
- The card hangs off the mark and the name inside the pill rather than off the pill itself. `useReorder` reads every `[data-reorder]` in the strip and refuses a list whose items do not share one parent, so a wrapper around the button would quietly take away the drag that reorders tabs.
- `tests/terminal-name.test.ts` holds the reading and the naming with no shell in the room, `tests/browser-tabs-probe.test.ts` holds the pill and when the card stands, and the case in `tests/terminal.integration.test.ts` runs a real command in a real pty and reads back which tab it was reported on and that the tab goes quiet again after it.

## Files

A file opened from a message that changed it opens on the change, showing the diff the way VS Code shows one. It is still a file you can type in.

- The lines that were taken out sit above the ones that replaced them, and the words that changed inside a line are tinted again over the line's own tint, the way VS Code marks what changed inside a line.
- A step only records the text an agent swapped, not where it landed. `baseline.ts` matches that text against the file on disk and reverses it, to get the file as it stood before. `diffRows.ts` diffs that against what is on screen, and does it again on every keystroke, so what you type is part of the diff from the moment you type it. VS Code waits 200ms, because its editor holds the document and draws the deleted lines beside it. Here they are text in the same box, so a keystroke has to land on the rows it was typed against and there is nothing to wait for.
- That box holds the deleted lines too, because it is the only way the text under the caret lines up with the rows drawn behind it. What is typed is mapped back onto the real file first with `toDoc`, and the caret is put back after with `toShown`. Never write the box's contents to disk. `docText` is the file and the rest is the diff.
- The caret never sits in a line that was taken out. It steps over the whole block, back or forward depending on which way it came, the way a cursor steps over a view zone in VS Code. Rubbing out at the head of a line under a block joins it to the line above, because that is the line above it in the file.
- Clicking dismisses nothing. It puts the caret where it landed and leaves the page where it was standing. Hide changes puts the diff away.

Files from the side panel's own New button opens the same tab with the project standing beside it, and the button in the toolbar puts that pane away and brings it back.

- The tree and the folder listing are the same thing, so only one is ever on screen. `FileView` shows `DirRows` when the pane is closed and an empty state when it is open, rather than the same folder twice.
- A folder opens where it stands and is read only once it is asked for, so nothing walks the whole project to draw the top of it. Which folders are open rides on the tab, beside where it has been, so a pane closed and opened again is where it was left.
- Turning the pane on opens every folder down to the file already showing and scrolls to it. A tree that lands back at the top of the project when you were reading something four folders down is a tree nobody uses twice.
- The filter searches the whole project rather than the folders that happen to be open. `listRepoFiles` asks git what the project is made of, which leaves out everything `.gitignore` covers for free, and only a folder that is not a repository is walked by hand.
- What was typed is matched letter by letter, in order, by `matchFiles` in `src/shared/files.ts`. A run inside the file's own name beats one spread across the folders above it, and the letters that landed are handed back so they can be picked out in the row.
- A right click on a row opens what it names in a tab of its own. Three kinds of row do it, the folder listing, the tree and the filter's results, so what is shared is the menu rather than the row: `useFileMenu` in `fileMenu.tsx` hands each one a handler for its button and the card to stand beside it. It is a new tab every time rather than the one already holding that file, since keeping the file you are reading while this tab goes on somewhere else is half the reason to ask for one, and a row that says new tab and does nothing is worse than a second tab.
- A path nobody here can follow is shown as it was written, and Private file is the one exception. Being missing was what raised the chip before, so `/agents/spawn` and `/users/12/posts` came back as somebody's file and the words went off the screen: a slash is how an address is written as well as a folder, and the app cannot tell the two apart by looking for them on the disk. `personalPath` in `src/shared/files.ts` is the whole of the exception, a named file under somebody's own folder, and both halves have to hold. Everything else falls through as `local` and reads as text. The two ways of being wrong do not cost the same, which is what settles it: a full path shown where a chip would have done is a line somebody reads and moves past, and a chip standing over a sentence takes the sentence away.
- A path may have spaces in it, and a step that is nothing but a path is where that has to hold. `Application Support`, `Google Drive` and a folder somebody named in two words are all on an ordinary Mac, and a Read of one of them was a row that would not open: it fell through to a card holding the path as text, which is the one thing a person cannot follow. `parseFileRef` is where spaces are allowed and `fileTokens` is where they are not, and the split is what makes it safe. A whole string that is a path is unambiguous however many spaces are in it, and a path hunted for inside a sentence would swallow the words after it. A false positive costs one lookup and no link, since `openable` still asks whether the file is really there.

A file that is written to be read as a page is offered both ways, and one button in the toolbar is the switch. `canPreview` in `src/shared/files.ts` is which files those are, markdown and html, and `preview` rides on the tab, so walking from one page to the next keeps reading them the way you asked.

- The page is drawn from the words in hand rather than from the file on disk, so an edit typed in the text is on the page. There is no typing while it is up, since the box the caret lives in is the diff and a page is not one, and Save and Discard stay where they were.
- A markdown page is drawn by the app, in `MarkdownView`, and every picture it points at is read off this machine by `mdImages.ts`, because a page drawn in the app cannot reach a file by its path.
- An html page is drawn by the browser the panel already has, in a webview, because a page rendered by anything less than a browser is not the page. It is written out to the app's own folder first, by `Previews` in `src/main/preview.ts`, and never beside the file: a page written into the project would be committed and synced to the whole crew for the few seconds somebody spends looking at it. A window takes its own pages away when it goes, the way it takes its terminals.
- Standing somewhere else is what makes a base necessary. `withBase` in `src/shared/htmlPage.ts` hands the page the folder it really lives in, so the stylesheet, the pictures and the scripts beside it are still reached, and a page that says its own base keeps it. Without it the page still draws, unstyled and with a hole where every picture is, which is the failure no suite can see: `tests/html-preview-probe.test.ts` holds the words and the switch, and `yarn preview-check` is the only thing that can see the rest of it, since it stands a real page up in a real window and reads the color, the picture and the script back out of it.

## Writing a path

A path typed into the composer completes as it is written, and what lands is a link the crew can press. `src/shared/pathMention.ts` is the project half and `src/shared/machinePath.ts` is the machine half, and neither one holds a window.

- A run with a slash in it opens the menu. A bare word never does, or every word anybody types would open one.
- A slash at the head of an empty box still belongs to the commands. `/g` is `/goal` and `/ghost`, and the moment no command answers, a path takes it over. A path in the middle of a sentence never competes at all.
- The project is read once for the window, in `state/projectFiles.ts`, and a folder off this machine is read one directory at a time, in `state/machineFiles.ts`. Both are keyed on the place, so a project switch cannot serve the last one's files. Walking down a folder is the menu filtering a listing it already holds rather than a read a keystroke, which is the whole reason the directory is what gets asked for rather than the query.
- A leading slash is read at the root and then under your own folder, in that order, so `/Documents/Repositories` is the folder somebody means rather than a folder the machine does not have. `~` is the other way into the same place.
- Both halves stand in one menu and what was typed the head of comes first: the project's, then the machine's, then the letter by letter matches in the same order. `pathMenu` is the whole of that rule. A name starting with what was typed beats a run of letters scattered across a path, which is what makes `/Us` read as `/Users` rather than as every project file with a u and an s in it.
- A dot at the head of what was typed asks for the hidden ones and nothing else. Read as an ordinary letter it matched every name with a dot in it, which is every file there is.
- **What lands is project relative wherever the project holds the file, whatever was typed to reach it.** The full path to a file in the project goes in as `src/renderer`, so it resolves on every machine in the crew rather than on one. The row says the path that will be written rather than the one being typed, or picking one is a surprise at the moment it is too late to take back.
- A path outside the project goes in as the full path, expanded, and never as a tilde. A tilde means the reader's own folder rather than the writer's, so one would open a different file on every machine, where the full path reads as Private file on all of them but this one. Unfollowable is honest and quietly opening the wrong file is not.
- A folder takes its slash with it and the menu stays open on what is inside it, so the next press picks a child, and the slash is also what makes a folder a link once the message is sent.
- A path is marked in the box before it is sent, the way a name about to be handed to somebody is, and only one the app knows is really there: held by the project, or revealed by a directory the menu has already read. A guess would dress a line up as a link and then send it as plain words.
- A path with a space in it stops completing at the space, since a run is read back to the last space and there is nothing else to read it back to. `Google Drive` is the one people meet.
- `tests/composer-paths.test.ts` holds the project half, `tests/composer-machine-paths.test.ts` holds the machine half and the composer, and `tests/machine-dirs.test.ts` holds the reading against a real folder, a link to one included.

## Review

What has changed since the last commit, in the side panel. It is where somebody working on their own spends the long half of the day: an agent finishes, and this is the screen the work is read on before any of it is kept. `ReviewView` is the panel and `useRepoWork` is what it reads, one call every three seconds that never lands on top of an action.

- Reading is the whole of it, so a change is drawn in the lines around it. Git already sends three either side of what moved and `rowsOf` threw all of them away: a line with nothing around it cannot be judged, and two stretches four hundred lines apart ran together as though they were neighbours. `reviewRows.ts` is the reader that keeps them, and `rowsOf` stays exactly as it was for a step in a thread, where a diff is a glance at what an agent did rather than the work itself.
- `line` on a row is what the highlighter reads and `at` is the file's own number. They are two fields because they are two things: the document handed to shiki is the one being drawn, so a file whose first stretch starts at line four hundred would hand it four hundred lines it does not have and every row would lose its colour.
- What stands between one stretch and the next is a rule rather than a count of the lines nobody is being shown, since the numbers either side already say how far it is. It carries what git worked out the lines below it sit inside, because landing partway down a file is the one thing that cannot be read off the lines themselves.
- **The list and the file being read stand one above the other, and the list is never taken away.** Every desktop client draws these two together: VS Code keeps its sidebar while the diff is in the editor, GitHub Desktop puts a 250 wide list beside one, and not one of them has a next-file key. That is not an omission, it is what having the list on screen means, since the list is how you get to the next file. Crew's panel is 480 across, narrower than GitHub Desktop's own floor for the pair, so the same two panes are stacked rather than laid side by side: the list keeps the width a path needs and the reading gets the whole of it for a line of code. The accordion this replaced was neither, a diff in a slice of a column under a list it pushed off the screen.
- One file is read at a time. Several open at once was the answer while the list was the only surface there was, and it cost the reading the bottom of the panel: the second file opened under the first and everything worth comparing was already scrolled away. Picking another puts it in place of the one before it, the row it stands on says which, and the arrows walk the list with the reading following as they go.
- `split.ts` is where the two panes meet, as numbers and nothing else. Both have a floor, because a list under a heading and a few rows and a diff under a hunk are furniture rather than somewhere to work, and a panel too short for both floors is halved rather than handing the list more than the window has. How tall the list stands is this machine's own, in `localStorage` the way the volume is, so it is never sent.
- The reading wraps and a step in a thread does not, which is `wrap` on `DiffLines` and the one place the two diverge. A glance at what an agent did is read past rather than read, and the line it cuts off is one nobody was going to reach. A file under review in a 480 column is the opposite: the line running off the right is the line being judged. GitHub Desktop, the one client built for a narrow pane, wraps and never scrolls sideways at all. What a wrapped line does with its second half is the whole of whether that is readable, so it hangs under the code: flush against it, every wrap reads as a new line of the file and a diff stops having a shape.
- Which files you have been through is yours rather than the crew's, so it is held on this machine in `state/reviewed.ts` and nothing about it is ever sent, the way an answered question on a board is. It is keyed on the place and on a digest of the diff, so a file an agent has written to again comes back unviewed: a mark held against the path alone is a promise the screen cannot keep.
- The row going quiet is what says it has been viewed, and the mark is the quietest thing on the row rather than the loudest. White is the one action color and this is not the action. Not viewed, there is no mark at all until the pointer is there, or a column of empty circles down a list of files is a column asking to be dealt with and most of them never are.
- Nothing about committing stands on the panel until there is something to commit. It was a field and a button at the top of the screen for the whole of the time there was nothing to save, which is a dead box and a dead press above the one thing anybody came here to do. What stands instead is the same button Implement plan is, in the same place at the foot of the same panel: one white pill the width of the column, standing in the layout rather than floating over it, and it opens the card the message is written on. On glass it was a pill inside a card, which is two rounded boxes for one press, and it covered the last lines of whatever was being read underneath.
- **Git's own words are the words here, and this is the one screen in the app where that beats writing plainly.** The rule about plain words holds everywhere Crew is speaking for itself. This screen is Crew putting a hand on somebody else's tool, and there the tool's own name is the plain word: everybody in front of it already has a repo, a branch and a remote, stage and discard and stash are the names of real places and real moves rather than jargon for them, and every client anybody has used for ten years calls them that. Translated, the panel lost the one thing that makes a tool usable on sight, which is knowing what it does before reading it. Going in did not say where, Take all out read like a delete, and somebody who knows git had to work out which of git's moves each line meant. Never translate them again.
- The groups are git's own groups and they are named the way VS Code names them: Merge Changes, Staged Changes, Changes, and Stashes for what is put by. An empty one is not drawn. Each folds away, since a group somebody is done with is a group in the way of the one they are not.
- A conflict stands in Merge Changes at the head of the list rather than in among the ordinary edits with a mark on it. It is the one thing on the screen that has to be settled by hand, and the way out of it is staging what has been settled: nothing offers to discard one, because taking a conflicted file back to what the index holds throws away both sides of it rather than one edit.
- Every row wears the letter git would give it, in front of the path, where `git status --short` puts it and where a client that reads like a terminal puts it. VS Code has it at the far end and it went there once for that reason, but the end of a row is also where the counts, the two moves and the viewed mark all have to stand in 480 pixels, and a column read as a gutter is scanned in one pass where the same column hunted for on the right is not. A column that says the same thing on most rows is noise only while it is loud: muted, it is a place to look rather than a thing to read, and the whole of its worth is the row where it differs. Only the two the row cannot otherwise carry are colored, an add and a delete, and a deleted name is struck through the way it is everywhere else.
- A row is a row rather than a card. It was `rounded-card` on `ink-800` with a gap under it, which is 46 pixels a file and seven files in a panel that had to hold forty: seven objects standing apart rather than one list to run an eye down. Flush rows at 28 with a wash under the pointer is what every client does and it is what makes the count of files on screen the count of files there are. VS Code's own row is 22 and GitHub Desktop's is 29, so 28 is the band rather than a number picked here.
- Untracked is worked out here rather than sent.
