A file git has never been told about comes back as an add that is not staged, since nothing can stage itself, so that pair is the whole rule and there is no kind to add to `RepoChange` for it. It is worth saying: a file nobody has added yet is a file that commits by nobody's decision.
- A row holds the two moves it is really for, and the rest is on a right click, the way it is in Files and the way it is in every client this is meant to feel like. The slot for them is held whether or not the pointer is there, so nothing on a row travels as it is reached for and a staged row with one move in it lines up with an unstaged row that has two.
- Discard stands last in a row's menu, under a divider, and never above Stage. VS Code has it the other way and that is an accident of its own sort rather than a decision: its menu items carry the same order number and the tie is broken on the title, where Discard comes before Stage in the alphabet. What is deliberate is Crew's rule, which is that the one press nobody can undo goes last.
- Viewed is the word, not read. It is what code review has called it since GitHub, and how far through you are reads as progress rather than as a debt. Marking a file viewed and moving to the next one is one press, in the pane where the reading really ends, because that is the whole of what a review is made of and doing it in two presses is doing it twice.
- How far through the reading somebody is stands as a line under the header rather than as a sentence. Said in words it is a row of the panel carrying a number that is only ever glanced at, and the branch, the size of the whole change and the two ways to move it already have that row. It wears no `Tooltip`, and that is a rule rather than an omission: a tooltip is an inline-flex box that shrinks to what is inside it, so a bar asking for the whole width comes out at nothing at all, and being inline it stands on a line box of its own, which was twenty pixels of nothing under the header. Anything full width that has to be measured has both of those problems.
- One header row, not three. It was the branch, then a summary, then the first heading, which is a hundred pixels of chrome over the one thing anybody came here for.
- Pressing Push commits the whole working tree first, `.crew` included, which is `commitWorkingTree` in `src/server/git.ts` rather than anything the panel decides. Nothing on this screen says so yet.
- **The branch is the way in, since the panel already stood on its name.** Pressing it opens the branches, a search and the list, and the row a search lands on switches to it. There is no second control for this and no screen of its own: what says which branch is being reviewed is what changes it, the way the board's own name is what opens the boards.
- The list ends on the way to a branch that is not there yet, the way the toolbox ends its own row on the slot that opens the builder and the rail ends on the way to a crew you are not in. It is one row in two states rather than two rows. With something typed that answers to nothing it makes that branch outright, and with nothing typed it opens the screen the name is written on, so a branch is one press from the search that just failed to find it.
- **What the row says is the name git will really take rather than the one being typed.** `cleanBranchName` in `src/shared/branch.ts` is the rule, so "my new thing" reads back as `my-new-thing` before it is pressed. It is the same reason a path completing in the composer says the path that will be written: picking one is otherwise a surprise at the moment it is too late to take back. Only what git refuses is taken out, so a name it would have accepted is never quietly rewritten.
- A branch that is only on the remote stands in the list under its plain name, and switching to one is git making the local branch that follows it. A name more than one remote answers to is left out, since git cannot tell which was meant either. Nothing about this is a second kind of row: it wears the cloud rather than a word, and the check says which one you are on.
- **A branch made here has no upstream, and the sync loop used to die on that forever.** A pull with no upstream can only ever fail, so it was logged as a failure, the pass stood down before it reached the push, and three of those raised the trouble toast: every branch anybody made was a project that quietly stopped syncing. `upstream` is what asks, the pull is skipped where there is nothing to pull, and `pushCurrent` sets the upstream on the first push instead. The one that publishes a branch says it pushed rather than "Already up to date", since a branch that has just landed on the origin is not nothing.
- A switch takes the sync lock every other verb takes, and it is only ever run on a branch git itself just reported, the same rule that keeps a stage off a path nobody named. Git refuses a switch that would overwrite work, and its own words are what come back.
- `tests/repo-branches.test.ts` holds the reading and the two verbs against real repos and a real origin, the remote only row and the push that sets an upstream among them.
- `tests/review-panel.test.ts` holds the groups, the conflict standing in its own and offering no discard, the letter on every row, a group folding away, one file read at a time under the list, the arrows walking it, viewed moving to the next one, the pane following a file that has just been staged, the lines around a change, the numbers coming off the file, the wrap being the reading's and never a step's, the floors under both panes, the right click, the mark surviving a reload and coming undone when the diff moves, and the commit staying off the panel until there is something to commit.

## The boot

The first thing the app says. It opens travelling, drops out of it, and the crew lands on the other side. `views/Boot.tsx` is the moment and `components/boot/` is what it is made of.

- It happens once, on the way in, and never again. `App` holds a flag rather than reading the connection, so leaving a session goes back to the list and not back to the mark. It ends when the app is ready and the mark has landed, whichever is later: an app with nothing to load would otherwise flash the whole thing past in three frames.
- A window flies in once while the app is being worked on, and a reload lands straight in the app. Watching it go past on every save is what teaches people to sit through it with their eyes elsewhere, and it is the one thing that would make the way in worse the more it is looked at. `seen.ts` is that rule, in `sessionStorage` so it belongs to the window rather than to the machine: a window opened fresh flies again, which is what leaves the flight there to work on, and a build people run always flies.
- The rules are numbers and nothing else. `warp.ts` holds the field, the speed and the projection, `nebula.ts` holds the cloud, and neither one draws anything or knows what a canvas is, the same split the games hold. `drawWarp.ts` is the picture and `WarpField.tsx` is the canvas and the loop, which is `useGameLoop`, so a frame is worth what it really took.
- Nothing is held in state. The field is written straight onto the canvas every frame the way the music's bars are, because a render a frame would cost more than the picture is worth.
- A star is a line from where it was to where it is, and `TRAIL` stretches that line well past one frame's travel. One frame is a few pixels however fast the field is going, so without the stretch lightspeed is a screen of dots. As the field slows the two ends of the line close up until what is left is a point, and that closing is the whole of dropping out of lightspeed.
- The mark lands white. Only the light behind it is lit, so `data-crew-lit` goes on the glow and never on the boot itself: lit, the discs become windows onto the mesh and the mark stops being the mark. `data-crew-lit` is the one hook for this and `CrewLogo` wears it too, so the button in the top bar and the light on the way in turn the same field on.
- `CrewGlow` is that light. It is the mesh the mark is a window onto, drawn without the mask and thrown wide, and it carries no sky: a sky is a rectangle, and a blurred rectangle reads as a box standing behind the mark rather than as light coming off it.
- The cloud is worked out pixel by pixel, once, and then only ever drawn, so its detail costs nothing per frame. A handful of soft circles is a smudge wherever you put them. What makes a nebula read as one is filaments, and a filament is noise folded back on itself: the field is domain warped before it is read, and ridged after, which turns broad humps into thin veins with dark between them.
- It covers the whole window. The middle is brighter than the corners by a little, never by enough to leave the edges empty, and `tests/boot-nebula.test.ts` reads the picture in tiles and holds it there.
- It is animated by moving against itself. Layers at different sizes turn, breathe and drift their own ways, and what they are scaled by is the travel the stars have already made rather than a clock, so the cloud rushes while the field is at lightspeed and settles as it comes off it. One layer over a window is wallpaper however good it is, because nothing in it moves against anything else.
- Two clouds are drawn, and the second one on the frame after the first, so the flight starts on time rather than waiting on both.
- Color comes from `MESH_COLORS`, which is the mark's own mesh. The stars are white but for a few, the cloud is lit by the same table, and nothing here invents a palette of its own.
- Reduced motion stands the flight down and lands the mark where it is.

## Several projects at once

A rail down the left holding every place you have been, and under each one the threads running there. It is opened by the mark beside the Crew logo: hovering it stands the rail over the page, pressing it pins the rail and pushes the page over, the way the browser does on the right.

- The rail runs the whole height of the window, the title band included, and there is no gap over it. Started under the top bar it was a strip of somebody else's chrome sitting on the rail's head, and the wrapper standing there took the clicks meant for the button underneath, so the one control that puts the rail away could not be pressed while the rail was open. It stands above the top bar in the ladder, on `z-50` with the panels, which is also what keeps the chrome's own scrim off the first rows of the list.
- It is written last in the tree it stands over, and that half is about the drag region rather than the ladder. `-webkit-app-region` is flat geometry and a `z` means nothing to it: the bands are read in the order they are written, so a drag band written after the rail hands the rail's own rows back to the window to be dragged by. The design board's header is one of those, the width of the page, and the top of the first project in the rail sat inside it, which reads as a row that moves the window instead of opening the project. Last, the rail's `app-no-drag` is the final word on that band. What is painted over what is settled a rung above, by the page being one stacking context.
- **The mark and the button that opens the rail belong to the window's corner rather than to the top bar.** `WindowCorner` is that pair, standing at `z-[55]` above the rail and below a dialog, and it is the one rung the ladder gained: the rail covers the band they used to be drawn in, and a button that is one press from the pointer has to stay where the pointer is. Nothing about them moves, ever. The rail opening under them changes neither their place nor which element they are, so the pointer that opened the rail is still on the button that shuts it, and the `mac:pl-[64px]` beside the traffic lights is written once here instead of moving between the top bar and the rail.
- The corner holds the hover it does not start. Reaching from the rail up to the mark leaves the rail, and reaching from the button across to the mark leaves the button, so both would have shut it a breath later. The button is the only thing that opens it and the whole corner is what keeps it open, which is a press on it while it stands and no rail that opens for a pointer resting on the mark.
- **A drag band reports no hover at all, so nothing standing over one may be revealed by a hover.** macOS hands `-webkit-app-region: drag` to the window rather than to the page, and what it swallows is every mouse event over it, hover included, so the head of a pinned rail cannot say the pointer is on it. The button was off until the pointer was on the rail, and the two rules cannot both hold: the band is the one place the pointer is invisible, so wherever the reveal was read from, the band was the dead spot in it. It was the corner's own hover first, which left the whole rail dead, then the rail's, which left the band dead, and the band is exactly where somebody coming across the window meets it.
- So the button stands, in both states, and there is nothing left for the band to have to reveal. The head of the rail is the window's the way a title bar is, the two controls come out of it in a reach of their own at the left end, and the sliver beside them drags the window. A toggle standing there all day was the thing that rule was written to avoid, and it costs less than a control that cannot be found: it is quiet, `text-fg-muted` until it is reached, so it reads as the chrome it stands in rather than as the one lit thing in the corner, and pinned or away it is the same button in the same place wearing the same weight.
- That reach runs the full height of the band rather than the height of the button, so a pointer coming up off the rows lands on it without crossing a pixel of drag region. It is what every title bar does with its own controls, and it is why the traffic lights are the only other thing in it.
- **Pinned, the rail is a hole in the window rather than a surface in the page.** What comes through it is the macOS material behind the glass, and that only reaches the screen where nothing in front of it is painted, so `#root.railed` stops the app painting its own floor and the columns beside the rail paint theirs. The window was already transparent, so nothing else about it moves, and the material is `under-window` rather than `sidebar` because a sidebar's is the livelier of the two and this one stands under Crew's own tint.
- The tint is the app's own `--glass-bg` and nothing else, the same three values anything wearing glass without the class reads. Bare, the material is whatever is behind the window, which on a blue desktop is a blue rail, and a panel of the app's that takes its color from somebody's wallpaper is not the app's any more. Over the tint what is left of it is the light rather than the color.
- A hovered rail cannot have any of that, and it is right that it does not. It stands over the chat rather than beside it, and a backdrop filter samples the page it is drawn in, so the material behind the window is behind the thread as well and could never reach it. It wears `.glass` instead, the same tint over the app it covers, and adds `glass-strong` over the design canvas the way every other floating panel does.
- **A rail opened by a pointer has to be able to turn around, so it is never taken out of the tree.** A hover is a gesture somebody is still making: the pointer sweeps past the corner, reaches for the button, changes its mind halfway. A keyframe cannot answer any of that, because it starts at its own `from` however far the rail had already got, so an arrival caught halfway either ran to the end and vanished or began again from off the screen the next time. And a rail that is unmounted has no way out at all, which is what the grace in front of it was really covering: a hundred milliseconds of a panel standing still, and then it was simply not there. It is mounted whenever it is not pinned and shut with a transition instead, which moves from wherever the thing is standing, so leaving mid arrival turns it around from there. The grace is shorter for the same reason: the leaving is now the forgiveness, since coming back during it catches it.
- It comes from its own left edge rather than growing, so the rows inside it never move, and it goes quicker than it arrives, the rule the screen being left already holds. Both timings are on `.rail` and `.rail[data-open]` rather than on one of them, because a transition is written on the state being moved to and that is the only way in and out can differ. What is shut is `visibility` rather than an opacity: the glass throws a 48 pixel shadow, so a rail parked off the left edge at full strength paints a dark band down the side of the app forever, and hidden also takes it out of the tab order and out of reach of the pointer without anything having to be said about hit testing.
- Pinning from a hover is the one gesture where two rails exist at once, and the hovered one is held for the length of it. The pinned rail is revealed by a box growing from nothing, so dropping the hovered one at the press leaves it being unmasked from its left edge while an identical rail vanishes off the top of it. Held, the two are the same pixels and the only thing that moves is the page being pushed over. `PIN_MS` is that length and it is the width change's own, written once so the two cannot disagree.
- The material follows the theme Crew is wearing rather than the one the system is, which is `nativeTheme.themeSource` off the `app:theme` the renderer already sends. Left alone it is the system's answer, so a light Crew on a dark Mac is dark text on a dark frosted rail.
- Windows keeps the surface it always had. Its window is opaque, there is no material behind it, and a hole cut in an opaque window is a hole.
- **A project you switch away from keeps running.** That one rule is the whole feature and it is why this was main process work rather than a screen. `src/main/index.ts` held one `AppSession`, and `startHost` began with `await this.stop()`, so every switch killed the agents working in the project you left. A rail on top of that is a faster way back to the list rather than a way to work in two places.
- `src/main/crews.ts` is the registry, and `AppSession` now means one live crew and nothing else. The seven things that were about the machine rather than about a session moved onto it: the recent projects and joins, forgetting either, what opening a folder would do, what the CLIs on this machine can do, and installing one. Nothing there touches a live session, so keeping them on one was what made the class mean two things at once.
- A place is keyed the way the list already keys it, `project:<folder>` or `join:<link>`, from `src/shared/places.ts`. The row in the rail and the session running behind it are named the same thing, so nothing has to be matched up.
- Which crew a window is looking at is a map from the window's own id, the same shape `terminalsFor` already uses, so two windows on one machine can be in two different projects. Every handler that used to read the one session reads the one this window is in, and a window with none is handed a session that has never been started, which is what keeps "Open a project first." saying what it always said.
- **Crews are never remade to be switched between.** `switchTo` moves which crew a window is looking at and nothing else: no server closes, no runner reconnects, no git loop stops. The window closes its socket and opens one on the other crew, and the welcome hands it the whole session, so coming back is complete and instant with nothing cached and nothing to migrate.
- The connection goes straight from one crew to the other and never through home. That single line is what makes a switch seamless rather than a flash of the way in, since `App` unmounts everything the moment `connection` reads `home`.
- **The port is the machine's and the code is the crew's, and getting that the wrong way round is what made a link good for one run.** A crew held a listener of its own, so which of them got `PREFERRED_PORT` fell out of the order projects happened to be opened in and every crew after the first took whatever was spare. Somebody far away was sent an address that moved when the host opened another project, restarted, or opened the same two in the other order, and there was nothing they could do about it from where they were standing. A code is written down in the crew's own `session.json` and never changes, so it is the half of the address that can be relied on, and the port is the half that cannot. `Door` in `src/server/door.ts` is that turned round: one listener, every crew on it under its own code, and the code is the first segment of every path into one, the socket at `/<code>/ws` and the http under `/<code>`. Nothing had to be handed around for the http half, since a runner and a window both read their base off the socket they are on and `httpBaseFrom` carries the code through for free.
- **A private crew is never on the door that answers the network, and that is held by the bind rather than by a check.** `Doors` in `src/main/doors.ts` opens two, `0.0.0.0` for the crews that are shared and `127.0.0.1` for the crews that are not, and inviting people is a crew moving from the second to the first. One door with a rule about which sockets may reach which crew would work and would put the whole of that guarantee on a line of code being right, where a crew that is not on the listener cannot be reached from the network whatever anybody writes. Only the open door asks for `PREFERRED_PORT`, because it is the only one a link is ever written from.
- A code two crews answer to is the one thing a door refuses, and that crew takes a door of its own the way every crew used to. It is a real case rather than a guard: an older crew's code was three bytes, and two of those are not far apart.
- **The code carries the whole of the address now, so it is eight bytes rather than three.** The port was accidental entropy and it is gone, so what stands between somebody on the network and a crew they were never sent is the code alone. `CODE_BYTES` in `src/shared/link.ts` is the length and it is only ever read for a crew being made: a code already written down is that crew's forever, or a link somebody was sent stops working, which is the thing this whole change is about.
- Two crews opened in the same breath would both find the port free, so `Crews.start` runs one at a time and `Doors` opens one door at a time. That is also what makes one folder asked for twice at once one crew rather than two, which is the same race a beat earlier.
- **A rule like this cannot be held by a suite that runs on a machine with Crew open.** The real app is sitting on the preferred port, so every crew under test falls back to a port of its own and a suite that only reads the numbers passes whatever is broken. `tests/crew-door.test.ts` reads what a door really answers instead, two crews at one port each on its own code, a code nobody answers to turned away at the socket and at the http both, and a private crew's door standing on loopback. `tests/crew-port.test.ts` keeps the collision, since `portToAsk` is still what a second Crew on the machine runs into.
- Turning sharing on and off moves the crew between the two doors and is meant to be the only thing that happens. The door it arrives at is already standing where another shared crew put it, so the port is the one it was always going to be, and the window is told where it went rather than reconnecting to where it used to be.
- A second Crew process is the one case a guest's address still moves. `crew` opens an app per folder and only one of them can hold the port, so the second takes one of its own and writes its links from there. Nothing here brokers between the two, and the common case, one app with every project in the rail, is the one this answers.
- **What belongs to the machine stays, what belongs to the crew goes.** A terminal is this machine's shell rather than the crew's, so a terminal tab belongs to the window and is left standing through a switch: start a build in one project, switch, and watch it finish from another. `stash` and `restore` on the browser store partition on exactly that, the project's tabs are lifted out and the incoming project's land in their place, and the terminals hold the tail of the row so the one thing that persists is never the thing that jumps under the pointer. A terminal that was the tab you were on stays the tab you are on, which is the panel not changing at all when you switch, and that is right: you were typing in a shell and you still are.
- A terminal keeps the folder it was opened in, because the shell has a cwd and may have been `cd`'d anywhere since, and that is the truth rather than something to correct.
- The warm spare is a small map keyed by folder rather than one shell. `claim` refuses a spare warmed for another folder, which was free when a window saw one project a session and is three seconds of a blinking cursor every time somebody switches. Two is the project in view and the one before it, so moving back and forth between a pair never pays for a login shell twice, and `warmTerminals` runs on a switch off the folder now in view.
- The huddle ends when you leave the project it is in. A call belongs to a session, and the leave goes out before the socket closes so everyone else hears an ordinary departure rather than watching a tile hang about until the host notices the connection has gone.
- **Leaving is done with this crew rather than done with the app.** With another one still running the window lands there instead of on the list, and only the last one out goes home.
- The threads under a place are the ones really running there, read off that crew's own `CrewSession` in this process. A place that is not running shows no threads, which is honest: nothing here is guessing at what is in a project it has not opened. A crew somebody else is hosting shows none either, since its events are on their machine.
- `activeThreads` in `src/shared/threads.ts` is that fold, and it leaves out what is not somebody's own work: a helper, a question asked on the side, and anything finished or archived. A ghost thread is never in it without a rule of its own, because a ghost's events go to one socket and never reach the session's own list. Every thread that is open is in it, however many a long project has: it was cut to a handful once, and what that cost is the one thing the rail cannot say, which is that there are more. A thread left off the list is a thread with no way in but the chat, and the chat is exactly what the columns take off the screen.
- **The rail is what holds the room rather than the fold, and it holds it in rows.** `THREADS_SHOWN` is how many stand under a project before the list scrolls inside itself, and past that the group is as tall as it always was however many threads are really in it, so a project working forty of them does not push every other project off the bottom of the rail. It wears `scroll-fade`, the rail's own, since a list cut off at a row boundary with no bar reads as a list that ends there, and `overscroll-contain`, so reaching the end of one project's threads does not carry the whole rail along with it. `.rail-threads` in `styles.css` is the room, and it is worked out from the row rather than written down as a height: a row is one line of the app's own small type with its padding either side, so the list follows the ramp instead of drifting from it the day the ramp moves. The count is handed in as `--rail-rows`, since how many rows stand is the rail's decision and the height of one is the stylesheet's. `tests/rail-threads-rule.test.ts` holds the arithmetic and `tests/sidebar-probe.test.ts` holds every thread being on the list and only a long one wearing the scroller.
- They stand in the order they were last worked in rather than the order they were opened, so a thread somebody has just come back to after a week is at the top rather than six rows down under everything opened since. `stirs` in `src/shared/threads.ts` is what counts as something happening in one: a run starting or ending in it, and a message landing in it. A step is left out on purpose, because a run has already put its thread at the top and nothing it says on the way can move it from there.
- `CrewSession.onEvent` is the one hook this hangs off, on `emit`, which is the single funnel every event already goes through. The list is only pushed again for the events that really reshape it, `RESHAPES_THREADS`, because a run emits a step every few hundred milliseconds and pushing the whole rail for each would be the rail redrawing all afternoon. Nothing may move a thread up the rail without being in that set, or the order on screen is right only until the next thing that is.
- What says a message landed is `message.route` rather than the message, which is why that one is in `RESHAPES_THREADS` and in `THREAD_EVENT_KINDS` beside it. It is five ids, so a joined window's rail is seeded with the real order and every chat message stays off the runner's own channel and out of what that window holds in memory.
- Pressing a thread in another project switches to it and opens that thread, and the opening waits for the welcome rather than happening at once, because until the welcome lands this window has no such thread to open.
- What a project keeps while you are away is its side panel and the thread you had open, in `state/projectMemory.ts`, and terminals are named there as the exception since that is the one place somebody would go looking for them. Everything else re-arrives in the welcome, so nothing else is worth keeping. Which tab of the top bar you were on is the window's rather than the project's, and stays where it is.
- **Where you were reading in the chat is the project's own, and it is the one thing a switch loses by the app never unmounting.** `useStickToBottom` keeps a place per key, and the chat's key was the word chat, so every project shared one. The feed is the same box the whole time: the events go, the content collapses, and the browser clamps the scroll to the top of what is left, which is a scroll event like any other and wrote that clamp over wherever somebody had really been. So the key is the place and the chat together, a key that has changed is a view standing somewhere else and restores again rather than only on a mount, and nothing is written down for a place until it has been restored into, or a project with no chats in it records a position it never had. Coming back pinned was always the bottom, since the bottom is worked out from the content rather than remembered; it was the reader who had scrolled up who came back to the top of a month of chat.
- **The slot at the end of a nav row does not take the pointer, and a control standing in it asks for it back.** `NavRow` draws that slot as a box laid over the right of the button rather than inside it, since a button cannot hold another button, so whatever stands there is in front of the row for as far as it reaches. The count beside Tasks is a label rather than something to press, and it was eating every click that landed on it: the row it is drawn on did nothing, which reads as a number that is somehow not part of the row it sits in. The slot wears `pointer-events-none` and `NewPage` wears `pointer-events-auto`, the same rule the Scribe pill's margin holds. Nothing about it can be seen in a suite, since jsdom does not hit test, so `tests/sidebar-probe.test.ts` holds the pair of classes that decide it.
- The rail ends on the way to a crew you are not in yet, the way the toolbox ends its own row on the slot that opens the builder. It is one button rather than two, since opening a folder and joining with a link are one errand with two ways through it, and both rows wear the words the way in uses. Joining was on the way in and nowhere else before this, so a link somebody sent you meant leaving the project you were in to paste it. A join that does not happen is said on the card the link was typed on, since that is where the person is and the link is still in front of them.
- A place can be called what you call it, from the right click that already takes it off the list. It is what the rail says from then on, which is the whole of why it is there: a crew somebody invited you to stands in the list as the address it was joined at, and an address is not a thing anybody recognizes at a glance. The row still says underneath what it really is, the whole path for a project and the address for a crew, or a name of your own is a name that hides where the work is being done. It is yours rather than the crew's, held in `localStorage` beside the order the projects stand in and never sent, so two people in one crew each call it what they like. `placeNames.ts` is where one is kept and `placesOf` is the one place a name decides what a row says, so the way in and the rail read the same list. A name blanked gives the real one back, which the card says without a word by standing the real name in as the placeholder.
- **A project is moved up the list by taking a copy of it rather than by pushing the row along.** What was picked up stays where it is and goes faint, a copy of it travels with the pointer, and a hairline with a dot at its head stands in the gap the drop would land in. Nothing in the list steps aside for it: the line is what says where it lands, and rows opening a slot as well is the same thing said twice. A tab in the side panel still carries itself, which is what a tab strip does everywhere, so `carry` on `useReorder` is what tells the two apart rather than the axis.
- **The pointer is what says where a carried one lands, and the box is what says it for one that travels.** A tab really moves with the pointer, so its own leading edge is what meets the one beside it and `landing` reads that edge. A project does not move at all, so there is no edge to read: the box left behind is as tall as the project and every thread under it, and its bottom edge stands a long way under the pointer, so the line lands a whole row before the cursor reaches the gap and the taller the project the further ahead it fires. `landingAt` is the pointer's own answer and it asks the plain question, which half of a row the cursor is really in.
- What is in hand is the row itself rather than a drawing of one, which is what `PlaceFace` is: the mark and the name, drawn once and worn in both places. It hugs what it holds rather than standing the width of the list, since it is a thing being carried and not a row that has left its slot.
- The line is a hairline and it is white. White is the single action color and a drop is the action, so it is white at full strength, and at two pixels across a column that narrow the same white reads as a rule cutting the list in half. The dot at its head is what gives it presence instead.
- What was picked up goes well under the opacity the rows around it rest at. A project that is not the one you are in already sits at 45, so a dim a little under that is a row that looks like every other quiet row, where what is left behind has to read as the hole the thing came out of.
- The gap between projects is the row's own rather than padding each one carries, or there is no gap for the line to stand in the middle of: `gapOf` reads it off the boxes the way `room` does, so nothing is told the number twice.
- The line stands in the list and what is in hand stands in the body. The list clips whatever runs past its own edge, and the line has to scroll with what it is pointing between, so neither one can be where the other is. Both are placed on the commit that mounts them rather than on the frame after, or the first thing anybody sees of a drag is the pill standing in the corner of the window on its way to the pointer.
- The list stops answering the pointer for the length of a drag and the cursor is the window's, so nothing lights up under a drag that is only passing over it.
- **A menu that stands beside the row it was opened from is reached across a gap, and the gap is what closes it.** The card stands past the row's right edge with room between them, so the pointer aiming at the card is over neither one for a moment, and a row that shuts its menu the instant it is left shuts it on the way there. It happened most of the time and not every time, which is what made it read as flakiness rather than as a rule: a fast hand moves in one step from the row into the card and is never in the gap at all, and a slow one lands in it. Leaving is forgiven for `REACH_MS` instead, and the card holds the menu while the pointer is in it, so reaching for a row is one gesture rather than a race. Both halves are needed. The grace alone lets go of a menu somebody is still reading, and the card alone never gets the chance, since the pointer has already crossed the gap to reach it.
- **A press pins it, and until then it belongs to the pointer.** Hovering opens it and leaving takes it away, which is right for a menu somebody is passing over, and wrong the moment they have chosen it: a menu that only ever follows the pointer cannot be left standing while somebody looks at what is under it. Pressed, the pointer leaving does nothing and the way out is a press outside, Escape, a row, or the row again. `useHoverMenu` is the whole of it and it is one hook rather than one of these written per menu, since `SubMenu` had the same gap and the same missing press.
- **The press that opens a popover placed at a point was a press outside it.** A `Popover` given `at` has no anchor to compare a click against, so it treated everything outside its own card as outside, the row that opened it included: the press shut it and the click after reopened it, which reads as a menu that flickers and cannot be pinned, and as a second press that reopens rather than closes. `anchor` is what whatever opened it hands over, and presses inside that are inside. It cannot be worked out from where the popover sits in the tree, which is what the anchored path already does: a menu opened at a point stands inside whatever drew it, and for a context menu that is the whole canvas, so every click on the page would count as a click inside the menu.
- **The rail is open while the pointer is on it or something it opened is standing over the page.** The menu is not inside the rail, so reaching into it is leaving the rail, and the rail slid away under a menu hanging off one of its own rows. `near` and `over` are those two, `peeking` is either of them, and `hold` is what the menu says about itself for as long as it stands. It is the same thing the corner does in holding the hover it did not start. `peek(false)` still runs its grace while a menu is held, so the rail goes when the menu does rather than a moment before it, and `hold(false)` never closes anything on its own: the pointer may be resting on the row that just closed it, which is what `near` is there to answer.
- `tests/crews.test.ts` holds two windows in two projects in one process, `tests/sidebar-probe.test.ts` holds the hover, the press and the panel changing hands, the reach across the gap, the pin, the second press and the rail held open under a standing menu, `tests/sidebar-drag-probe.test.ts` holds the copy, the line and everything the drop takes off with it, and `tests/active-threads.test.ts` holds the fold.

## Where a crew lives

Opening a folder is the only way in, and there is no host and join to choose between any more. Every session is the same session: chat, agents, docs, boards, the toolbox, the music and the games, served over the same ws server. What changes is where the history is written and who can reach the socket, and those are two separate answers rather than one mode.

- A project is asked one question, the first time it is opened, and never again. In the project puts `.crew` in the folder, where it goes out with the repo and everyone who has the project gets the crew. In the Crew app puts the same `.crew` beside the app, so nothing is ever written into the project. Each answer is written from where the work is kept rather than from what the choice is not, so the pair reads as two places rather than as one place and its opposite, and both say the same three things, the chat, the docs and the designs, so the one word between them is where they live. `projectPlan` in `src/main/session.ts` is what the app asks before it opens anything, and a folder it already knows comes back with the answer it was given, so nothing is asked twice.
- `CrewHome` in `src/shared/project.ts` is that answer, and `Store` is handed a root rather than building one. Everything downstream hangs off `this.root`, so docs, boards, attachments and music follow without knowing which of the two they are in.
- A crew kept on this machine is keyed on the repo's first commit, never on where the folder happens to sit. `projectKey` reads `git rev-list --max-parents=0 HEAD`, so moving a project or cloning it again finds the same history, and a folder with no commit falls back to a hash of its path and is remembered from then on.
- Git runs when the project has git, because Push and Pull are somebody's own code and their own button. Only a crew that lives in the folder starts the loop, so a project kept on this machine is never committed to under anyone and no prompt waits on a sync. That is `auto` in `startHost`, and it is what `synced` says.
- A folder with no git in it is somewhere to work. It opens, on this machine, with nothing to sync. Nothing is turned away at the door: `listRepoFiles` already walks a folder by hand when git fails, and `GitSync.usable()` already stands down.
- Multiplayer never needed git. Whoever joins is handed the whole session in the welcome and everything after it comes over the wire, and attachments and music come over http from the host, so a crew that syncs nothing is still a crew. Git only moves code.
- Inviting people is the listener moving and nothing else. `setShared` closes the socket and opens it again on the same port, `0.0.0.0` instead of `127.0.0.1`, and the session, its code, its history and everyone in it stay exactly where they are. The only thing anybody sees is their own socket coming back a moment later, which it does on its own. Never remake the session to share it.
- Sharing is off for a crew kept on this machine and on for one that lives in the folder, and either way it is one item in the profile menu. Turning it on copies the link in the same breath, because those are one action and not two.
- The way in is a list of places rather than a form. Every project you have opened and every crew you have joined stand in one list, newest first, because picking one is the same choice whichever it is. `placesOf` in `src/renderer/src/views/home/place.ts` is that list and `PlaceRow` is the row, and where a project keeps its crew is the mark on the left rather than a word.
- Under the list are the only two ways to reach a new one: open a folder, or join with a link. Each screen the two of them lead to is a screen inside the same column, carried by `ScreenSwap` the way the music panel carries its own, so nothing opens a second box to aim at.
- The way back and who you are are the window's own corners rather than rows in the column, the way the top bar holds them once you are in: the back button on the left, past the traffic lights on a Mac with the same `mac:pl-[64px]` the top bar wears, and your face on the right. Both are `app-no-drag`, since the band they stand in is what the window is dragged by. Standing in the column they were rows the screen had to be laid out around, and every screen was pushed off centre by a chevron above it.
- Your name is asked once, on the way in, and after that it is the face in that corner. Nothing can be opened without one, so an empty name takes you to that screen rather than putting a line of red under the button.
- The one question a project is asked stands as two options side by side rather than as a column of rows, because it is a choice between two places and a column reads as a list. It is the only screen wider than the rest, which is `WIDTH` in `Home.tsx` beside `DEPTH`. Nothing stands under the two of them. A footnote there is a line about a choice already made on the cards, and one that is true of both answers made the two look different where they are the same.
- **You go by one name, and every place is opened under it.** A row still remembers what you were called there, and that used to be what it opened under, which meant the name screen was a dead end: a name set there was written over by the first row anybody pressed, and the crew you came back to had you as whoever you were the first time. Two Crews on one machine are two people only while each of them opens under its own name, so the host has one member a name and neither app can quietly take the other's. Renaming yourself in a session is the same act as setting it on the way in, so it is written down as the name you go by and the next place you open opens under it. The name is still handed to the join rather than read back off state, since state is a render behind, which is the same mistake the GIF picker's send guard is written to avoid.
- **A run from source keeps a profile of its own, and that is `app.setPath('userData', ...)` rather than a state folder beside the shipping one.** `runtimeStateDir` already put the saved session, the agents and the projects of a `yarn dev` run under `dev/`, and it was half the answer: the Chromium profile stayed where it was, so `yarn dev` and `yarn start` shared one `Local Storage`, and the name you go by lives in it. Two Electron processes on one profile is a leveldb lock, so whichever came second wrote a name that never landed, and both apps joined the same crew as the same person however many times either of them was renamed. The whole profile moves to the same folder Crew's own state was already in, so nothing is migrated and a run from source is a separate user with a separate name.
- Taking a place off the list is a right click on it. A button fading in over the arrow was a second control standing on top of a row that has one thing to press, and it cost the arrow that says where the row goes. A project and a crew somebody invited you to both go, by folder and by link, so no row on the list is one you are stuck with.

## A crew in a repo of its own

A project can be open to everyone and its crew not be. The code stays where it is and `.crew` moves into a repo only the crew can reach, so cloning gives a passer by the project and no chat. It is not a third answer to where a crew lives: it is a crew kept on this machine with a repo behind it.

- The address rides in the project, in `.crew.json`, one field and nothing else. No branch, no key and nobody's name, because anything more is something two machines can disagree about. It cannot live in the repo it points at, which is the whole reason it is in the project rather than in the crew.
- An address is not a secret. What decides who may read a crew is the repo it names, so anyone who clones is handed the way in and GitHub is the door. Knowing where a door is has never opened one, which is what makes this different from a key committed beside the thing it locks: everyone who cloned would have the ciphertext and the key together, and that is obfuscation rather than privacy.
- Every member commits and pushes, so every member is a replica and every member needs write. The repo's own list of people is the crew, GitHub administers it, and there is nothing for anybody to lose. A wrapped key per member would work and it would mean publishing a public key, waiting to be re-wrapped, and rotating the whole thing to remove somebody.
- `cleanCrewRemote` in `src/shared/project.ts` is the one place an address is decided, so the same rule holds wherever one is written down, read back or pushed to. The ssh forms are turned over to https, because an address is written for everybody else and a machine without that key is locked out of one that works fine for the rest of them.
- Not a submodule. A submodule pins a commit, and this one moves every five seconds.
- Opening a project that names a crew fetches it before anything opens, and a fetch that fails is said out loud. Nothing here ever falls back to a crew of this machine's own, because that is two histories under one name and it looks exactly like it worked. Starting one anyway is a press on the screen that says so, which is that answer picked on purpose rather than fallen into.
- Why it did not work is never guessed at. A repo that is not yours and one that is not there answer the same way on purpose, and a machine with no way out to the internet is a third nobody can tell from the other two, so all three read as a crew this computer cannot reach.
- A project that names a crew has already answered the one question a project is asked, so somebody who cloned it is never asked where to keep a crew they are about to be handed.
- Two loops run side by side, the crew's own and the project's own, and a prompt waits on whichever of them is running. Both are asked for by what is going right now rather than by what was going when the session opened, so turning one on mid-session is the whole of turning it on.
- Where a crew is kept and whether the code goes out with it are two answers rather than one. A crew that rides in the folder carries the code because it is the same repo. One kept outside it carries the code only where somebody said so, which is the switch on the People page, and it is off until it is asked for: a folder opened privately is still never committed to under anyone. The answer is the project's own and it is remembered, so it is asked once. Both ends have to have it on for work to travel both ways, since each machine only ever pushes and pulls for itself.
- `.crew/.gitattributes` is the union merge rule, written by the `Store` and standing inside the crew rather than at the top of a repo. That is what makes one rule cover a crew that rides in somebody's project and one that has a repo to itself. Nothing wrote it before this, so every crew living in a folder was taking a conflict on every crossing append and only the test helper was ever writing one.
- An empty repo is the only safe place to put a crew. Two crews pushed to one address is two histories that never met, and nothing here can merge that, so a remote with anything in it is refused and a push that fails takes the remote back off rather than leaving one pointing nowhere.
- A pass that gets nothing out is ordinary once. Three in a row is history piling up on one machine, and that reaches the screen as a toast rather than a line in a log nobody reads.
- Joining over a link needs none of it. Somebody who takes a link is a guest of a host and is handed the whole session over the wire, so two ways in stay two ways in: clone the project and be a replica, or take a link and be a guest.
- Moving a crew out of a folder does not unpublish anything. Every message already pushed is in that repo's history forever, and moving only decides where the next one goes. Taking the old ones back is a rewrite and a force push, not a setting.

## The crew command

`crew` in a terminal opens a crew on the folder you are standing in. It comes with the app, and one press on the This computer page in the settings puts it on PATH. `bin/crew.mjs` is the whole of what it does: plain node, no dependencies and no build step, so the one command works from a checkout and from the installed app alike.

- The folder is the one you are in unless you name another, and a relative one is worked out from where the command was run rather than from wherever the app happens to start. `--name` is who you are, `--in-project` and `--in-app` are the two answers to where the crew lives, `--share` and `--no-share` are who can reach it, and `--join <link>` joins somebody else's crew with this folder as the place your agents work. `--help` and `--version` answer for themselves and open nothing.
- Where a crew lives and who can reach it is the host's own, so `--join` is refused either of them rather than quietly ignoring them. Everything else is the same question the app asks the first time a project is opened and remembers from then on, which is why giving it again on a folder you have opened before changes nothing.
- What the command worked out rides in one flag, `--crew-open=` with the request as JSON, rather than as the row of flags the app would then have to learn the meaning of a second time. The command owns what its own words mean and the app only says whether it can use them: `cleanOpenRequest` in `src/shared/cli.ts` keeps the folder, the name, the two answers and the link and drops the rest, so nothing on a command line can reach further into the app than that. A plain node script cannot import the app's TypeScript, so the flag is the one thing written down twice, and `tests/crew-cli.test.ts` sends a real request through both halves and holds them to agreeing.
- A window launched for a folder opens that folder, so main leaves the last session where it is while a request is waiting. Resumed as well, the two would race and the one that lost would be the folder somebody just asked for.
- The renderer acts on it rather than main, because the name you go by lives in the window and the one question a project is asked is a screen in `Home`. `follow` walks the same path a press on a row does: no name goes to the name screen and comes back to the request when it has one, a project the app has never opened goes to the question, and a known one opens straight away. The request is handed over once and cleared, or leaving a session would land you back in the folder the terminal asked for.
- Which Crew it opens is `CREW_APP` if that is set, then the Crew it came with, then the installed app, then the one built in this checkout. Nothing is looked up on PATH, since the command is called `crew` too and a Linux install puts its own `crew` there.
- Every run is an app of its own, which is what lets the command open two folders as two crews. Nothing here takes a lock, so several windows on one folder is still supported.
- The command comes with the app. `extraResources` carries `bin` into `Resources/cli`, so what ships is the file in the repo and nothing about it is generated at build time. The version is read out of the app's own archive, because the command stands beside it and the thing running the command is the app, which reads into its own asar. Never copy `package.json` in beside it to save that lookup: electron-builder takes a file named in `extraResources` out of the archive it would otherwise be packed into, and a build whose asar has no `package.json` fails its own sanity check. Only a real `electron-builder --dir` says so, and every suite passes either way.
- Somebody who downloads Crew has no node, so `bin/crew` is what ships on PATH rather than `bin/crew.mjs`: a shell script that follows its own link home, finds the app it came with, and runs that app as node with `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`. Electron is a node, which is the whole reason this needs nothing installed. It sets `CREW_APP` to the app it came out of unless one is already set, so a command from `~/Applications` opens that Crew rather than whichever one is found first.
- That variable rides in the environment, so `childEnv` in `bin/crew.mjs` takes it off before the app is spawned. Passed on, the Crew being opened comes up as a node process with no window and nobody ever sees it.
- Putting it on PATH is one press, on the This computer page in the settings. It is a link into `/usr/local/bin`, made by the app where it may and asked for with a password where it may not: `osascript` on a Mac, `pkexec` elsewhere. A password dialog somebody dismissed is a decision rather than a failure, so nothing is said about it. The row turning over is what says it worked, so nothing else does either.
- The rules are in `src/shared/crewCommand.ts` and the doing is in `src/main/crew-command.ts`, because the rail in the settings reads the first one and nothing in the renderer may reach for node. That is also why the paths there are joined by hand.
- Windows is the one place it does not go. There is no folder every shell already reads, so the file ships and `commandScript` comes back null, which main answers as `off` and the row is left out. Nothing in the renderer decides this: it asks the machine and draws whatever it is told.
- It still reaches PATH from a checkout, with `npm link` or a symlink of `bin/crew.mjs`, and the `bin` entry in `package.json` is what npm reads. A build run from source links that same file, so `yarn start` has the command the shipping app has.
- `tests/crew-command.test.ts` runs the real launcher, through a real link, against a stand-in app that says what it was handed. A fake cannot stand in for a shell script, and every unit around it passes whether or not the thing ever finds the app.

## Commands

A word with a slash in front of it, lifted out of the box onto a chip beside the plus and sent as a field of its own. `commands.ts` is the one table of them, and `where` on each row says which composer offers it: the chat's open a thread, and a thread's are about the thread already open.

- Neither set is offered where the other belongs. `commandsIn` is what a composer asks for, and both `commandTyped` and `slashCandidates` are handed that list rather than reaching for the table themselves, so the menu and the word typed out can never disagree about what there is.
- A thread holds one at a time, because they are one choice about the one message being written. Picking another takes the place of the one before it, and what stands is read back off what the thread can still do rather than off what was picked, since the run it was picked against can end while the message is still being typed.
- `/steer` and `/queue` are only there while there is a turn to go into. Without one every message queues and runs, so offering either would promise a choice that was never there, and the pair is left out rather than greyed. `live` on the row is what says so, so what needs a turn is a word on the command rather than a list of names written out again in the filter.
- `/steer` is the default said out loud, since a message for the agent already running goes into that run whenever it can take one. `/queue` is the override, and it is the only way there is to hold a message back until the turn ends.
- `/btw` is a conversation beside a thread rather than in it. It opens in the panel, reads the thread for whatever the question needs, and says nothing back into it, so the work going on there is not interrupted and nobody scrolls past a question they never asked.
- One is a ghost, so it runs on your own machine and nowhere else, none of it is written down, and it goes with the window. A thread somebody else's agent is on is still one you can ask about: one of yours answers it, which is `startAside`.
- `aside` on the thread is the thread it was asked from, and `buildPrompt` reads that thread and then the talk beside it. They stand as two blocks rather than as one run of lines: a question and its answer are turns of their own, and after a few of them there is nothing to say where the thread ended and the conversation about it began. The thread is read again on every turn rather than remembered, so an answer keeps up with work that has carried on since. It answers itself and sends no work out, because helpers on a thread nobody can see is work nobody asked for.
- It is not the crew's work, so it is no card in the chat, no row in the task list, and no toast when it lands: the panel that opened to answer it is already on the screen.
- It is two way. `AsideView` is an ordinary thread in a panel, composer and all, so the next question goes where the last one was answered: an answer is rarely the end of it, and starting again in a tab of its own is being told to explain the whole thing a second time. Nothing typed there can reach the thread it is about, whatever is written in it, since it is a message into a ghost of its own and the thread is only ever read.
- A question asked from the thread's own composer opens a tab, so one asked while an answer is still being read leaves that answer where it is. Two of those are two conversations, and each one goes on where it stands.
- `/fork` is the thread so far, carried on in one of its own. What was said up to that moment is what the agent reads, and the thread it came from is left exactly as it was, so a good place can be gone on from several ways at once and none of them stands on the others. Nothing is ever said back into it.
- Nothing about a fork is copied. `forkedFrom` and `forkedAt` on `thread.started` are the whole of it, and `forkContext` folds the talk back out of the events up to that moment, so it replays for free, costs the log nothing, and a fork of a fork reads the whole line it came down and none of what any of those threads went on to do afterwards.
- It reads as one run of talk rather than as two blocks, which is the opposite of what an aside does and for the opposite reason. An aside is a conversation about a thread, and a fork is that conversation carrying on, so nothing in the prompt says a fork was made at all.
- It carries the mode, the plan and the board with it for the same reason. A fork of a ghost thread is a ghost, or a hidden thread leaks the moment somebody forks it, and a fork of a helper's thread is a thread of its own rather than a second helper, since nobody sent it out.
- Whoever asks for a fork names the id it opens under, in `forkId` on `chat.send`, the way whoever makes a playlist names that. The window that asked opens it the moment it lands, since a message was just typed into it, and no other window moves: everyone else has the card in the chat, the way they do for any thread.
- A fork reads as a thread that starts mid conversation, since what came before it is in the prompt and not on the screen, so `ForkedFrom` stands at the head of it and names the thread it carried on from. That is the way back as well as the one thing the screen cannot say on its own: whoever made it knows, and everybody else opened a card that answers a conversation they cannot see.
- `/fallback` names who takes over when a run in this thread ends badly. A CLI that has run out fails the run and says so, so an agent that failed and an agent that is out of tokens arrive at the same place, `agent.end` with `ok: false`, and there is no second path to build. Nothing here reads the usage bars, which are a guess about the next run rather than an answer about this one.
- Nothing reads it until a run falls over, so a thread with one named queues and steers exactly as it did before. Setting it is a send of its own, answered and returned before anything is enqueued, so it never competes for the queue with the message written after it.
- A run somebody stopped never falls over. `stopped` is already how the host tells a decision from a fault, and reading a decision as a fault hands the work straight back to whoever just called it off.
- One hop per message. The retry carries `fellBack`, so a fallback that fails too is the end of it rather than a pair of agents handing one message back and forth, which is the rule the toolbox chain already holds.
- The thread moves with it, so the next message goes to the agent that is now on it. That is `thread.agent` with no name on it, which is why `byName` there is optional: a hand-off nobody made has nobody to name, and the transcript draws that case as its own line rather than as somebody handing the thread on.
- A fallback nobody is running takes nothing. Pushed onto a thread whose agent is not here, the message would sit at the head of the queue holding up everything behind it, and the failure is already on the screen saying what happened.
- It is named by mentioning them, which is the one way the app already names an agent in a composer, and naming nobody takes it off. That one send is an empty box, so it is the single exception to nothing typed being nothing sent.
- Nothing is emitted into the thread as a message when one is set, because an arrangement is not something somebody said. The note in the transcript is the record and the only place a standing fallback is visible, which is right: it matters on the day something breaks, and a permanent row in the header would be chrome earning nothing on all the others.
- Messages already queued behind the failed one still name the agent that failed, so each falls over once before moving. That is self-correcting and costs a run each. Moving the whole queue would overrule somebody who named an agent by hand.
- **`/goal` is what somebody wrote and nothing else, and that one sentence is the whole of why goals work at all.** A run is a prompt built around a message: the thread so far, and then the words about helpers, about showing a page, about the board and about what the crew has learned, which are 7,379 characters of curl on their own. The condition was that whole prompt for as long as `/goal` existed here, so the condition went past what Claude will take on the first message of the first thread anybody tried it in, and the CLI refused the run before it had done a thing: no turn, no step, an answer back in under a second. It never worked once, on any thread, which is exactly what it was reported as. `goalCondition` in `src/shared/goal.ts` is the rule and `GOAL_LIMIT` is the ceiling, and the host is where it is applied, because the host is the one side holding the message on its own rather than the prompt built around it.
- The condition is a live stop condition rather than a line in a prompt, so it goes over the wire as one: `goal` on the prompt message is the words, where it used to be a flag. That is what lets each CLI say it in the shape its own transport has, and every one of them had it wrong in its own way. Claude has a real `/goal`, which takes its whole message as the condition, so the condition is a message of its own and the prompt is the one after it, in the same run. Codex has `thread/goal/set`, which was being handed the prompt as its objective. Local was handed the plan brief, which says to change nothing, so a goal run there was the opposite of a goal run. Kimi and Grok have no goal at all and were being sent a `/goal` nothing parses, so they are told in words instead.
- A CLI with no command for one gets `goalBrief`, at the head of the prompt. Anything with a real command for it says so through its own dialog and never through the body, which is `goalCommand` and a dialog together: one without the other is a condition with nowhere to go, so the words are used for that too rather than the goal being dropped.
- A condition longer than the ceiling is cut at a word, and nothing is said about it. The whole of what somebody wrote is still in the prompt right underneath, so what is lost is the tail of the finish line rather than the tail of the ask, and a run that happens is worth more than one refused over a length nobody can see.
- `tests/goal.integration.test.ts` holds the condition being the message and never the prompt, the two messages Claude opens on, and the cut. `yarn goal-check` is the only thing that can see the rest of it: it runs a real turn against the real CLI with a prompt well past the ceiling and a condition that is not met by what the prompt asks for, so a goal that never landed reads as a file with one line missing. Every suite here was green the whole time this was dead.

## The default agent

An agent standing on the chat's own composer, so a person working with one of them all day writes what they want rather than their name and then what they want. It is picked under the plus, on a screen inside it the way the GIFs are, and it wears a chip beside the plus until it is taken off.

- **It is a standing choice rather than a command, which is the whole of how it behaves.** A command chip is about the message being written and goes with the send. This one is about every message after it, so it survives the send, the reload and the app being shut, and the only way off it is the X on it. Written as a command it would be a thing to pick again on every message, which is the typing it exists to save.
- Nothing about it is on the wire. `aimedAt` on `sendChat` is a door the app already had, for a control that knows the agent by id rather than by what somebody spelled, so the host is handed the same thing an `@` hands it and nothing about the message, the thread or the log knows the difference.
- **Naming somebody outright beats it.** `aimOf` in `src/shared/llm.ts` is that rule and it sits beside the one that reads mentions out of the words, since the two are the same question answered in order: what somebody wrote now is the whole of who they meant.
- **An agent that is not here takes nothing, which is what writing its name already does.** The words stay a message in the chat rather than opening a thread nobody is on the other end of. The other answer, handing it to whoever is here, belongs to a tool pressed with nobody watching, and this is somebody at a keyboard with the chip in front of them.
- The chip says whether they are here, on the pet's own dot, so the one thing the screen can say about that is said there rather than in a line of type.
- **It is yours rather than the crew's**, so it is kept on this machine in `state/defaultAgent.ts` and never sent: two people in one crew each aim their own composer. It is kept on disk rather than in the window, since a renderer reloads whenever the project is edited under it, which is most of an afternoon with an agent working.
- **It is one standing choice rather than one per project.** An agent is the machine's own, kept in that machine's `agents.json` and pooled into whichever crew it is joined to, so an id names the same agent wherever it turns up and there is nothing about a project for it to belong to. Keyed by the place it also went off on its own, which is the whole of why it was: a crew joined over a link is keyed by that link, and the port in one moves whenever the host reopens, so the choice quietly came off a project nobody had left.
- The name on the chip is read off the crew's own list rather than out of what was written down, so a rename reads as the name they carry today. An id the crew has nothing for draws nothing rather than being forgotten: the agents arrive with the welcome, so a window still connecting would take the choice off on the way in, and a crew that never had them is one to walk back out of with the choice still standing.
- What was written down before this was one agent per place, so it is read as nobody rather than as an id. A record that cannot be read is the one that stands forever, since the chip it would put on the screen is the only way to take one off and an id nobody answers to draws no chip.
- The picker is the agents that are here, the same list the `@` menu offers, and with nobody here the row is left out rather than opening on an empty card. It has no way back and no heading, the rule the GIFs already hold: the rows it was opened from are one press away again, and a row of faces one press after asking for one says what it is.
- **Asking for it is a movement rather than a cut.** The rows give way to the faces the way one screen of the music panel gives way to the next, on the same `ScreenSwap` in the same card, so the plus is one box that grows to whatever it is holding and the GIFs travel for free beside it. Read the plain way it was two different popovers blinking one into the other, and a screen inside a card that arrives with no movement says it came from somewhere else.
- Only the chat has one. A thread already has an agent, and a message in it goes there whatever anybody picked.
- The chip is `ComposerChip`, which is what a command chip is made of too, so the standing one and the passing ones are one control at one height. It stands first in the row, so a command arriving beside it never moves it out from under the pointer.
- `tests/composer-default-agent-probe.test.ts` holds the picking, the chip standing through a send and into another project, a written name beating it, an agent that is not here taking nothing, the choice read back off this machine, the older record reading as nobody, and the two screens standing in one card that carries them. `yarn plus-look` is the only thing that can see the rest of it: it stands the real menu up in a real window, presses through both screens and reads the box back every frame, so a card that cuts rather than travels and an edge that walks under it are things somebody can see rather than things a green suite hides.

## Helpers

An agent splits work off to a helper and carries on with its own. It makes one up as it needs it, a short name and the whole of what the piece is, so the shape of the work decides how it is divided rather than a list somebody wrote last week. `/agents/spawn` on the host's own http is the whole of the way in, and every provider gets it at once because it is a curl line in the preamble rather than a flag on a CLI.

- A run of one is an ordinary thread with a parent. That is what gives it steps, thinking, diffs, tokens, cancel, steer and a place in the log without any of it being invented again, and it is what lets you open one in the side panel and type into it.
- The parent never blocks. It sends out, gets an id back, and keeps working. When a helper finishes the host pushes the answer into whatever the parent is doing: into the live turn through `sendSteer` where the agent is steerable, and as a turn of its own where it is not. `subagentReturn` in `src/server/session.ts` is that one decision.
- The handoff is not a chat message. `silent` rides on the steer and on the queued prompt, so no `message.route` is written pointing at nothing and nobody scrolls past a helper's raw answer pasted in as though it had been said. The chip is the record, and the chip opens onto the whole thing.
- Returns coalesce over `RETURN_COALESCE_MS`. Three helpers finishing in a breath are one interruption, because three steers into a live turn is three times the model is pulled off what it was doing. One line each under one heading, and `returnText` writes the whole of it on the host, since a breath of returns has no single name to frame it under.
- `subagent.ended` carries `ok` and `ms` and no text. The answer is already in the log as that thread's own `agent.end`, and writing it twice is a log that doubles for nothing.
- What the parent says and what the screen says are two different reports. The parent's line is intent, one line about who it sent where, and it streams as it is written. The chips are truth: each reads its own thread's state and its own latest step, so the row reports whether or not the agent ever mentions it. The line has to say what the screen cannot.
- Only `RunStatus` spins. A working chip lights its mark and pulses it, the way a live step does, or four helpers under one run is a field of spinners.
- A thread with helpers still out is not ready for review. `threadWorking` counts its children, or a parent whose own turn ended lands on the review badge and raises a finished toast while three agents are still going on its behalf.
- A helper is nobody's row in the feed and nobody's task in the panel. It reads inside the thread that sent it, which is where somebody would go looking for it.
- Its two events are the one pair that do not belong to the thread they name. `subagent.started` and `subagent.ended` carry the helper's own thread and read on `parentThreadId`, so `eventsOfThread` is what every view gathers a thread's events with rather than a filter written out again at each one. Read the plain way, the chip lands inside the helper pointing at itself and never lands in the thread that has something to say about it, which is a spawn that runs, returns, and leaves no mark on screen anywhere. `tests/subagent-chip-probe.test.ts` holds it.
- The mark is a picture, seeded by the run's own id, drawn the way the music covers are: the same shader, the same palette, seen through a shape of its own. `subagentShape.ts` holds the numbers and `SubagentMark.tsx` draws, the same split the covers hold. A lobed mark is a pointy form and takes the live area the way the star does; a disc and a hexagon sit on the circle keyline and a square on the square, so a flower and a disc carry the same weight in a row. `MIN_WAIST` is where a rosette stops being one shape and starts reading as scattered dots at 18 across. `yarn covers` draws every mark at the three sizes against both surfaces, because a generator like this cannot be judged from its numbers.
- Inside a helper's own thread the work is the helper's, so it stands under the name the agent made it up with and the mark drawn from its run's id, rather than under the machine that happened to run it. That is `as` on `buildThread`, and what it hands each of the agent's own items is a name and a seed and no agent id at all: nothing looks a helper up as a member or as an agent, so the hover card and the presence dot quietly stand down rather than naming somebody who is not there. What a person typed into the thread is still theirs. Read the plain way, every helper in the panel is a row of the same pet under the same name, and the one thing the screen is there to say, which of them you are reading, is the one thing it does not.
- The panel says a helper once. The way back and what it is doing stand on one row, and nothing under it repeats them: a mark, a name and a state over a transcript that already carries the words, the run status and the files is the same thing told three times down a 480 panel. The tab pill carries the name, `ScreenSwap` carries the row, and the list underneath is one line a helper.
- A thread's helpers are one tab, and everything opens it: a chip, the row under the chips, and the button in the thread's own header. `parentThreadId` on the tab is what makes that one thing rather than three, so `openSubagent` is handed the thread that sent the helper as well as the helper. Opening a second one takes the place of the first and the list is one press back, rather than a row of near identical pills down the top of the panel, and it is what lets the header button say whether the helpers are the thing being shown.
- A helper's own helpers stand in that same list rather than in a list of their own. A helper can send one out itself, and what it sent is still work the thread in the chat sent out, so `useSubagentRuns` walks the whole family and the list is the one place all of it is. Which helper sent which is the order and the indent, each one standing under the one that sent it, so the list says it without a word: read as a flat row of subjects, three helpers deep is three lines that could have come from anywhere. A tab is keyed by the thread in the chat and never by the helper a chip happens to stand in, which is `rootThread` in front of `openSubagent`. Without it a chip inside a helper opened a second helpers tab, keyed to a thread nobody is in, so `leaveThread` took it down on the next move and the way back out of it was a list that had never held it.
- That tab stands only while you are in the thread that sent them, the way the plan and the board do, so what is left in the row is about the thread on the screen. `leaveThread` is that rule and it runs off `openThreadId` rather than off the thread's own things, since a helper is opened by hand and never opens itself, so nothing else would ever take one down. Every way back is a press: a chip, the row under the chips, the button in the header, or the panel's own list.
