- The chips arrive in the words they were sent under and scroll away with them, so a thread that has ever sent a helper out keeps a way in that stands still: a row in the side panel's own list, standing only for a thread that has sent one, wearing `GroupGlyph`, the mark a helper carries everywhere else in the panel.
- The panel never travels sideways. It is half the width the thread column is, so what stands past its edge is slack rather than something to go and read, and a bar under the words that scrolls to nothing is a control that does nothing. Anything in a transcript that really has more to the right owns a scroller of its own, a code fence, a diff, a terminal card and a table, so clipping the column costs none of it. The aside holds the same rule for the same reason.
- Every route names the promptId of the run asking, and the host takes it only while that prompt is one it is running right now. A board being drawn on is a different weight of thing from a shell running on somebody's laptop, so the live prompt is the credential: it costs one lookup and nothing has to be handed around. A helper is reachable only from the thread that sent it, however its id was come by.
- A CLI asked for by name is asked for. Nobody running it is a refusal rather than a quiet fall back to the parent's own, which would read as the request being ignored.
- What helpers may do on a machine belongs to whoever owns it, because a helper runs a real CLI there and spends their tokens on their files, which is why it stands on This computer rather than on the Crew side of the settings. Off means their agents never send work out and never take anybody else's. It lives in that window's `localStorage` and is said again on every connect, the way the volume is kept except this one has to reach the host to be worth anything. `FAN_LIMIT` is the crew's ceiling and `DEFAULT_FAN` is what a machine carries before anybody says otherwise.
- A helper inherits `ghost` from its parent without exception. A hidden thread whose children are not hidden is a hidden thread that leaks.
- Stopping a run stops the helpers it sent out, and a helper somebody stopped wakes nobody: its work was called off, so waking the parent would open a turn on a run that was just cancelled.
- `WAKE_LIMIT` sits beside `RUN_LIMIT` and `DEPTH_LIMIT`. Without it a parent that spawns on every wake is a loop the crew watches burn tokens all afternoon.
- A helper makes no sound at all, going out or coming home. It was a quiet pair of its own once, `helper.out` and `helper.home`, well back in the room and landing nowhere near the note a finish lands on, and being quiet was not enough: a crew of them out and back through an afternoon is a noise every few minutes about work nobody asked to hear about, over the top of the thread that sent them. The chips are on the screen already saying it, and they say it better, since each one reports its own state whether or not the agent ever mentions it. The helper's own `agent.end` is silent too, and that is a rule of its own in `soundFor` rather than something that falls out of the rest: read the plain way, a helper that stopped chimes in the voice that means the work somebody asked for is done.
- The one thing nothing here can merge is two agents editing one file at the same time. Half of that is words, in the preamble, which says to send out work that does not sit on what you are holding. The other half is the panel, which lists the files each helper changed.

## Showing a page

An agent puts something on the screen beside the chat: the file it has just written, the three it changed, the server it has started, or a site worth looking at. It is the browser and the file view the panel already holds, asked for from the outside, so nothing new is drawn and nothing is installed.

- `/page` on the host's own http is the whole of the way in, and `pagePreamble` in `src/shared/showPage.ts` is what an agent is told. It is written on the machine running the agent, the same reason the helpers and the board write theirs there: only that side knows the address it reaches the session at. Every call names the promptId of the run asking, which is the credential the board and the helpers are reached on.
- Nothing about it is state the host holds. The call is one `page.shown` on the thread, so it replays for free, and a ghost thread's page reaches the one window that opened it without any of that being said again here.
- It opens for whoever is reading that thread and nowhere else. Anyone else has the row in the thread, and a panel taken over by work somebody is not watching is the one way this becomes a thing people turn off.
- A file opens in the file view and only an address opens in the browser. `openShown` is that fork, off `filePathOf`, and it is what makes this any file rather than an html one: a stylesheet is lit, a picture is drawn, a log is read. Opened as a raw `file://` tab, everything that is not a page came out as text nobody could read, and half of them downloaded instead.
- A file is read the way it is written to be read. An html one and a markdown one open as the page they are, with the words they are written in one press away, and everything else opens as itself. That is `showFile` in `state/browser.ts` rather than the `openFile` a file link in a step uses, which lands on the text: going to read what changed and being handed the finished thing are two different errands, and the whole of what was on the screen before this was the html an agent showed, drawn as a page. A tab already standing on the file reads it again, since what is being shown is almost always what the run has just written.
- The same file arrives from another machine under that machine's own path, so a file is placed before it is opened, through the `locate` the whole app already reads paths with. That is what turns it into a path in this project, and one that lands nowhere here is left alone rather than opened onto nothing: the row says it is somebody else's file, which is the one thing it can honestly say.
- One call may name several, up to `PAGE_LIMIT`, and they all open in the order they were named, with the first of them standing at the front. Opened from the back instead, the row of tabs reads backwards, and the row somebody put a thing in is the order they put it in. A call naming one bad address is refused whole rather than opening the rest, or a model watches for the file that quietly never came.
- The row is the way back to it once the run has scrolled away, so it carries the addresses rather than tabs that may have been closed since. Pressing it loads the page again rather than standing at what it looked like before the change being shown, which is `showPage` in `state/browser.ts` and the reload `BrowserTabView` does off its own `generation`. An address the webview has tidied is the same address, or every showing is a second tab.
- It is a step and reads as one: the same column, the same word, the same rhythm as everything else the run did. `PageRow` is drawn from `StepRow`'s own parts for that reason, and several fold into one row that opens the way a run of edits does. It was a pill before this, which is what a helper wears, so a run that showed a file read as a run that sent an agent out.
- It closes up against the steps either side of it, which is `promptId` on `page.shown` and the page counting as a step in `follows`. Left out of that rule it sat a whole gap clear of its neighbours, and a row standing alone in a run reads as something that happened beside the work rather than as part of it. Which run it belongs to is never left to the field alone, since no page shown before that field existed carries one and every row already written down would stand apart forever. `runOfPages` reads it off the log instead: the call is made from inside a turn, so the run open on the thread when the event landed is the run that made it, which is the order the log is written in rather than a guess at it. A page with no run open when it landed is the one that keeps the wider gap, and it has earned it.
- One mark, whatever it put on the screen. It was a page, a stack of pages or a globe depending on what was named, which is the row saying in a second language what the words beside it already say: the mark is the tile and the words are the file.
- Three ways to name one and no others, in `pageUrl`: a real address, a machine it is already serving on like `localhost:5173`, and a full path to a file. A person typing in the address bar gets the benefit of the doubt and lands on a search; an agent gets none of it, because what it writes is opened on every machine in the crew without anybody pressing anything. Anything else is refused in words, so a model reads what to write instead of watching a page it named quietly not appear.
- The title is the agent's own line about what to look at. One page it said nothing about is named by its file or its host, which is the shortest true thing either one can be called. Several are named by the count, since one file's name is not a line about three.

## Ghost threads

`/ghost` in the chat opens a thread that belongs to the window it was typed in. Nobody else sees it, none of it is written down, and it is gone when the window is. Everything else about it is an ordinary thread: an agent takes it, it queues and it steers, and the work it does to the project is real work on real files.

- It is held in memory on the host, keyed by the socket that opened it, the way a call is. Two windows on one folder are one member and two different people here, so the other one is not shown it either.
- Every event a thread raises carries its id, and that is what lets one place decide all of this: `emit` hands a ghost's event to that one socket and puts it on the thread's own transcript instead of on the log. The three thread-scoped messages that are not events, the steps, the tokens and the queue, go through `toThread` for the same reason. Never broadcast one of those by hand.
- That transcript is what the agent's next turn is built from. `threadContext` reads it rather than the session, so the thread has a memory without the session having one.
- A run in flight rides in the snapshot as well as in the log, so `pooled` leaves a ghost's run out of it. Anyone connecting mid-run would otherwise be handed its steps.
- A thread somebody else opened is not there to be written in, however its id was come by. `hiddenFrom` is that guard, on the chat, on the status and on the plan. Reading one is the same rule: a reply and a reaction have to find what they point at, and that was never written to the session, so `reactionTarget` searches the transcripts the asker can see and no others. Handed every transcript there is, a reply is a way to read a line out of somebody else's thread by naming what it points at.
- Busy is the one thing about a run that reaches everyone, so `statusOf` does not count a ghost's. An agent reading as busy with no thread anywhere to point at says a hidden one is going.
- A picture sent to one is held in memory on the host and answered from there, never saved beside the session. The machine running it has to put one on disk for the agent to read at all, so the prompt says it is a ghost's and the runner writes it outside the project and takes it away with the run. Without both halves the one part of a ghost thread the crew syncs is its pictures.
- The window going takes the thread with it: whatever it was running is stopped, its pictures are dropped and the transcript with them. The entry it stood in stays behind empty, because a run still coming back from a machine somewhere has to land somewhere sealed, or its last steps would be written down as an ordinary thread's.
- It only ever goes to an agent on your own machine. A prompt reaches whatever machine runs the agent and the CLI there keeps its own record of it, so anywhere else the thread is hidden in the app and sitting in somebody else's folder. Naming somebody else's agent says so, to the one who asked and nobody else, and inside a thread it reads the way naming an agent who is not here reads.
- The agent still runs a CLI, so what that CLI keeps for itself on your own machine is out of the app rather than out of the world.
- It wears a dashed stroke wherever it is drawn. On the card in the chat it wears the Ghost chip, beside the Plan chip if it is also a plan, and the dashed header stays over the composer inside the thread.
- Inside the thread it says Ghost mode on the head of the composer, which is `GhostBar`: an `ink-800` band standing behind the header with the mark and the words centered on it in white, tucked the way the queue above it is so only its head shows. It is nothing to press and nothing to hover, and it says what is hidden on the box it is typed in rather than over the thread.
- Arming ghost turns the composer over before a word is written: the box drops to the page's own surface and takes the dashed stroke, so what is about to be hidden looks it. It wears the same thing inside the thread, under the dashed header. The border stands in both states so nothing shifts when it goes on, and the patch the composer paints behind an emoji reads the same surface, or a solid grey square stands in the field.
- A command is not text. Typing `/ghost` and a space lifts it out of the box onto a chip beside the plus, and it is sent as a field of its own, so the same word written inside a sentence is only a word and nothing is ever cut out of what somebody wrote. `commands.ts` is the whole of what a command may be and `CommandChip` is what one wears, in the composer and on the card. The host reads the field and never the message. Inside a thread the field is ignored, the way the text was.
- The chip in the composer stands in the row the plus stands in and is that button's height and outline, because it is a control rather than a label. Backspace on an empty box takes the last one off.
- The plus offers no huddle while ghost is armed. A call is the whole crew by definition, so a way into one standing under a message nobody else will see is the one thing in that menu that contradicts the box it is in.

## Syncing

Every machine commits its whole working tree, integrates, and pushes on a loop, host and joiner alike. Agents on different machines write to the same branch at the same time, so `GitSync` in `src/server/git.ts` has three hard rules. Each one is here because work was destroyed without it.

- Never stash. Commit everything first, so nothing is sitting uncommitted when the sync integrates. Autostash stranded people's work in stashes nobody ever went back for.
- Never rebase on the automatic path. A rebase replays every local commit and rewrites the working tree once per commit, landing on top of files an agent is writing right now. Pull with `--no-rebase`. A merge leaves local commits alone and touches only what came in.
- Never revert. Taking back someone's edits defeats the point of pooling agents. When a conflict cannot resolve itself, back out of it, put the pending files back, and try again on the next pass.

Only `.crew/session.json` resolves itself, by keeping the local copy. Logs ending in `.jsonl` union merge through `.gitattributes`. Several windows can share one folder, so a lock file in `.git` keeps them off each other, and an interrupted merge or rebase is finished or backed out at the start of every pass.

The chat log is written in segments, and that is a memory rule rather than a tidiness one. It used to be one `.crew/chat.jsonl` that every commit rewrote whole, so a 23MB file went into the log again every thirteen seconds and the history ended up holding eleven thousand copies of it. Nothing about that is expensive until git repacks, and then it is: a repack redoes the delta search across every one of those copies, with a window that is unbounded by default and a thread per core, which on a laptop is tens of gigabytes and a machine that dies rather than a slow pass. `chatLog.ts` seals a segment at a megabyte and opens the next, so a commit only ever writes the tail. `.crew/chat.jsonl` is still read and is never written again, which is what lets a session that predates this keep its history without a migration. Order comes back from each event's own `ts` rather than from the order lines happen to sit in, since a union merge across two machines interleaves them anyway, and an identical line from that merge is dropped rather than replayed twice.

A prompt waits for a sync before it starts, so the queue in front of it is what decides how long it takes an agent to say its first word. Nothing may put a pass on that queue on a clock. The loop arms the next pass once the last one has settled, and a request that arrives while a pass is already waiting is given that pass rather than one of its own, so the queue is never more than one running and one waiting. A pass on a busy folder takes longer than the interval between them, and a queue fed on a timer only ever gets longer: agents sat on "Starting" for seven minutes while the passes ahead of them drained.

**A spoken turn is the one that does not wait, because somebody is sitting in silence while it does.** A pass is a commit, a pull and a push on the project and on the crew, and it queues behind whatever pass is already running, so on a busy crew it is tens of seconds in front of a conversation that has to feel immediate. `voice` on the prompt message is what says so, and the runner starts the pass and carries on rather than awaiting it, so the work still lands and the first word does not wait on a remote. Every other kind of turn is held exactly as it was: somebody who typed is reading the thread while it starts, and an agent that has to see fresh code is worth the wait. What it costs is a spoken turn working from files that are a few seconds old, which is the same staleness the scheduled loop already lives with between passes. `tests/voice-sync.integration.test.ts` holds both halves against a pass that never settles, and without the rule the spoken half of it hangs for the whole timeout, which is what the feature did.

## Updating

A new Crew is out and the app says so, on one pill in the top bar. It stands only while there is one, and pressing it is the whole update: the new Crew comes down and the app comes back on the other side. The releases on GitHub are the feed, and `electron-updater` is what reads them.

- One press and no second one. Pressing starts it, the pill fills as it comes, and the app relaunches itself the moment it lands. A download that finished and then waited to be let in is a second thing to press for something already decided, and the press was for the update rather than for the half of it that fetches. Whoever pressed it wanted the new Crew, so they get it.
- A restart never installs anything, and nothing installs itself on the way out. `autoInstallOnAppQuit` is off, and it was the wrong thing to lean on twice over: it is only read at the moment a download completes, and its handler returns early once `quitAndInstall` has been called at all, so with the press installing at once the flag was already dead. Worse, it would install at a quit, which is exactly when nobody is watching and other Crews may be live. Crew decides when the app may be replaced instead. Nothing is lost by that, since the download stays on the disk: the press after a restart lands straight on ready and installs in a moment.
- The install waits for every other Crew on the machine. `crew` opens one Crew per folder, so several are ordinary and none of them can see the others through electron, and the Windows installer force-kills every one it finds: `--updated` is precisely what skips the "app is running" prompt and jumps to `taskkill /im`, after 1300ms of grace. There is no runtime option off that, only a custom NSIS macro at build time, so pressing update in one window used to force-kill the others mid-run, which is the one thing the rules for agents say never to do. `mayInstall` in `src/shared/update.ts` is the rule and `OtherInstances` in `src/main/instances.ts` is the count, kept as pid files in `userData/live` the way the sync keeps a lock file, since that is the only thing every platform can be asked the same way. A folder that cannot be read is nobody rather than somebody, or an unreadable folder would hold every update up forever.
- Only an installed Crew marks itself, because only an installed Crew is one the installer replaces. A run from source stands nowhere the installer will reach, and marking one meant every machine with a checkout open held its own updates: `yarn dev` is running on those machines the whole time, so the pill was pressed, the install was refused by a process nobody thought of as a Crew, and the app sat there. `shipping` in `src/main/index.ts` is that one answer and it is the same `fromSource` the icon, the dev tools and the updater read.
- A mark older than the machine coming up is nobody. A pid is handed out again after a reboot, so a Crew that died without tidying up leaves a number that something else is wearing by the time anybody reads it, and the file answers as a live Crew from then on with nothing anywhere to say why the updates stopped. `outlivedBoot` in `src/shared/instances.ts` is the rule, read against `os.uptime()`. The margin is which way to be wrong: reading a live Crew as gone is what lets an installer take its work down, so a mark has to be clearly older than the boot rather than a moment older, and a clock that moved under us is not enough to make one stale.
- A Crew that has not been marked cannot be counted, so the first release carrying any of this is the one update where another Crew still running an older build is not seen and is killed the way it always was. Every one after it is covered. Nothing can be done about that from here, since the count is written by the app that is running.
- Held, it says so on the press rather than on the pill. The pill is still standing there offering the same thing, so what says why is a toast, the way the server's own `notice` says why a command did not happen. That means the stage stands too: an install that did not happen leaves it on ready and writes only the reason, because the bytes are here and the restart is the one thing left to do. Standing back down to a stage that says an update is available read as the whole download coming undone under the press. It is landing that asks for the install rather than being landed, or a hold read off the stage alone would ask again for the thing just refused, and answer itself, forever. Being held a second time is worth saying a second time, which is why `told` is part of the state: keyed alone, the same word said twice would be written once and a second press would read as a button that does nothing.
- Whatever quitting puts down is put down before the app is replaced under it, and once. `settle` in `src/main/index.ts` is that one shutdown, shared by the quit and by the install, so nothing is shut down twice when `quitAndInstall` quits the app itself. `GitSync.stop` only stands the loop down, so `quiet` is what waits out a pass that is already committing and pushing: without it the Windows installer's `taskkill` arrives about 1300ms later and cuts a commit and push in half.
- An install that did not happen says nothing for itself, on either platform, so the app is what says it. The Windows installer is spawned detached with its exit code never read, and there is no `exit` listener anywhere in the package, so a silent installer that took the cancel default is unobservable. On macOS `quitAndInstall` does nothing at all when Squirrel never staged the update: no quit, no error, no timeout. What says it did not happen is the app still standing here 30 seconds later, and a Squirrel refusal at verify time is heard sooner than that, because the constructor re-emits every native error and one arriving while an install is going is that install failing. Landed was terminal before this, so the pill sat on the offer of a restart forever and nothing anywhere said why.
- `autoUpdater.logger` is a real file, `updates.log` in `userData`. The default logger is `console`, which a packaged app writes to nowhere, so every update failure out there left no trace at all. Three methods are required and `debug` is optional. It is truncated past half a megabyte and a log that cannot be written is never worth taking an update down for.
- The check keeps going while the pill stands. It stopped for good once anything was found, so a window left open for a week never heard about the release after that one. `worthChecking` is the rule: only a download in flight and one already down are left alone, since neither has anything to learn.
- The pill is the whole of the way in. There is no row in the settings and no item in a menu, because either would be a second way to do a thing that is already one press away, and neither could say the one thing that matters, which is that there is something to press at all.
- It is solid white, the way every other action in the app is. White is the single action color and this is the one thing in that row worth pressing, so a quiet pill in the same `bg-fg/[0.06]` as the chrome around it was a button nobody saw: it read as another piece of furniture, and it is on screen for the few days between a release and somebody taking it. It is the only white thing in the top bar, which is what makes it the whole of the announcement without a badge, a dot or a color the app does not otherwise wear.
- While the new Crew is coming down the pill is not a button, so it drops to the quiet track and the white is what travels across it. The word is written twice, once on each side of the fill, and the two copies stand at the same size in the same place with the top one clipped to the fill: dark on the white that has passed and light on the track it has not reached. One copy in either color vanishes for half the download, and a percent beside the word is the fill saying the same thing again in figures.
- What the pill can say lives in `src/shared/update.ts` as a plain move from one stage to the next, and `src/main/updates.ts` is the thin part that hands `electron-updater`'s words to it. That split is the only way any of it is tested: main imports electron and a suite cannot, so `tests/update.test.ts` drives every stage by hand without a release, a network or an app anywhere near it. `tests/update-pill-probe.test.ts` is the other half, and it renders the pill through the real store at every stage and reads the words it says.
- The three ways it does not happen are three different sentences, so `why` rides in the state and the words are picked off it where they are drawn. A download that did not arrive and an update that would not go on are the app's own failures and wear the warning mark. The other Crews are neither: that one is an instruction, so it goes in the plain tone.
- `autoUpdater` is never imported by name. Main is bundled as ESM and `electron-updater` is a CommonJS module that hands that one export over as a getter on its exports rather than as a plain assignment, so the lexer node reads a CommonJS module's names with cannot see it: every other export of that package imports by name, and this one throws on the way in and takes the whole app with it before a window is ever made. The module comes in whole and the getter is read off it. Reading it is what builds the platform updater, so it is read once, behind the same gate that says the run is packaged, and a run from source never builds one at all. Nothing about this is visible in a suite, because a suite cannot import main, and `tsc` reads the package's types where the export is a plain one, so both were green while the built app would not start. The build is what says: `yarn build` and open it.
- A check that could not reach the internet says nothing at all. Only a download somebody asked for and did not get is worth a word, and that word is a toast rather than the pill, because the pill is still standing there offering the same thing again. Read the plain way, every laptop that closed its lid on a wifi network would wake to a failure it never asked for.
- Nothing is checked from a run out of source. There is no release behind one and no signature to hold it to, so `fromSource` is the same gate the icon uses. It stops looking once it has found one, and looks again every hour until then.
- macOS will not install an update to an app that is not signed, and nothing about a build says so on the way past. An unsigned run signs nothing, notarizes nothing, warns about neither, and produces a dmg, a zip and a whole `latest-mac.yml`, so the workflow goes green and ships a release every Mac quietly refuses. `forceCodeSigning` in `build.mac` is what turns that into a build that fails instead.
- Signing is held by the build and notarizing is not, so the workflow is what holds it. electron-builder skips notarization with a warning when the Apple credentials are not in the environment, so a build missing one secret is signed, unnotarized and green, and every Mac that opens it is told Crew is damaged. The step after the build reads the app back instead: `codesign` has to name a Developer ID Application authority, and `xcrun stapler validate` has to find a ticket on the bundle. Both are read off what was built rather than off what was passed in, which is the only thing that says the notarizing really happened.
- The credentials are five repo secrets and nothing else. `CSC_LINK` is a Developer ID Application certificate exported as a base64 `.p12`, `CSC_KEY_PASSWORD` is what it was exported under, and `APPLE_ID`, `APPLE_APP_SPECIFIC_PASSWORD` and `APPLE_TEAM_ID` are what notarytool is handed. Developer ID Application is the one certificate that works here: Apple Development runs a build on your own machine and Apple Distribution goes to the App Store, and neither of them can be notarized for anybody else to open.
- Hardened runtime is enforced the moment the app is really signed, and it is what makes the entitlements load bearing. Ad hoc signed nobody checks any of them, so the app that ran here for months carried none and worked. Scribe reads the caret by sending an Apple Event to System Events, which macOS refuses a hardened app unless it carries `com.apple.security.automation.apple-events` and an `NSAppleEventsUsageDescription` in the Info.plist. Without the pair, dictation reads as "click into a text box" over an open composer, in a build that passed every check. The microphone and the camera are the same shape of rule, and `disable-library-validation` is what lets the three native modules load at all.
- What the Mac remembers about Crew is keyed on the signature, so it is a different app to macOS the first time it is really signed. Accessibility, the microphone, the camera and screen recording are all asked for again, and the stale row in System Settings has to come out before the new one takes: left there, the switch reads as on and the key hook hears nothing. Nothing in the app can do this, and nothing about it is a bug.
- macOS updates from a zip and never from the dmg, so `build.mac.target` carries both. The dmg is what a person downloads once and the zip is what every update after it rides on.
- What ships is `dependencies` and nothing else, which is why there are five of them. electron-builder packs the whole production closure into the asar whatever `files` says, and the renderer is bundled by vite, so anything the renderer imports is already inside `out/renderer` and a second copy of it in `node_modules` is weight nobody ever loads. Every package the app uses was in `dependencies` once, which is 284 of them and 830MB, `onnxruntime-node` alone 218MB with nothing in main, preload, server, runner or shared importing it. Cut to the five that are really loaded at runtime it is 23 packages and 71MB, and that took the app from 899MB to 292MB and the zip every update rides on from 347MB to 116MB. A package belongs in `dependencies` only if something outside the renderer imports it, and everything else is dev.
- The prebuilds for other platforms are already gone, so nothing here excludes them by hand. electron-builder drops `prebuilds/<platform>-<arch>` for every platform but the one being built, which is what takes node-pty from the 62MB in the package to the 2.8MB a Mac ships, and a rule written here to do it again would be a rule saying what the builder already does.
- The Windows installer is built as `Crew Setup 0.1.0.exe` and written into `latest.yml` as `Crew-Setup-0.1.0.exe`, because the feed records the name that is safe to put in a URL. electron-builder renames it as it uploads, and `--publish never` means it never gets to, so the workflow renames every file with a space in it before the release is made. Without that the app asks for a file the release does not have and the update is a quiet 404.
- `--publish never` still writes the feed. The update files are made because there is a `publish` block in `package.json`, not because anything is being published, which is what lets both platforms build on their own runners and land on one release afterwards. Two matrix jobs publishing to one tag race each other, so nothing here ever does.
- The release must not be a draft or a prerelease. The app asks GitHub for the latest release and GitHub does not count either of those as one.
- A Crew that was never signed cannot update itself to a signed one. Squirrel checks what came down against the running app's own requirement, and an ad hoc signature cannot be satisfied by a Developer ID one, so a locally built Crew installs the dmg by hand once and every update after that is ordinary. It stays ordinary only while `com.jamel.crew` and the Team ID hold: changing either voids all four permissions for everyone and takes the update path with them.
- Never add `@electron/fuses` with its security defaults. `RunAsNode` is what the `crew` command and the Fn key listener are both built on, so turning it off takes away the way in from a terminal and the way in from a bare modifier at once, and the asar integrity pair breaks the listener's own entry point inside `app.asar`.
- Nothing is ever rebuilt from source, which is `npmRebuild: false` in the build config. Every native module here is N-API and ships a prebuild for each platform in the package, so there is nothing to build and the ABI is stable across node and electron anyway. Left on, electron-builder hands each one to `@electron/rebuild`, which shells out to node-gyp, whose bundled gyp imports `distutils`, which Python dropped in 3.12, so the build dies on the first native module on any runner with a current Python while passing on a laptop still running 3.9. The rebuild is worse than unnecessary: node-pty and uiohook-napi both look in `build/Release` before `prebuilds/`, so what a rebuild leaves behind is what loads, whatever architecture it was really built for.
- The Mac build is arm64 and nothing else. Half of what a native module ships is a package per architecture, `@koromix/koffi-darwin-arm64` and `@img/sharp-darwin-arm64`, and only the one matching the machine is installed, so an x64 app cross-built on an arm64 runner carried arm64 binaries and nothing that could load them. That is a runner rather than a flag, so the target is `--mac --arm64` and the check reads `lipo` back off the app it built. Windows is not affected, since it builds x64 on an x64 runner and installs the x64 packages for itself.
- The build installs with the yarn this project is on, which is 1.22.22, and `--frozen-lockfile`. `yarn.lock` here is a v1 lockfile, and yarn 4 reads one by converting it to its own format, which counts as changing it, so an immutable install refuses and the build fails on its first step before anything is compiled. The lockfile rule is what should be strict: a release resolving different versions from the machines it was written on is not the app anybody tested. Never install with corepack yarn 4 without migrating the lockfile with it.

## Dev tools

A shipped Crew has none. `fromSource(app.getAppPath())` is the gate, the same one the icon and the updates read, so a run from source keeps them and only the packaged app loses them. `inspectable` in `src/main/index.ts` is that answer, worked out once and handed to everything that needs it.

- It is off in two places at once, because either one on its own is half a door. `devTools` in `webPreferences` is what makes `openDevTools` do nothing at all, and it goes on every window the app makes: the main one, the tray panel and the Scribe pill, which all read it off the `PanelPage` they are handed. The row in the menu is the other half, since a switch left on with only the row taken away is a window anything in main could still open an inspector on, and a row left standing over a dead switch is a menu item that does nothing.
- The View menu is written out in `appMenuTemplate` rather than taken as the `viewMenu` role. That role carries Toggle Developer Tools and its accelerator, and there is no way to keep the rest of it and drop that one row. Reload and the zoom stay, since `⌘R` is the app's own and the canvas is written around it.
- A page in the side panel is a real browser carrying preferences of its own, and a webview inherits none of this from the window it stands in, so `will-attach-webview` says it again for whatever a window embeds.
- There is no way to loosen it, and no environment variable stands beside `CREW_SHIPPING_ICON` for this. An escape hatch is the ability, which is the thing being taken away.
- `tests/window-options.test.ts` holds all of it. It walks the whole menu tree rather than one submenu, so nothing can put the row back under another menu unnoticed.

## The installer

What somebody sees the first time they meet Crew: a disk image window with the app on the left, Applications on the right, and one line saying what to do. The ground is a light grey, the words and the arrow are ink, and the colour is one aurora anchored off the left edge with half of it past the frame, so Crew stands at the leading edge of the light and Applications is calm grey at the other end.

- The light comes from beyond the window. `BLOOM.x` is negative, so the source itself is never in the picture and what is in the picture is falloff, which is the rule a rim light is lit by. It falls off as a Gaussian rather than as a `smoothstep`, because a smoothstep reaches nought at a place and that place is a visible boundary: on a light ground it read as a cloud with an edge sitting in the left third, rather than as light. A Gaussian has no edge to find. It is a little taller than it is wide, so it hugs the whole left side rather than reading as a semicircle somebody cropped.
- **On a light ground a glow cannot add light, it can only stain.** There is nowhere above the paper for it to go, so a screen or an add blend is invisible here and the composite is a lerp toward the colour. That has a consequence worth naming: every mesh colour is darker than the paper, so what reads as a bloom is a controlled darkening, and the layers with the least lightness between them and the paper are the ones that read as light. The violet and the base were the two turning light into ink, and both are raised toward the paper's lightness with their chroma held. Lowering their opacity instead only makes them grey.
- The shading agrees with where the light is. The one wash over the ground darkens toward the bottom right, away from the source, which is what stops the grey reading as flat paint. It is a few per cent and no more.
- The grain is over everything, ground and colour alike, and it is paper rather than dither. Dither is one 8-bit level and this is many times that on purpose. It is monochrome, added to all three channels together, and centred so the mean brightness is what it was.
- Each scale is shot at its own size. One shot at `@2x` rasterised down to `@1x` averages the grain away, so the file most Macs would show had half the tooth of the one it was tuned on. `make-icon.mjs` shoots the mesh twice, once per scale, and builds a picture from each.
- **Finder pins the icon labels to near-black, so the ground has to stay light enough to read them.** That was the whole reason the dark ground failed, and it is a rule rather than a preference now: `tests/dmg-window.test.ts` holds the label colour and the headline ink above 4.5:1 against `GROUND`. It also holds `GROUND` and the shader's own `PAPER` to being one colour, which is written down twice because a shader is a browser global that cannot import a drawing.

- **One picture ships, and it is a still by choice rather than by necessity.** `DMG.at` is the moment it is cut from, and `yarn dmg-preview` stands the whole thing up as a real page so a moment is picked by watching rather than by guessing a number.
- **Finder does animate a disk image background, which is worth knowing and was not obvious.** An animated GIF and an animated PNG both play behind the icons when handed to `background picture of options`, and it was measured rather than assumed: a volume wearing a still png photographed four times over six seconds came back byte identical every time, and the same volume wearing an animated one came back different every time. It is not shipped because of what it costs. The grain is the whole of that cost: it is noise, noise does not compress, and a second of it at retina runs to megabytes a frame however it is encoded. A paletted, undithered, ping-ponged animated png at 8 frames a second was the best of them and still eleven megabytes against the still's three quarters of one. If it is ever wanted, that is the recipe, and the frames have to be spaced at the real frame rate: sampled seconds apart in mesh time it does not read as drift, it reads as flashing between pictures.
- **A background is drawn one image pixel to the point, so a retina picture has to say it is one.** Handed a 1320 by 800 file with nothing else said, Finder fills 1320 by 800 points and the window shows the top left quarter of it, which reads as a headline running off the edge. A `pHYs` chunk saying 144 dots to the inch is what makes the same file land at 660 by 400 points with every pixel of its detail, and `tagDpi` in `scripts/dmg-art.mjs` is the whole of writing it. That is one file rather than the pair a multi-representation tiff needed, and the suite holds the picture to being `DMG.retina` times the window and tagged to match.
- The grain is a fixed size on the page rather than one device pixel. Tied to the device pixel it halves physically at retina, so the ground was a different material at each scale, and it is also what makes an animation impossible to compress. `uGrainCell` is the block it is drawn in, worked out from the width being rendered, so the paper looks the same whatever it is drawn at.
- **The title bar cannot be taken off and its words cannot be chosen.** Finder's own dictionary gives a window `toolbar visible`, `statusbar visible`, `pathbar visible` and `sidebar width`, and nothing for the title bar, which is drawn whatever anybody asks. What it says is the volume name, and the volume name is what somebody reads on the desktop and in the sidebar when they go to eject it, so it stays legible on purpose.
- The line at the top is not an instruction. Six of the nine real installers opened while this was written say nothing at all, Docker names the gesture rather than aiming it, and Warp writes two words that could not belong to another app. The rule Crew already holds is what settles it: "Drag Crew into Applications" said Crew twice and Applications once within a few inches of both words, and the arrow had already said drag, so four words broke the one rule three times.
- **Finder pins the icon labels to near-black in both appearances the moment a background picture is set.** Measured off the pixels: the words under both icons come back `#030303` in Light and `#030303` in Dark, byte for byte, while the same volume with no background picture tracks the appearance properly and reads `#dddddf` on dark. There is no label colour key in the view options blob, so there is nothing to write and nothing to opt out of. The two words under the icons are the one part of this window that cannot be designed, and on a dark ground they were near-black on near-black. That is the constraint the light ground answers rather than works around: near-black on light grey is the one legible thing Finder was ever going to give, so the ground was moved to meet it instead of the design being built around a floor it could not clear.
- The title bar takes `DMG.chrome` off the bottom of the picture, so the bottom 48 of what is drawn is never seen. The window is the height of the picture rather than the picture plus a title bar, because a picture taller than the content area is cropped and one shorter leaves a band of nothing at the bottom, and a title bar is not the same height on every Mac. Everything is composed inside `height - chrome` and `tests/dmg-window.test.ts` holds it there.
- `src/renderer` is not involved. The mesh is a shader in `scripts/dmg-mesh.js`, written as a browser global with no imports, so the same file drives the live page and the frame that ships. `scripts/dmg-shoot.mjs` renders a frame in Electron, which is the only place here with a real GL context: headless Chrome hands back an empty canvas, and an empty canvas is a background that looks like nothing went wrong.
- Uniform arrays are indexed in `main` and the values passed into `band`, never indexed by a function parameter. GLSL ES 1.00 only allows a constant index expression, a loop counter counts and a parameter does not, and the whole shader fails to compile over it.
- The bloom is contained. A glow that runs to the edges is cut flat by the frame and reads as a stain rather than as light, so it falls to nothing well inside the window and only a whisper of colour reaches the rest.
- The words are set in the system sans, one face and one size. A hand was tried and it undid the rest: the grain, the glass tile and the aurora are a considered picture, and a casual face standing over them reads as a novelty rather than as the same piece of design.
- `resources/dmg-background.png` is the one file, written by `yarn icon` along with everything else. Nothing in `resources/` is edited by hand.
- Where the icons stand is written down twice, in `DMG` and in the `dmg` block of `package.json`, because one is a drawing and the other is a build config that cannot import it. The suite holds the two to agreeing.
- `yarn dmg-check` is the only thing that can see the rest of it. It builds a real disk image with the real background, sets it up the way electron-builder does, and photographs the mounted window, because whether Finder really lines the picture up with the icons is not a thing any suite can answer.

## Plugins

An MCP server the crew plugs in once, that every agent on every machine then has. It is a page under More in the sidebar, and it belongs to the crew rather than to a machine: what one person installs, everybody's agents get.

- The page is the app's own list of what there is to plug in, and above it whatever the crew has already taken. `PLUGIN_OFFERS` in `src/shared/plugins.ts` is that list and the one place a service is added. An offer the crew already holds is left out of the offers below, since it is standing in Installed a few rows up, and two rows for one thing is the same thing said twice.
- A row wears the service's own app icon rather than one of Crew's. It is the second place in the app that draws a picture nobody here made, and it is the same rule `ProviderMark` holds: a mark saying which service is about to be reached has to be the one somebody already knows off their own dock, so drawn again on the 24 grid it would be Crew's drawing of somebody else's logo. `PLUGIN_ART` is the record and the pictures are kept as they were given. One the crew wrote itself has no logo to wear, so it falls to `GeneratedField`, the same scene a helper's mark and a music cover are photographed in.
- It is two events in the chat log the way memory is, so it syncs for free, replays for free, and rides in the snapshot as a list of its own. `plugin.added` and `plugin.removed` are folded off the snapshot's own events the way the shelf is: a plugin is a row in a store rather than a moment to scroll past.
- The same name twice is one plugin, keyed on `pluginKey` the way a duplicate memory is keyed on its words, so the second add comes back with the id the first one got. A full crew is refused with the move to make rather than the oldest quietly falling off the end.
- **The plugin is the crew's and the key is the machine's.** A record goes in a log the whole crew shares, so a token written into one is a token handed to everybody who ever clones. What the record carries is the names of the keys a server wants, and `machineKeys` in `src/runner/plugins.ts` reads the values off the machine running the agent and nowhere else. A plugin whose keys that machine does not have is left out of the config rather than started without them.
- Nothing in the store needs a key typed in, which is why there is no screen for one. A server that signs itself in is the whole of what may stand here, and that is every row: the remote ones hand the person their own sign-in, and the two that run on this machine want nothing at all. Slack was written on a bot token once and taken back out for exactly that, and it is back on its own remote where the sign-in is the service's own. A row that installs and then quietly does nothing is worse than a row that was never offered, so anything wanting a key stays out until there is somewhere to put one.
- **A row is earned by asking the server, never by reading a list.** Every offer here answered a real `initialize` before it was written down: a 401 with a `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer` is a live server asking somebody to sign in, and a JSON-RPC result is one that wants nothing. The published lists are wrong in every direction and none of it shows until somebody installs the row: Semgrep is listed open and asks for a token, Atlassian's `/v1/sse` was retired in June 2026, and Stack Overflow's `/mcp` is a 404. A wrong address is the one failure this page cannot say anything about, since a plugin that never answers looks exactly like an agent that ignored you.
- The machine running the agent is what turns the crew's plugins into an MCP config, for the same reason. `openMcp` writes it to that machine's own temp area at `0600`, since a stdio server's environment carries tokens, and the run takes the file away in its `finally` the way a ghost run drops its pictures.
- Claude takes `--mcp-config <file>` and never `--strict-mcp-config`. That one means ignore every other MCP configuration, so it would quietly take away the servers somebody set up for themselves. Crew's are added and the machine's own are left alone, and Codex and Kimi are wired to behave the same way.
- Codex has no per-turn MCP in the app-server protocol and takes them as `-c mcp_servers.<name>={...}` in its own TOML, with no space either side of the `=`, since `-c` splits on the first one it finds. Never through `CODEX_HOME`: `auth.json` lives in there, so overriding it signs the run out.
- Kimi takes remote plugins over ACP on `session/new`, and its schema wants `headers` as an array rather than as the object the docs read like. Kimi Code 0.37.2 refuses the ACP standard shape for a local stdio server before it opens the session, so those stay out of Kimi's payload. Its own Shell and file tools remain available. The same local plugin still reaches every provider that accepts it.
- Grok and Local get none, and nothing anywhere pretends otherwise. Grok's `--plugin-dir` is on `grok agent` while Crew spawns `grok -p`, and the only other way in writes a config file that every other run on the machine would race. Local is our own tool loop with no MCP client in it at all.
- Nothing about what is plugged in is said in the preamble. MCP tools announce themselves to the model, and a line saying their tools are already in your hands would be untrue on the two providers that got none and on any plugin dropped for a missing key.
- **The plugin standing in the crew's own list is what says the install happened, and the answer to the request is only the quick way to hear it.** `plugin.result` is a message the host learned to send a day after the installation record itself landed, so every crew hosted on a build from that window took the plugin, wrote it down, said so as `plugin.added`, and answered nothing. Read off the answer alone the install sat for ten seconds and then reported that it had not happened, which is the one failure here that is worse than a plain refusal: the sign-in had already worked, and the row tore it back down on the way past. So `installPlugin` watches the store as well, `PLUGIN_INSTALL_MS` is the ceiling rather than the wait, and a crew that has simply not answered yet keeps the sign-in it was just given, since the plugin may still be written down under the very installation id it was made for. A refusal still takes it, because there is nothing left for it to point at.
- **The board is told about a plugin only where the run really carries it.** `boardsPreamble` is handed what this machine has connected on a provider that can hold an MCP server at all, rather than what the crew happens to hold, so Frontpages is named on the runs that have `search_frontpages` in their hands and on no others. That is the rule about the preamble above read from the other end: on Grok and Local, and on every machine that has not connected it, the guide was promising tools that were not in the room.
- `tests/plugins.test.ts` holds the host side, `tests/plugin-mcp.test.ts` holds what each CLI really gets, what a missing key does and which runs a board hears about Frontpages on, and `tests/plugins-probe.test.ts` holds the store page and the install finishing on the plugin landing.

## Rules for agents working here

- `src/server`, `src/runner`, and `src/shared` must never import electron. Tests import them directly.
- Run `yarn tsc --noEmit` and the tests that cover what you touched before considering a change done. Name them: `yarn test tests/huddle.integration.test.ts`. There are over a hundred suites and the whole run takes a long time, so never finish a change by running `yarn test` on its own. Run the full suite only when someone asks for it.
- People keep more than one crew window open on the same folder, and that is supported. Never kill or quit a running instance to get a quiet repo. Work with it running, and expect your edits to be committed under you while you work.
- **A test earns its place by the behavior it holds, and by nothing else.** Every feature ships with coverage of its behavior, which is the rule in Code above, and this is the other half of it: hold the thing the feature promises somebody, and stop. A case per branch of every helper is a suite nobody reads and nobody dares change, and it is paid for twice, once in a run that is already long and again in every change that has to drag it along. Never hold what the type checker already holds. Never write a constant down a second time in a suite, or changing it means changing it in two places and the test is only ever agreeing with itself. Never add a case that passes for the same reason as the one above it, and never add one for a shape the app cannot produce. The suites worth having are the ones the sections above name by their own file, because each of those holds a rule somebody would otherwise break back.
- **A test that passes on its own and fails in the whole run is broken rather than slow.** It is the one kind of failure that teaches people to press rerun instead of reading the output, and a single one of them makes every other red run unreadable, which costs more than whatever it was holding. Fix it or take it out. Never paper over it with a retry, because a retry is the suite agreeing to lie about a race that is still there, and never reach for a longer timeout first: a timeout is what a real deadlock looks like from the outside, so raising it hides the fault rather than the flake.
- **The run is capped at four processes in `vitest.config.ts`, and that number is load bearing rather than tuning.** A full run on an eighteen core machine left `maxForks` at the default of seventeen and came back with 62 failures across 45 files. Half of those were suites that had drifted behind the app and were real. The other half passed on their own in five seconds and only ever failed beside sixteen others. At four they fell to nine, and at four with the waits below they fell to none, for 93 seconds more wall clock, which is nothing set against a run nobody could believe. What runs out is not the processor: at that cap the run spends about three times its own wall clock in test time, so nowhere near saturated, and what really contends is the disk and the processes these suites spawn, which is a real server, a real git repository and a real runner each. A workspace was tried and taken back out, integration on two processes and everything else wide, and it was worse rather than better: 26 failures, with vitest's own `onTaskUpdate` timing out seven times, because the two projects run beside each other and it is the total that the machine feels. One cap over the whole run is the answer, and the timeouts are 60 seconds because a suite that boots a server and a repository is not the same shape of thing as one that reads a stylesheet.
- **The waits in `tests/helpers/session.ts` are the suite's own budget rather than a second clock inside it.** `waitUntil`, `waitFor`, `waitForEvent`, `waitForClose` and the welcome all gave up at 10 seconds while the test around them was allowed 30, so a handshake that takes two seconds on a quiet machine was being failed at a third of the time it really had, and every caller that needed longer was already passing a number of its own. They are 30 now. Nothing is hidden by it, because a wait that has really hung still fails on the suite's own timeout, and the tighter number was never a decision anybody made.
- `yarn lint` is part of finishing, beside `yarn tsc --noEmit`, and so is the formatting of whatever you touched. CI reads the formatting of the files a change really moves rather than the whole tree, which is `yarn biome format --changed --since=origin/main .`, so a file left unformatted is not a job that fails for you. It is one that fails for whoever opens the next pull request over that file, having changed nothing about it.
- **Two of biome's own rules are wrong about this codebase and are off in `biome.json` rather than argued with again.** `noUselessThisAlias` wants `const Parent: any = this` in `ShapeUtil.ts` written as `class ... extends this`, which is three type errors rather than a tidy-up: a mixin class wants a single rest parameter, a spread argument wants a tuple, and the class is then held to implementing everything it inherits. The `any` is what holds all three off and it is deliberate. `noUselessStringRaw` calls a `String.raw` useless wherever that one string happens to carry no backslash today, which leaves a block of regex sources in `fileLinks.tsx`, and a driver source injected into a window in `scripts/`, part armoured and part not, and the next escape written into one of the bare ones is a bug nothing catches. A third is on and has a bug worth knowing: `useJsxKeyInIterable` cannot see a `key` in a `createElement` props object that also carries a member called `text`, measured against biome 2.5.6, which is the whole reason `tests/selector-cost-probe.test.ts` builds its rows in a loop rather than in a `map`. Leave it a loop.
- No real address is ever written into a suite. A join link, an attachment host and a photo all want an address that is somebody else's machine, and the one to hand is whatever the machine you are sitting at happens to be on, so the crew's own home network went into a dozen suites and out to a public repo with it. `127.0.0.1` where the test really means this machine, and `192.0.2.10` where it means another one: that range and the two beside it, `198.51.100.x` and `203.0.113.x`, are reserved for writing things down and reach nothing. Never a `192.168.x`, a `10.x`, a `172.16.x` through `172.31.x`, or a plain routable address off the internet, whether or not it is one of ours. `tests/address-rule.test.ts` reads every file under `tests/` and holds it.
